# TO THE MOST SERENE
# W I L L I A M III.
### BY THE GRACE OF GOD,
## OF ENGLAND, SCOTLAND, FRANCE, & IRELAND
# *K I N G,*
### DEFENDER OF THE FAITH, &c.

->To rejoice well, and to fare well. <note>original: "Εὐ χαίρειν, καὶ Εὐ πράττειν." A classical Greek greeting wishing both joy and prosperity.</note><-

### MOST SERENE KING,

UNDER the auspices of Your Majesty, Your University of Oxford enjoys deep Peace and ample Privileges; it is only right that we render our due thanks (after Immortal God) to Your Clemency. And since it has seemed fit to the University to publish from its Press certain of my Mathematical Works (partly now for the first time, partly having been published separately before) now collected into Three Volumes during Your Reign: there is no one else to whom they or I would rather dedicate them than to *Your Majesty*. Although these works are too minor to deserve being offered to Your Highness, they are at least what our Humility is able to provide. And (however slight they may be) they might seem to have gained some value for themselves through Your Benign Acceptance, if such is granted. It is not within our power (and thus we do not attempt it) to worthily Proclaim Your extraordinary achievements (performed at Home and in Military service) or to Celebrate them with just Praises in a brief discourse; but rather to Admire and Congratulate. We pray to God that (by His favor) Your Empire may be Happy and Prosperous in all things. And we implore Your Clemency, that we may be numbered among Your most Faithful Subjects, both *Your University of Oxford*, and

->Of Your MAJESTY,<-
->*The Most Humble Subject, &*<-
->*Most Devoted Client,*<-
->JOHN WALLIS. <note>John Wallis (1616–1703) was a leading English mathematician and the Savilian Professor of Geometry at Oxford.</note><-

# PREFACE.

There will be no need for a long Preface to this *Third* Volume of *Mathematical Works*; since a great part of those things which I prefaced in the *Two Preceding* volumes may also be of use here.

This volume is occupied, for the most part, in Publishing, Restoring, and Purifying several Monuments of the Ancient Greeks; which have lain hidden privately in Manuscript Codes <note>original: "Codd. MS." - handwritten manuscript books</note>, worthy of a better light.

There occurs, in the first place, the *Harmonic* Work of *Claudius* PTOLEMY: Whom I consider to be the Leader <note>original: "Coryphæum" - the head of a chorus or leader of a group</note> among the Ancient Greek Writers on the Subject of *Music*. He explains their *Harmonics* <note>The mathematical study of musical sounds and intervals</note> in a proper order, with a Demonstrative Method, as befits a Man of Mathematics. I was the first to publish this from Manuscript Codes in Greek, in the Year 1682, illustrated with a Latin Version and Notes.

To this I have subjoined an *Appendix*; in which I presented a brief Synopsis of the *Harmonics of the Ancient Greeks* (those which survive today), and compared it with *Modern Music*; by comparing the Diagram of the Ancients with the Modern *Guidonian Scale* <note>The "do-re-mi" system of musical notation attributed to Guido of Arezzo</note>, and their Modes with the various Signatures of our *Keys*. From this it is clear what is common to both (though under a different form of Speaking and Designating); and how Their *Harmonics* are contained within Ours.

That this was performed not unsuccessfully was attested by several learned Men (skilled in these matters); and at the same time they requested that I should likewise wish to publish *Porphyry’s* Commentary on *Ptolemy’s Harmonics*, and the *Harmonics of Bryennius*; which until now have lain hidden unpublished.

Hence it has come about that to *Ptolemy’s* Harmonics (now published for the second time) I have now subjoined PORPHYRY’S Commentary, and BRYENNIUS’S Harmonics, now for the first time unearthed from Greek Manuscript Codes; illustrated with a Latin Version and Notes.

And so we now have published all the Ancient (surviving) Greek Writers of *Music*. Indeed, some time ago the most Distinguished Man *Marcus Meibomius*, in the Year 1652, published in Greek and Latin *Aristoxenus, Euclid, Nicomachus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Bacchius, and Aristides*, Greeks; (and *Martianus Capella*, in Latin); that is, their Musical Works (which survive): And I now present the Harmonics of *Ptolemy, Porphyry*, and *Bryennius*; who are the only ones (that we know of) remaining from the Ancient Greek Musicians who were hitherto unpublished.

To these it seemed good to add (which I had previously published in the Year 1676) ARCHIMEDES’S *The Sand-Reckoner* <note>original: "Arenarium" - a work where Archimedes calculates the number of grains of sand that would fit in the universe</note>; a Golden Book indeed, but miserably corrupted. *Johannes Hervagius* had published this in Greek in the Year 1544 at *Basel*; faithfully (as it seems) from the Manuscript which he had, but one greatly full of faults: And *Rivaltus* in *Paris*, in the Year 1615, with hardly much better success: And meanwhile *Federicus Commandinus* (a Man who deserved very well of Mathematical Matters) had published the same in Latin; but, likewise from a Greek Code (as he himself often complains) that was greatly full of faults. I at last (since I did not have a Manuscript Code which I might consult) attempted from my own Conjectures, and the Traces of Truth which remain in the *Hervagian* Edition (for that of *Rivaltus* contributed little to this), to Purify and (as much as I could) Restore the excellent Author, illustrated with a new Version and Notes; which labor men skilled in these matters judged to be not unsuccessfully spent. With how much toil and study this was done (in a Work so full of faults), let those judge who have experienced the like.

<summary>The author introduces the third volume of his mathematical collection, focusing on his efforts to edit and restore ancient Greek musical and mathematical texts by Ptolemy, Porphyry, Bryennius, and Archimedes.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Porphyry, Bryennius, Archimedes, Harmonics, music theory, The Sand-Reckoner, manuscript restoration, Marcus Meibomius, history of mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The author continues the preface to his collected mathematical works, detailing the specific treatises and correspondences included in this volume.</meta>

# P R E F A C E.

I was also adding (as I now repeat) Archimedes' *Measurement of the Circle* (previously published in a corrupt state, though less corrupt than the *Sand-Reckoner* <note>original: "Arenarius"; a work by Archimedes on large numbers</note>), along with the Commentary of *Eutocius* upon it. It seemed appropriate to subjoin this Commentary (then published for the first time in English <note>original: "Latine"; translated here as English for the target audience</note> from a Greek manuscript, illustrated with notes, and cleansed of many errors); both because the text of *Archimedes* was so brief that it entirely required a commentary, and so that the method used by later Greeks in commenting on the writings of the ancients might be clear from this; and also that it might be perceived with how much labor and difficulty they performed the extraction of roots and other more intricate arithmetic operations before the reception of the numerical figures we now use <note>The author refers to the transition from Greek alphabetic numerals to the Hindu-Arabic numeral system</note>; and how much more expeditiously and clearly we now complete those same tasks.

After these were published, I was the first to publish, in the year 1688 (from a Greek manuscript), the book of *Aristarchus of Samos*, *On the Magnitudes and Distances of the Sun and Moon*: along with the Latin version by *Federico Commandino*, which agreed sufficiently with our Greek manuscript. (Which I also repeat here.)

And at the same time, a fragment of the Second Book of the *Mathematical Collections* of *Pappus of Alexandria*, previously missing. Indeed, those parts of this *Mathematical Collection* which *Commandino* had published in Latin (for we do not yet have them published in Greek) begin from the *Third Book*. For the first two were missing (and are still missing today). Since, therefore, in one of the manuscripts (for we have several Greek manuscripts of *Pappus*) I found a significant fragment of the *Second Book* (from which a judgment could be made about the remainder), it seemed fit to publish it here as well (corrected and likewise rendered into English); both because I believed it would not be unwelcome to learned men to know what was contained in the First and Second books, now lost; and so that we might be less anxious about their loss, since it seems clear that nothing else was contained therein than the more complex arithmetic operations of that age, which we now (after the reception of the *Indian Algorithm* <note>The modern system of arithmetic using ten digits</note>) dispatch more promptly.

Moreover, it pleased me to insert these smaller treatises (previously published separately) into this volume, so that they may be kept here with the rest.

It also seemed fit to subjoin to these a collection of certain letters regarding mathematical matters, which passed between me and certain most celebrated men. Namely, Messrs. *Leibniz, Oldenburg, Brouncker, Newton, Mencke, Flamsteed*, and others. In these, various recent inventions and methods are discussed promiscuously and in various places; such as: *On the Arithmetic of Infinites; On Methods of Interpolation; On the Transformations of Figures; and the Distribution of Composite Figures into their Component Parts; On the Quadratures of Curvilinear Figures; On Tangents of Curves; On the Rectification and Flattening of Curves; and many methods relating to these; On Infinite Series; On Interscendent and Transcendent Equations and Notations; On the Evolution of Convoluted Figures, the Explanation of Complicated ones, and the Restitution of Dislocated ones; On the Method of Fluxions <note>Newton's version of calculus</note>; On the Differential Calculus <note>Leibniz's version of calculus</note>; On the Logistics of the Ancients as distinct from their Arithmetic; On Geometric matters to be treated by abstract logical consideration;* and others. Likewise, *On Explaining Cryptography; On Teaching the Mute and Deaf; on the notable Parallax of the Annual Orbit of the Earth finally observed; and the Construction of Instruments relating to that;* and many other things.

After all these things, which relate more closely to Mathematics, there follow certain *Miscellanies* (on several subjects diverse among themselves). Which, if in a strict sense they should not be called *Mathematical Works*, I hope at least they will not be unwelcome even to mathematicians; and some such men have demanded that they be published by me. If anyone does not like to associate these with mathematical writings, he may bind them separately.

Among these occurs, in the first place, *On Speech* <note>original: "De Loquela"</note>, a Grammatico-Physical treatise, first published in the year 1653. In which is handed down the mechanical formation of all spoken sounds (of whatever languages I know); that is, by what position and motion of the organs of speech each sound is produced. A work which I do not know if anyone undertook before me. It is true, indeed, that even the Ancients distributed letters into their classes (namely Labials, Linguals, Dentals, Palatals, Gutturals, etc.): but to explain the whole economy of speaking, I do not know that anyone before attempted, let alone achieved. By the help of which, I have taught several people, who were entirely deaf, to speak distinctly.

<summary>The author describes the inclusion of works by Archimedes, Aristarchus, and Pappus in this volume, alongside his scientific correspondence with figures like Newton and Leibniz, and his pioneering work on phonetics and deaf education.</summary>
<keywords>Archimedes, Aristarchus of Samos, Pappus of Alexandria, Isaac Newton, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, Calculus, Fluxions, Arithmetic of Infinites, Phonetics, Deaf education, History of Mathematics</keywords>

## ADMONITION.

SINCE I have noticed that in the two preceding Volumes, certain Errors have crept in which are not noted there: it seemed best to indicate here how they are to be corrected. If, however, any other error of the Press, the Pen, or the Calculation should have crept in elsewhere; let the kind Reader who detects it either Correct it or Forgive it. Indeed, in such a Complex Calculation, it is a wonder if an error does not occasionally steal in.

## CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
### ON HARMONICS
#### THREE BOOKS.

<note>original: "ΚΛΑΥΔΙΟΥ ΠΤΟΛΕΜΑΙΟΥ ΑΡΜΟΝΙΚΩΝ ΒΙΒΛΙΑ Γ'" and "CLAUDII PTOLEMÆI HARMONICORUM LIBRI TRES". Ptolemy was a 2nd-century scholar living in Alexandria; while famous for astronomy, this work is the most scientific treatment of music theory from antiquity.</note>

<meta>This is the title page for the treatise on music theory by the famous astronomer and mathematician Claudius Ptolemy. It establishes the formal structure of the work in three books.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
## ON HARMONICS
### THREE BOOKS

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
## ON HARMONICS
### THREE BOOKS

<note>original: "ΚΛΑΥΔΙΟΥ ΠΤΟΛΕΜΑΙΟΥ ΑΡΜΟΝΙΚΩΝ ΒΙΒΛΙΑ Γ" / "CLAUDII PTOLEMAEI HARMONICORUM LIBRI TRES". Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD) was a Greco-Egyptian polymath. This work, the Harmonics, is one of the most significant scientific treatments of music theory from antiquity, attempting to find mathematical ratios behind musical intervals.</note>

->TO THE MOST SERENE<-

# CHARLES II.

->BY THE GRACE OF GOD<-

->OF ENGLAND, SCOTLAND, FRANCE, & IRELAND<-

# KING, &c.

->*Long Life and a Happy Reign.*<-

---

->MOST AUGUST KING,<-

Since I am the first to bring to light the excellent author, Claudius Ptolemy <note>A celebrated 2nd-century mathematician and astronomer whose work on music theory is presented here</note> (who is not unworthy of Royal patronage), writing on the subject of *Harmonics* <term>Harmonics: the mathematical study of musical intervals and ratios</term> in his own language <note>original: "sua ipsius lingua" referring to the Greek text</note>: to whom rather than the best of Kings should I dedicate it? That most longed-for book has remained hidden until now in private libraries (such was the perceived obscurity of the subject matter); it has lacked a public audience. It finally commits itself to the eyes of all, since it hopes to be bathed in the light of Your Majesty, so great a Prince. Both the authority of the man himself and the nature of the Office I hold persuaded me to publish it. Indeed, Sir Henry Savile <note>1549–1622, a scholar and benefactor who founded the chairs of Geometry and Astronomy at Oxford</note> (the munificent Founder of two Professors of Mathematics in Your University of Oxford, himself a man most learned in all things, and especially skilled in Mathematics) expressly recommended to his Professors, among other things, that they should simultaneously take care of the *Harmonics*: nor did I find, among the ancient writers on Harmony, anyone more preferable than Ptolemy, whom—

...which is more worthy of public view. To the Reader
A 3
<meta>catchword: gii,</meta>

## P R E F A C E.

Before I proceed to the text of Ptolemy, the learned Reader must be briefly advised regarding the purpose of our work.

About thirty years ago, in 1652, the illustrious Marcus Meibomius published (in a praiseworthy work) the *Ancient Musicians*: Aristoxenus, Euclid, Nicomachus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Bacchius, Aristides (the Greeks), and (the Latin) Martianus Capella. He also seemed to promise (had circumstances been favorable) Ptolemy and Bryennius (and perhaps also Porphyry’s commentaries on Ptolemy). But these latter works we have not yet seen; nor do I hear they are now to be expected (on account of "straitened circumstances at home" <note>original: "rem angustam domi" — a Latin idiom for poverty or lack of funds</note>, which often befalls learned men). Since, therefore, learned men especially desired Ptolemy (who has never before appeared in Greek, and indeed, in Latin, in such a way that he seems "published yet not published" <note>original: "ἐκδεδομένον ἢ μὴ ἐκδεδομένον" — meaning the existing Latin versions were so poor they hardly counted as being available</note>), I have been persuaded (at the urging of several friends, learned men) to undertake an edition of this work from manuscript codes, adding at the same time certain Notes which would shed light both on this Author and on the science of Harmonics itself. In this matter, I must briefly explain what I have provided.

The codices <note>term: codex — a manuscript book</note> which I used were, in total, eleven Greek manuscripts (besides the Latin version of Gogavinus), which I designate with the letters A B C D G K M S V O. [To which a twelfth, F, is now added.]

A and B are Greek manuscripts, beautifully transcribed, deposited in the archives of the Bodleian Library (at Oxford), numbers 164 and 165. These formerly belonged to the most distinguished Knight *Henry Savile*, who was the head of two illustrious colleges at once: namely, Warden of Merton (at Oxford) and Provost of Eton (near Windsor). He was a man most learned in other fields, but specifically most skilled in Mathematics and Greek literature; he was also the munificent founder of two Professors of Mathematics in the University of Oxford. These, I believe, he had caused to be transcribed many years ago (at least before the year 1609), either both from the same Vatican codex (or another in Italy), or (which I rather think) codex B from there, and then A from B. (With the intention that he might give one to the Bodleian Library and the other to the Mathematical Library he established for the use of his Professors; but by some chance it happened that the Bodleian now holds both.) For that both were from the same source, no one who compares them can doubt: indeed, they very rarely differ from each other, and then (as you might easily guess) by a scribe's error. That A was transcribed from B (not the reverse), I conjecture because I sometimes find a small word in B which is missing in A, but not vice versa; and (in which I most trust) because in B, on page 136, I find an interlinear note [namely, *the combined ratio* <note>original: "λόγος συναμφότερος"</note>] written in Savile's hand, which in A [*the combined ratio*] has crept into the text; and elsewhere several times there are similar instances. However it may be, I treat these two as one; and if there is a manifest error in A where it differs from B, I do not usually delay the Reader with it, or at least rarely.

C is the third Bodleian codex, placed among the Barocci books, number 124. This, along with the rest of the Barocci collection, was brought here from Venice from the former Barocci library (at least in the year 1629 or earlier), by the generosity of the most honorable *William, Earl of Pembroke*, then Chancellor of the University of Oxford. This codex, as I conjecture from the paper, the method of binding, the writing itself (written with a reed, not a quill), and the form of the characters, was obtained from Greece; and it is the oldest of all those I use.

G and I, I likewise treat as one. I was certainly (as is clear from an attached memorandum) the property of *William Laud*, Archbishop of Canterbury and Chancellor of the University of Oxford, in the year of our Lord 1638, and was given by him to St. John’s College, Oxford. From whose library (by the favor of the exceptionally learned man, *William Levinz*, Doctor of Medicine and Professor of the Greek Language...

<meta>The previous page likely introduced the first few manuscript sources (A, B, C, and I) used for this edition of Ptolemy's Harmonics. The catchword "facere" indicates the transition from the preceding leaf.</meta>

# PREFACE

<note>original: "P R Æ F A T I O"</note>

I received the use of manuscript **I** (from the library of St. John’s College, Cambridge, by the kindness of the most worthy President of that same College). It is a book most beautifully written—though not of great antiquity—and based on a reliable original. I received the loan of manuscript **G** from Thomas Gale <note>A famous English classical scholar and antiquarian (1635–1702)</note>, Doctor of Divinity and the most learned High Master of St. Paul’s School in London. This manuscript, as I understand it, was copied from **I** (the St. John’s manuscript) not many years ago. Therefore, where it differs from **I** due to a scribe's error, I did not think the reader should be delayed by it.

In manuscript **I**, there were various scholia <note>Ancient or medieval grammatical or explanatory notes</note> written in (and subsequently transcribed into **G**), some in the margins and some between the lines. Their author is uncertain—perhaps someone added them to the book for their own use—but since they can be omitted without great loss, I decided to leave them out. I did the same, on occasion, with manuscripts **A**, **B**, and **C**.

As for manuscript **K**, I do not know what it was or who owned it. However, I use that letter to refer to whatever manuscript was compared with manuscript **I**, from which variant readings were noted in the margins of the latter. These readings frequently, though not always, agree with manuscript **C**.

Manuscript **S** likewise belonged to Savile <note>Sir Henry Savile (1449–1622), founder of the Savilian Chairs at Oxford</note> and is preserved in the Savilian Mathematical Library. But it is incomplete, reaching no further than the first book, and barely even to the end of that.

I received the use of manuscripts **V** and **O**, which are beautifully written, from the library of the most learned Mr. Isaac Vossius <note>A famous Dutch scholar and book collector (1618–1689)</note>, who shared them voluntarily. I assigned them the first two letters of the name "Vossius." However, they arrived late—specifically, after the printing work had already reached Chapter 12 of Book 2. For this reason, no mention of them was made in my notes before that chapter, but only from the beginning of that chapter to the end of the book. Nevertheless, I compared both with the pages already printed and have placed their variant readings at the end <note>original: "ad calcem"</note> so that nothing should seem missing. I found scarcely any variation in them from our other manuscripts of any importance that I had not already noted from one of the others. Indeed, manuscript **V** (which seems the older of the two, written in the year 1557 as indicated at the end) agrees almost everywhere with the previously mentioned Barocci manuscript **C**; thus, there is no doubt that both were derived from the same source. Similarly, manuscript **O** agrees with manuscript **I** (from which **G** was also copied) and has nearly the same variant readings written in the margin that I previously mentioned were copied from **K**—except that manuscript **O** sometimes incorporates some of these into the main text. Thus, there is no doubt that this too was derived from the same source as **I**. However, the scholia I mentioned seeing in **I** and **G** are not found here.

Finally, I came across manuscript **D**, which is among the Barocci collection, number 41. I assigned it the letter **D** so it could be compared with the other Bodleian manuscripts **A**, **B**, and **C**. It is a manuscript commentary by Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy's Harmonics</note> on some part of these books (namely, from the beginning to the end of Chapter 7, Book 2). It frequently contains the text of Ptolemy inserted piece by piece, especially in the earlier chapters. But I discovered it too late, not until the printing had reached the last two leaves of Ptolemy; consequently, I received no help from it in preparing the edition. However, for the benefit of readers, I have collected the variant readings from it (at least those that seemed of any importance and were not obvious scribal errors, of which there were many) and placed them at the end with the others among the "Additions." I have also seen to it that these were written into their proper places in at least two copies of the books intended for the Bodleian and Savilian libraries, should anything further need correction.

Lastly, while the Additions themselves were under the press, I came across manuscript **M** in Magdalen College, Oxford, which contains the aforementioned commentary by Porphyry. I have also placed the variant readings concerning Ptolemy's text taken from that source at the end among the Additions. In that manuscript (as well as in a certain Bodleian one), the *Harmonics* of Manuel Bryennius <note>A Byzantine scholar who wrote on music theory c. 1300</note> are also found. [Manuscript **F** belongs to University College; but it is incomplete, containing only the third book. I had not seen it before this second edition was to be sent to the press. It agrees, for the most part, with manuscript **C**, from which it was perhaps copied.]

In comparing these Greek manuscripts with one another, I have used such diligence that I believe I have missed hardly anything of importance.

Where the manuscripts differ, I generally follow the reading that seems better to me; however, where both are acceptable, I sometimes follow the majority of manuscripts even if the reading of the few pleases me more.

I provide the variant readings everywhere so that everyone is free to make their own choice.

<meta>The author continues his explanation of the editorial principles used in transcribing and correcting the Greek biblical or classical manuscripts, specifically addressing orthography and grammatical nuances.</meta>

# P R E F A C E.

I have done this. And I have done so with such reverence that I fear rather lest I be judged too fastidious in noting tiny details which are either of no importance or are manifest errors.

I do this especially regarding the <term>paragogic nu</term> <note>original: "ν paragogico"; a 'movable n' added to the end of certain Greek words to avoid a clash of vowels</note>, after *e* and *i*, before a consonant, contrary to the usual custom. I observe this quite often, especially in Manuscript S: so that I may leave it to the Critics to decide whether that might not be the truer reading, even though we are accustomed to omit that *nu* nowadays.

Similarly, I often retain *touton*, *toiouton*, (and others of that kind,) even in the Neuter gender, as the preferable reading; since I find it so, for the most part, in most manuscripts: and it is according to Attic usage <note>The dialect of ancient Athens</note>.

I also note, especially in the Manuscript, *o* for *ai*, and *ai* for *e*, likewise *es* for *eis* or *ais*, and *o* for *o* <note>original: "ω pro ο"; omega for omicron</note>, and many things of this sort; likewise *u* for *b*, and vice versa; not because I did not think them manifest errors; but so that I might show by example how prone people were to slip in these matters, in that age and in those places, where *ai, oi, e, ei, i, u, ui* <note>original: "αι, οι, η, ῃ, ει, ι, υ, υι"</note> were pronounced almost indiscriminately, like *i*: or *e*: likewise *au, eu*, etc. as *av, ev*, or *af, ef*: so that it is no wonder if this is found more frequently in other manuscript codices.

Yet there are other smaller matters which, although they are sometimes noted, I did not think it worth the effort to do so always. For instance, in *de* or *apo* before a vowel, likewise *met' med' mete, kat' kath' kata*, and things of this kind; in which, if the Manuscripts agree, I follow them; if they disagree, I either follow the majority or use my own judgment; yet I do not always detain the reader with these. If, however, a punctuation mark intervenes, or emphasis or some other cause suggests a pause in pronunciation, I would prefer (if the manuscripts permit) to write the word in full rather than with an <term>Apostrophe</term>.

Almost the same must be said regarding the accent of <term>Enclitic</term> words <note>Words that "lean" on the preceding word for their accent</note>; whether retained, inclined, or lost entirely; for example *dia esti*, or *de esti*, or *d' esti*, and such like: in which matter the manuscripts vary greatly, and writers are accustomed to allow themselves great license, whether in marking them or not marking them; and so, this way or that. I therefore, in such a small matter (especially unless the reason of emphasis suggests one or the other,) follow the manuscripts where they agree; where they differ, I use my discretion; nor am I overly anxious, lest I seem too curious in a matter of nothing: leaving meanwhile to others their own judgment, if they should prefer it done otherwise.

Understand the same regarding words which are now accustomed to be written together, now separately, and indeed according to each person's whim: such as *hoste, oukepi, oudolos, mede, mononou, toigaroun, ephoson, exarches, ephexes, en genei*, and many others: in which I have tried to follow that spelling toward which most manuscripts generally lean, yet in such a way (for the most part) that I am consistent with myself, (writing the same words in the same way,) yet not too scrupulously; especially if some reason suggests otherwise; for example, *delonoti*, where it signifies "namely," "to wit," etc. but *delon hoti*, for "it is clear," or "it is evident that," etc.

Similarly, in <term>subscribing the iota</term> <note>original: "ι subscribendo"; the small 'i' written under a vowel</note>, or not subscribing it, (especially in certain tenses of the <term>Subjunctive Mood</term>,) where even the most skilled Critics or Printers agree neither with each other, nor always with themselves.

But, in compounds formed from *kai*, such as *kan, kapeita*, etc; (for *kai an, kai en*, etc.) I am accustomed to omit it; both because the Manuscripts almost perpetually agree in this; and because <term>Ralph Winterton</term> <note>A 17th-century Regius Professor of Greek at Cambridge</note> (who was very studious of these matters) in his Notes to the Minor Poets, formerly published at Cambridge, contends (not without reason) that this should be done: Because, for example, in *kai en*, it should not so much be said that *ai* & *e* (through <term>Crasis</term> <note>The merging of two vowels into one long vowel</note>) coalesce into a long *e*; but, having first been squeezed out (through <term>Thlipsis</term> <note>The suppression of a vowel</note>), the *a* & *e* finally coalesce (through <term>Crasis</term>) into a long vowel. Nor should the trace of what was squeezed out be retained: since that which is subscribed must be considered later than that under which it is subscribed; and since the intermediate *i* cannot be considered later than the whole long *e* (formed from *a* & *e*); it cannot be suggested by a subscript *i*. It is otherwise, however, if it is held in a later diphthong, (as that which is later than the others coalescing.) And so one should write *ko* <note>original: "κᾧ"</note> for *kai ho*, but *ko* <note>original: "κῠ"</note> for *kai hoi*. These things were indeed said not without reason. Yet not indisputably. For, if anyone should prefer the contrary, it could be retorted; just as that which is subscribed is later; so the Breathing mark, which is written above, must be judged prior to the vowel to which it is attached. Since therefore, in *ko* for *kai ho*, the aspirated Breathing, although proper to the later vowel *ho*, (and thus intermediate to those coalescing,) is written above the vowel *o* (formed from *a* & *ho*) as if it preceded both: likewise the (intermediate) vowel *i* could be subscribed to the same, even if not...

<meta>catchword: to both</meta>

<summary>The author explains his technical editorial choices regarding Greek orthography, such as the use of movable 'nu', iota subscripts, and the merging of words, citing scholarly precedents like Ralph Winterton.</summary>
<keywords>orthography, Greek grammar, iota subscript, crasis, Ralph Winterton, manuscript variants, phonetics, editorial method</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface, likely by John Wallis or a contemporary editor, discussing the philological and typographical principles used in editing Greek mathematical texts. It follows a discussion on the contraction of vowels.</meta>

# PREFACE.

latter than both. But if it be said that it should be written not as *chō* <note>original: "χώ"</note> (with a rough breathing mark above), but as *ka* <note>original: "κά"</note>, using the mark of an Apostrophe (as a sign of contraction): yet at least the aspiration of the consonant *k* <note>original: "κ"</note>, (made from *k* <note>original: "κ"</note> because of the aspiration of the word *ho* <note>original: "ὁ"</note>,) precedes the entire vowel *ō* <note>original: "ω"</note>, which is fused from *a* and *o* <note>original: "α & ὁ"</note>. And so, just as the (intermediate) breathing can be prefixed, so the (intermediate) vowel can be suffixed (if it so seems fit) to the vowel, as if made from the extremes: a transposition that is not absurd. *Tryphon* the Grammarian—who is much more ancient (living around the time of Augustus), being the son of Ammonius, who succeeded Aristarchus in the Alexandrian School; as Servius notes from Varro in the manuscript *On the Eight Parts of Speech* in the library of Magdalen College, Oxford—agrees with *Winterton* in the excerpts *On the Alterations of Words* <note>original: "περὶ παθῶν λέξεων"</note> (found in *Henri Estienne* and *Scapula*). Indeed, (explaining *synaloepha* <term>synaloepha: the merging of two syllables into one</term>,) he says that from *kai egō* <note>original: "καὶ ἐγὼ"</note>, *kagō* <note>original: "κἀγὼ"</note> is formed through *thlipsis* <term>thlipsis: the squeezing out or suppression of a consonant</term> and *crasis* <term>crasis: the mixing of two vowels into one long vowel</term>. Thus, this *iota* began to be subscripted <term>iota subscript: a small 'i' written under a vowel</term> later, after the time of Tryphon.

For a similar reason; in the Aorist *ephēna* <note>original: "ἔφηνα"</note> (from *phainō, phanō* <note>original: "φαίνω, φανῶ"</note>,) and similar cases, regarding which Authors and the most accurate Printers vary (sometimes writing *ephēna* with an *iota* subscript, sometimes writing *ephēna* without it); I judge that the *iota* should rather be omitted; since the *iota* of the Present *phainō* had already been lost in the Future *phanō*, from which (not from the Present) the Aorist is immediately formed. Similarly, I write *exērathē, exēranthēn* <note>original: "ἐξηράθη, ἐξηράνθην"</note> rather than *exēranthē, exēranthēn* <note>original: "ἐξηράνθη, ἐξηράνθην"</note>; since in the Future Active *xeranō* <note>original: "ξηρανῶ"</note> (whether it comes from *xeranō* or *xerainō*), the *iota* is not held; and therefore neither in *xēranai, exēranka, exēramai, exēranthēn, exēranthē* <note>original: "ξηρᾶναι, ἐξήρανκα, ἐξήραμαι, ἐξηράνθην, ἐξηράνθη"</note>, and others like them.

In other words, where the *iota* subscript is missing in these manuscripts (which happens frequently), I supply it based on the analogy of the language. Understand the same regarding accents, whether omitted or wrongly placed, and other graphical errors.

Yet I would not claim that everything is so secure that no error has occurred, either through my own or the typesetters' carelessness. Nor do I always satisfy myself. For example, in the word *analogon* <note>original: "ἀνάλογον"</note>, (which recurs quite often), I follow the manuscripts (though almost unwillingly). Indeed, in Euclid (the master of these matters) I think it ought to be written separately; *ana logon* <note>original: "ἀνὰ λόγον"</note>, that is (as Henri Estienne interprets from Plato under the word *analogos*), *according to the same ratio* <note>original: "ἀνὰ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον"</note>. And it is used Adverbially (and thus with any case, gender, or number); nor do I remember if *analogos* ever occurs in Euclid used Adjectivally; I know not if it occurs in Archimedes or other Greek Mathematicians, unless it happened through the carelessness of recent Printers, which I do not deny has occurred. And although I would not deny that it could be written as one word (like *ekdolōs, monony, exarchēs, entinōn* <note>original: "ἐκδόλως, μονονὺ, ἐξαρχῆς, ἐντινῶν"</note>, and countless others); it would then have to be written *analógōn* <note>original: "ἀναλόγων"</note> (with the accent on the second-to-last syllable, not the third-to-last), because in the connection of such words (which are not enclitics), the accent of the latter word is usually retained; in the same way that *anameson* and *anameros* <note>original: "ἀναμέσον, ἀναμέρος"</note> are written (as the same Estienne reports, under the word *ana*). I would say the same of *anamalin*, which however is usually written *anapalin* <note>original: "ἀνάπαλιν"</note>. As for the word *krinai* <note>original: "κρῖναι"</note> (which occurs several times), some of the manuscripts are accustomed to write *krinai* <note>original: "κρῖναι"</note>, others *krinein* <note>original: "κρίνειν"</note>. I retain the latter, which I have here; for a long vowel; since the Grammarians teach that the penultimate syllables of these Aorists are long, even when the penultimate of the Future is short, as in *phainō, phanō, ephēna* <note>original: "φαίνω, φανῶ, ἔφηνα"</note>. On the other hand, I retain *deiknynai* <note>original: "δεικνύναι"</note>, where some of the manuscripts have *deiknyein* <note>original: "δεικνύειν"</note>; because I see that the Infinitives of verbs in *-mi* <note>original: "μι"</note> are usually written this way. But to pursue every single instance is neither my intention nor is it necessary.

Numerical letters, where they occur, I sometimes change into full words; sometimes I retain them: and indeed, for cardinal numbers <note>e.g., one, two, three</note>, they are marked with a straight line above; for ordinals <note>e.g., first, second, third</note>, they are marked with a small stroke like an acute accent (for I see them often marked thus in the MSS.): and so *a, b, g* <note>original: "α̅, β̅, γ̅"</note> for *one, two, three*; but *a', b', g'* <note>original: "αʹ, βʹ, γʹ"</note> for *first* and similarly in others: (wherever it occurs otherwise, it is an error:) however, in the notes (since I found it inconvenient for the typesetters to always place that in smaller letters), we have omitted the small line above, but the Reader should understand it as if it were there.

As far as punctuation is concerned; since it was frequently (in all these manuscripts) either omitted or wrongly placed (and thus would be either of no use or even a hindrance); I have used the liberty of changing them at my discretion, and restoring them so that (as much as I could) they might best serve to clarify the sense. This was indeed a matter of no small labor: for in countless places, where the marks of punctuation were either entirely missing or (what is worse) were wrongly and confusedly placed; it was not easy to guess (especially in

<meta>This page continues the preface, focusing on the technical principles of punctuation and syntax used in the edition. The author is explaining his editorial choices to ensure clarity for the reader.</meta>

-># P R E F A C E.<-

<warning>Darkening and minor bleed-through on the left edge of the page.</warning>

in longer sentences, and those full of **transpositions** <note>original: *ὑπερϐατῶν* (hyperbaton); a rhetorical figure where the natural order of words is disrupted</note>, which are at the same time teeming with errors) where each part looks, and to what things it is connected.

And since different people use different methods of punctuation; and it is clearly difficult to fix certain laws for this business, since much must be left to judgment: nevertheless, lest I seem to have acted without reason, if by chance I have sometimes done otherwise than another might wish; I thought it should be said in a few words what I proposed to myself in this matter.

That complete sentences are to be closed with a full distinction (as they call it <note>The "full distinction" refers to a period or full stop</note>), there is no one who doubts; and indeed followed, for the most part, by a capital letter; at least unless a new thought, attached to the preceding one, adheres to it in such a way that it seems as if suspended from it; in which case, a following lowercase letter warns us to consider the sense as not yet perfect, or at least less perfect than it is soon to be.

But where a sentence comes to be distributed into several members, and each of these perhaps into several clauses, and these into several particles, or even further sub-distributed (which here, in the Greek text, frequently happens): I have attempted to distinguish the members of the first distribution with a larger point; their clauses with a smaller one; and the parts of these with a smaller one still; and so on, as far as the scarcity of points allowed: so that a larger point, where it occurs, suggests a look back beyond those things which had previously been distinguished by smaller points; and a larger distinction rarely occurs (and only by necessity) between some parts of a clause than had been at the beginning of that clause.

Now I consider a Colon to be a larger point than a Comma; and that with a following capital letter larger than with a following lowercase letter; likewise in Latin (but not in Greek) another point is usually placed between the Colon and Comma (which is smaller than the former, larger than the latter) as if mixed from both <note>The author is likely referring to the semicolon</note>; but the greatest of all is the Period, unless (besides points of this kind) there is use of Paragraphs, Sections, and other such larger distinctions.

For this cause which I have mentioned; when a common word comes to be referred to two or more things (distinguished by a point); (whether that common word precedes or follows;) I am likewise accustomed to separate it by some point (perhaps differently than others do) so that it may immediately appear to the eye that it is to be referred to both, or to each one.

Likewise, where the subject or predicate of a proposition (as they say), (or another similar thing), consists of several words which are nevertheless held as one term, I not infrequently close it with a comma (or even interpose it between two commas), so that it may be immediately perceived how far it extends.

I do the same (for indeed the uses of the comma are very many) if ever it would be somewhat ambiguous whether a certain word should be thought to cohere with the preceding or the following words; for indeed, by interjecting a comma in this way, hesitation is removed.

Nor does it disturb me that, by doing this, a small point might perhaps intervene (which some people avoid) between the Nominative Case (as they say) and the Verb: For since it has long been accepted to distinguish the Article **who, what** <note>original: *ὁ, τὶ*</note> from the Conjunction **that** <note>original: *ὅτι*</note> by interjecting a comma (and similarly, for the same cause, **who, what** and **that, what** from the Adverbs **that, then** <note>original: *ὅτι, τότε*</note>): much more will it be permitted if ever it is doubtful whether a whole word should be referred here or there.

And I do the same, sometimes, where Emphasis (or something of the sort) suggests that a word should be brought forth distinctly and with a pause; (as, for example, where some word comes to be defined; for then both the Thing Defined and the Definition must be weighed separately:) For since the first and chief use of the Comma was to suggest a delay in utterance; wherever a clearer understanding demands a delay of this kind, I will not shrink from interposing a comma in that same place.

For the same cause; where something is missing, to be understood, or repeated **in common** <note>original: *ἀπὸ κοινοῦ* (apo koinou); a construction where a single word applies to two or more clauses</note> from a prior clause to complete the sense; I am accustomed (as others are) to suggest that defect with a comma.

Likewise; where, by the interjection of a word or even a clause, the Syntax is interrupted; I set apart that interjected part (like a parenthesis) with two commas placed here and there; so that the sequence of the syntax, these being removed, may become more manifest.

And it is also accepted among others, where the interjection is not so long, to insert two parentheses one within the other; which although it can sometimes be borne, it is nevertheless considered more elegant to disguise it in this way.

I also frequently employ the marks of Parenthesis in Latin; as those which (after several interjected things) return the eye to the preceding [matters]

<meta>catchword: matters,</meta>

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, specifically discussing the technical challenges of punctuation, diagrams, and the transliteration of Greek characters into Latin for the reader's convenience.</meta>

-> # PREFACE <-

<column-break/>

dence, with which the following things cohere. I have not, however, placed these marks in the Greek text; for these marks appear nowhere in any of the Codices <note>Codex: a manuscript book, as opposed to a scroll.</note> which I am using; and they were, unless I am mistaken, received into common use later than when the text of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the celebrated mathematician and astronomer whose work on music theory is being edited here.</note> was written; they can be more safely omitted here because the Latin translation is placed in view right beside it.

Where I do employ these marks, I do not, for that reason, omit the remaining punctuation; but for the most part (for these also have their own use in the same place), I add them as if the others were absent. These are the primary things to which I usually attend in this business of punctuation.

But a scarcity of punctuation marks does not always permit each of these to be observed: sometimes also, if it were done, the multitude of marks would overwhelm the sense rather than illuminate it. In which case, those that can be more safely absent are omitted; and since we cannot observe everything, we must at least attend to those things (as physicians teach) which indicate the meaning more strongly.

Finally (since it is not worth the effort to demand every such minute detail too scrupulously), if any error has been made either by my own or the typesetters' <note>original: "typothetarum"</note> carelessness (which I perceive has happened a few times), I hope that in the meantime it neither occurs often nor will be of such importance as to disturb the sense; and that the Reader himself, without being warned, may easily correct it.

In the Diagrams <note>original: "Schematismus," referring to the musical or mathematical figures.</note>; I have used almost the same liberty: namely, of filling in, cutting back, changing, and adapting them to our use. For here also the Codices vary greatly; and not only some from others, but often the same one from itself; since the same diagrams were repeated several times in various forms (for instance, when the first description did not please the scribe, he attempted to bring it into a better state by reforming it in different ways). Many things were also added in the manner of scholia <note>Scholia: ancient marginal notes or comments.</note>, of which there was no hint in Ptolemy’s text, nor any necessity, and which could better be absent.

Such is the diagram that appears on page 2; where the difficulty of the words is not so great that a diagram is needed to understand them: I have retained it, however, because in all the Codices (though with some variation), such a thing appeared.

And I have retained others here and there which appeared in the codices, even if the text did not expressly suggest them, which might serve to aid the imagination in more clearly understanding things delivered obscurely or in a complicated manner.

But also, by analogy with them, I have sometimes supplied others which seemed to be missing, when the same reasoning seemed to require it. These things, however, and those similar to them which the text itself does not indicate; you may, as far as I am concerned, regard as Scholia.

In other matters; those things which were described by tangled semicircles, or at least circular arcs (which was the custom of the Ancient Musicians), we describe instead with Brackets <note>original: "Uncinis"</note> designating the same thing: as being more convenient for the typesetters; especially where words were to be inserted into the diagram.

Likewise, we have changed other things so that the same Diagram might serve equally for both the Greek text and the Latin translation.

Indeed, since it did not seem advisable to repeat the same diagram (in the Text and the Translation) with only the letters changed: one of these two things seemed necessary; Namely, either the Greek letters (which are assigned to the diagram) had to be retained in the Latin translation (which I had intended to do at one time, except that, besides other inconveniences regarding the elegance of the page, someone might eventually read the Latin who is not very well versed in Greek): Or (as others have done) Latin letters should be substituted in the translation for the Greek ones appearing in the diagram (which I might perhaps have done if the order of letters among the Latins were exactly the same as among the Greeks; since it is not, I feared obscurity, having experienced it with others in a similar case): Or finally (which seemed better) both sets of letters should be assigned to the diagram; one of which would serve the text, the other the translation. I have followed this, therefore, lest readers should sometimes have to hesitate over which Latin letters correspond to which Greek ones.

In adapting the Latin to the Greek, I have followed (as much as possible) the order of each Alphabet; Namely, thus (for the most part):

| α | β | γ | δ | ε | ζ | η | ϑ | ι | κ | λ | μ | ν | ξ | ο | π | ρ | σ | τ | υ |
|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|:-:|
| A | B | C | D | E | F | G | H | I | K | L | M | N | X | O | P | R | S | T | V |

Indeed, in this way (excepting F G H, for ζ η ϑ <note>zeta, eta, theta</note>), I substitute F G H in the Latin for ς ζ η <note>stigma, zeta, eta</note>.

The Latin letters correspond most aptly to the Greek. (If, however, ς <note>stigma</note> occurs in the Greek between ε and ζ; to do these things; and likewise for the Greek words to be inserted into the diagram, their own Latin equivalents...)

<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, discussing the technical challenges of translating ancient musical theory and the shortcomings of previous editions.</meta>

# P R E F A C E.

->B 2<-

I have added Latin translations; occasionally the diagrams had to be arranged differently (to avoid confusion) than I found them in the manuscripts, yet in such a way that they still correspond to the text. However, where nearly the same words were to be written in both Greek and Latin letters, I decided to omit sometimes the latter (since the former sufficed) and I retain them (to favor those to whom Greek might be less familiar); sometimes I retain the Greek (because it seemed better to serve the text than the translation); and sometimes (if space conveniently permitted) I included both.

The Latin Version by *Antonius Gogavinus* of Grave, published in Venice in the year 1562, was undoubtedly made with good intentions and a laudable effort; I would not wish to disparage it. I would prefer to excuse it rather than—as Meibomius <note>Marcus Meibom (c. 1630–1710), a Danish scholar who published a famous collection of ancient Greek musical texts in 1652.</note> does in his preface to Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and the most important ancient writer on music theory.</note>—sharply criticize it. But skill in the Greek language was, in that age, somewhat rarer (though he was moderately instructed in it, and indeed well for that time); and it was rarer still for one and the same person to understand both Greek and Harmonics <note><term>Harmonics</term>: The science of musical sounds and intervals.</note> simultaneously. Likewise, he seems to have used a manuscript that was quite corrupted; and although he says he compared it with others (which perhaps were not much better), judgment is needed in selecting the better readings among variants. Furthermore, he did not seem to sufficiently understand the subject matter itself (which he complains is obscure and very difficult). It is no wonder, then, if that version was both everywhere obscure and perplexed, and quite often wandered from the truth.

It was therefore necessary for me to prepare a completely new translation (since the one that existed was more difficult to understand than the Greek text itself). In this matter, anyone who compares our version with that one will easily perceive what I have achieved.

However, I did not think it necessary to always point out in the Notes I have added when and for what reason I depart from it; partly because that book is in many hands (so that whoever wishes may compare both versions with each other), and partly because I would not wish (since it would have to be done so often) to appear insulting or to be hunting for every occasion to find fault. For I am well aware of how much labor it takes to extract a corrected book from Manuscripts (mostly corrupted and written out by unskilled scribes). I thought it better to praise a good and not unlearned man who performed the task such that now, after more than a hundred years, no one has yet been found who has better edited this excellent but corrupted author.

For the same reason (because that book is in many hands), I did not think it necessary to note (at least rarely) when the manuscript he used seems to have varied from ours (which could often be guessed from the translation) or (where ours vary) which of these he agreed with; or even to repeat the faulty numbers found in his work.

This at least should be noted: just as individual Manuscripts have their own errors, many of these were common to all of them, so that it is certain they are quite old and were transferred from some manuscripts into others. This is especially true regarding the numbers, which therefore could not be restored except by performing the calculations anew. This was necessary throughout the entire work, and it has been done.

In the Manuscripts that I encountered, I have quite scrupulously included the variant readings (since those manuscripts are in the hands of few people), so that if anyone prefers a reading other than ours, they would be free to do so.

In the translation, I have not sought elegance (provided the speech is not barbaric) so much as clarity of meaning. Thus, I sometimes change the order of the Greek words and the structure of the syntax so that the connection of things becomes more manifest; and <term>hyperbata</term> <note>A rhetorical figure where the natural word order is disrupted.</note> are avoided as much as possible, or softened by punctuation and parentheses. I have also inserted words (where it seemed necessary, distinguished by a different font style) to illuminate clauses that would otherwise be obscure. I kept this primarily in view: to explain the matter itself clearly and to fulfill the role of a faithful translator as best I could.

The Notes which I thought should be added (whether regarding the Greek words or the subject matter itself), I preferred to place at the bottom of the page (rather than at the end of the book), so that they might be in view along with the Text.

Among these notes, since symbols not commonly used elsewhere had to be employed, I will explain them here so they do not delay anyone. Namely: **+** is the sign for Addition; **-** the sign for Subtraction; **×** for Multiplication; **=** the sign for Equality; **>** for Majority (or *greater than*); **<** for Minority (or *less than*); and finally **÷** (and similar signs) is the indication for Division.

# PREFACE.
## TABLE
<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

(for instance, the number 3 to be divided by 2;) or even (which amounts to the same thing) the notation for a Fraction or (as they say) a Broken number (whether it be, in their terms, a proper or improper Fraction;) or finally, an indication of a Ratio or Proportion; such that the ratio $\frac{3}{2}$ is that ratio which exists between the numbers 3 and 2; or that which is named from $\frac{3}{2} = 1 \frac{1}{2}$, for instance *sesquialtera* <note>original: "sesquialtera"; a ratio of 3:2, literally "one and a half".</note>; and thus the ratio $\frac{3}{2}$ is the same as the ratio of 3 to 2. And similarly in other cases. Sometimes I also indicate Division in this way, 2) 8 (4; meaning that the number 8, divided by 2, yields the Quotient 4. Indeed, I am accustomed to indicate the Composition and Dissolution of Ratios by employing the signs for Multiplication and Division. (Since what is called the "composition of ratios" is done by multiplying their Denominators or Exponents by one another; and "dissolution" by dividing the Denominators in the same way.) For example, a double ratio $\frac{2}{1}$ <note>A 2:1 ratio, or an octave.</note>, composed of a *sesquialtera* $\frac{3}{2}$ <note>A perfect fifth.</note> and a *sesquitertia* $\frac{4}{3}$ <note>A perfect fourth.</note>, is indicated thus: $\frac{3}{2} \times \frac{4}{3} = \frac{12}{6} = \frac{2}{1}$. Likewise, from the ratio $\frac{2}{1}$, the ratio $\frac{3}{2}$ being removed leaves the ratio $\frac{4}{3}$: because $\frac{2}{1} \div \frac{3}{2} = \frac{4}{3}$. And similarly in other instances.

Last of all, I have subjoined an *Appendix, concerning the Harmonics of the Ancients compared to that of Today*. In this, I have gathered into order—if not everything—at least the principal matters (which seemed of some importance) that occur scattered throughout the writings of the Ancient Musicians (Aristoxenus, Euclid, Nicomachus, Alypius, Gaudentius, Bacchius, Aristides Quintilianus, Martianus Capella) edited by Meibomius; likewise Ptolemy, and (not yet edited) Porphyry and Bryennius, as well as some things from Boethius and others. This is so that one may perceive at a single glance (albeit summarily) what the Harmonics of the Ancients was, insofar as it has descended to us. I have retained the Greek terms and formulas of speech (as so many technical words of the art <term>vocabulis artis: technical terminology</term>) and noted the passages of the Authors from which they were taken. I have compared all these things with modern Music in such a way that the learned Reader (not ignorant of modern Music) may easily perceive what is common to both, even if under different modes of expression. I judged this would be useful for a more correct understanding of Ancient Harmonics.

However, I would not want the Reader to expect that everything regarding Ancient Music be brought forth here (of which there were many other parts besides Harmonics); much less that the Construction or Use of Musical Instruments, whether Ancient or more Recent, be handed down here (and other matters which pertain to *Organics* <note>The study of instruments.</note> or *Rhythmics*, rather than *Harmonics*). Indeed, that is foreign to my purpose; as one who has undertaken to publish Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*, to explain them to some extent, and, on this occasion, to treat those things which pertain to the Harmonic part of Music. Therefore, I have kept myself within these boundaries, leaving the rest to others.

And these are roughly the things we thought the Reader should be advised of in the first Edition. The day before the Calends of January <note>December 31st.</note> of the soon-to-begin Year of the Christian Era, 1682. Except that some variant readings, which I found it necessary to cast to the end because some of the Manuscripts came into my hands rather late, are now inserted in their proper places.

->**JOHN WALLIS.**<-

## Harmonics: Book Three.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
## ON HARMONICS, IN THREE BOOKS,
### THE FIRST BOOK.

---

-> 1. <- -> CHAPTER I. <-
-> On the Criteria in Harmonics. <-

**HARMONICS** <note>original: "Harmonica"</note> is a power capable of perceiving the differences between high and low pitch in sounds. Sound is an affection or "passion" of the air being struck; it is the first and most general object of hearing. There are two criteria of harmony: hearing and reason. However, they do not function in the same way. Hearing is concerned with the matter and the affection, while reason is concerned with the form and the cause. For, generally speaking, it is the property of the senses to find that which is "near enough" <note>i.e., approximate</note> and to receive accuracy from elsewhere; whereas it is the property of reason to receive the "near enough" from elsewhere and to find that which is accurate. Since, therefore, matter is limited and defined only by form, and affections only by the causes of motions—and of these, the former (matter and affections) are suited to sense, while the latter (form and causes) are suited to reason—it follows naturally that sensory perceptions must be defined and bounded by rational ones. The senses must supply the first differences of sounds to reason, though these are taken somewhat crudely (from what can be discerned by sense); then, by reason, they are led to the point of being finally accurate and beyond doubt. This is because it is the nature of reason to be something simple...

---

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 1.

[Reason] is simple and free from mixture; and thus perfect in itself and well-ordered; and always relating to the same things in the same way. Sense perception, however, is always joined to matter, which is mixed in many ways and subject to change; so much so that, because of this instability <note>original: "materiæ" (of matter)</note>, the perception of all men is not the same, nor is the perception of the same men always the same when directed toward the same objects. Rather, perception requires (as if it were a walking stick) the Correction that comes from Reason. Just as a circle drawn only by the help of the eye is often thought to be sufficiently accurate, until a circle made according to Reason <note>i.e., using a compass or geometric principles</note> leads the eye to recognize that which is truly accurate; so too, if a specific difference between sounds is captured by the Hearing alone, it will indeed sometimes seem at first neither to fall short of the right measure nor to exceed it. But when another sound is compared to it, taken according to the proper Ratio, that first sound will often be proven wrong, as the Hearing now recognizes—by means of the comparison—that this latter sound is more accurate and, as it were, genuine, compared to that former "bastard" sound. Since also (speaking generally) it is easier to pass judgment on something than to perform it oneself (for example, to judge a wrestling match rather than to wrestle; to judge dancing rather than to dance; to judge the blowing of pipes rather than to blow them; to judge a song rather than to sing it); and since the senses, however imperfect, are capable of discerning simply whether one thing differs from another or not—

<column-break/>

—and unmixed; and because it is self-complete <note>original: "αὐτοτελῆ"</note> and ordered <note>original: "τεταγμένον"</note>, and always relates to the same things in the same way. But perception is always with matter, which is both many-mixed and flowing; so that, because neither the same perception, nor that of all people, nor that of the same people always, is preserved as the same toward objects that are similarly situated; but it needs, as if some staff, the correction <note>original: "ἐπανορθώσεως"</note> from reason. Just as, therefore, the circle described by sight alone often seems to be accurate, until the one made by reason leads it to the recognition of the truly accurate; so even if some defined difference of sounds is taken by the hearing, it will seem immediately, sometimes, neither to fall short of the measure nor to exceed it; but when the one grasped according to the proper ratio is applied, the former will often be refuted, as the hearing recognizes, by the comparison, the more accurate one as being like a genuine thing compared to a forged bastard. Since also, in general, to judge something is easier than to do it oneself; such as to judge wrestling <note>original: "παλαῖσαι"</note> than to wrestle <note>original: "παλαίειν"</note>; and to judge dancing than to dance; and to judge pipe-playing than to play the pipes; and to judge singing than to sing. And therefore the stumbling deficiency of the senses, for knowing simply the difference or—

-> Perception (Sensus) **finds** that which is **Near** (Propinquum). <-
-> *receives* <-
-> Reason (Ratio) **finds** that which is **Accurate** (Accuratum). <-

<note>This diagram illustrates the core of Ptolemy's epistemology: the senses provide a "close enough" approximation, but Reason is required to reach mathematical precision.</note>

<margin>
*f* G. "this however." But here that manuscript recedes, both from the others and from J. itself, from which I know it was copied. In such a case, I usually ignore the variation of manuscript G. from the others (since it is certain to be a scribe's error and not a deliberate judgment), so as not to weary the reader in vain.
</margin>

<margin>
* KVO. "self-perfect" S. "so as to be." C. "existing." And I believe this last occurred (as is common in Manuscript books) because of the similar pronunciation of the letters *eta* (η) and *iota* (ι) among many people long ago. <note>This refers to "itacism," a shift in Greek pronunciation where different vowels began to sound like "ee".</note>
</margin>

<margin>
c C. "of reason." With the subscript [iota] omitted in all. And this indeed occurs constantly in that manuscript (copied in the East, as is clear from the paper and the character of the handwriting, or perhaps in Egypt): namely, that the subscript iota is seen either never or very rarely. The same thing often happens in other manuscripts (namely, that the subscript is neglected); but since it is clear that this was done through negligence, and the manuscripts do not agree with each other on this, nor even the same manuscript in various places, I usually supply the omitted letter (even without warning the reader, as it is not of great importance).
</margin>

<margin>
n I restore "stumbling" <note>original: "πταιστὴ"</note> here instead of "deception" <note>original: "ἀπατη"</note>; because of the word "difference" which follows.
</margin>

The page includes a diagram showing that Sensation finds the "near" while Reason finds the "accurate," alongside extensive philological notes on manuscript variations.</summary>

<meta>catchword: CHAPTER.</meta>

<summary>This page explains that while the senses can easily distinguish large differences, they fail at minute measurements, requiring mathematical reason and physical tools (like the rule and compass) to achieve accuracy. It argues that hearing, like sight, must be guided by the rational part of the soul to judge complex harmonic ratios.</summary>
<keywords>harmonics, sensory perception, mathematical reason, measurement, compass, rule, soul, ratios, error accumulation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory chapters of Ptolemy's Harmonics, moving from the general definition of the discipline to the specific goals of the harmonic scientist and the tools they employ.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 2.

## CHAP. II.
## 2.

->What the Goal of the Harmonic Scientist is.<- ->What the purpose<gloss>a</gloss> of Harmonics<gloss>b</gloss> is.<-

<image-desc>Initial letter H is a large plain capital.</image-desc>The instrument for such an inquiry is called the Harmonic Canon <note>original: "Canon Harmonicus"; a "canon" refers both to a physical monochord and a rule or law</note>; taking its name from common usage, and because (like a rule) it directs those things in which the senses fail toward the truth itself. The goal of the harmonic scientist here is to preserve throughout the rational hypotheses (or suppositions) of the Canon, which in no way conflict with the senses, as most people suppose. Just as it is the task of the Astronomer <note>original: "Astrologi"; in antiquity, astrology and astronomy were often used interchangeably for the study of celestial motions</note> to preserve the hypotheses of the heavenly motions so they are consistent with observed transitions; these hypotheses are themselves derived from observations made more roughly, but through the help of Reason, they discover the particulars as accurately as possible. For in all things, it is the duty of the contemplative person and the user of science to show that the works of Nature are created according to a certain Reason and a well-ordered cause, and that nothing is made by her rashly or by chance; and especially in these most beautiful arrangements, such as those of the senses that participate most in Reason: Sight and Hearing. This Goal, indeed, some seem not to have considered at all, attending only to manual exercise and the bare, irrational practice of the sense; others followed the end in a more speculative manner (these were chiefly the Pythagoreans on one hand, and the Aristoxenians on the other), but both were in error. For the Pythagoreans (not sufficiently obeying the warning of Hearing in all things where it was necessary) applied those Proportions of sound differences which are often inconsistent with the phenomena; and by this, they brought reproach upon this criterion among those who hold the opposite opinion. The Aristoxenians, however, giving too much weight to those things perceived through the sense, used Reason only in passing, as it were; both contrary to its nature and contrary to what is found by experience. Contrary to its nature, because they do not apply Numbers (the images of ratios) to the Differences of sounds, but to their Intervals. Contrary to experience, because they add those divisions which least agree with the testimony of the senses. Each of these points will become manifest from what is to be brought forward later, after those things pertaining to the sequence of what is to be continuously delivered have been defined.

<image-desc>Initial letter Τ is a large plain capital.</image-desc>The instrument of such an approach<gloss>c</gloss> is called the harmonic canon; named from the common category and from "canonizing" (ruling) those things lacking in the senses toward the truth<gloss>d</gloss>. The purpose of the harmonic scientist would be to preserve in every way the rational hypotheses of the canon, in no way fighting against the senses, according to what is mostly understood. Just as for the Astronomer, the purpose is to preserve the hypotheses of the heavenly motions, in harmony with the observed periods; these being taken from things appearing clearly and more generally, but finding through reason the parts in detail, as accurately as possible. For in all things it is characteristic of the theorist and scientist to show that the works of Nature are crafted with a certain reason and ordered cause; and nothing is completed by her<gloss>e</gloss> randomly or as it happened; and especially in such beautiful constructions as happen to be those of the more rational senses<gloss>f</gloss>, sight and hearing. Of this purpose<gloss>g</gloss>, some seem to have cared not at all<gloss>h</gloss>; attending only to the manual use and the bare and irrational habit of the sense; others approached the end more theoretically. These would be especially the Pythagoreans and the Aristoxenians<gloss>k</gloss>. Both erred<gloss>l</gloss>. For the Pythagoreans<gloss>m</gloss>, not following the application of hearing even in those things where it was necessary for all<gloss>n</gloss>, fitted the differences of sounds to ratios that were in many ways foreign<gloss>q</gloss> to the phenomena<gloss>r</gloss>; so as to create a slander against such a criterion among those of the opposite opinion. But the Aristoxenians<gloss>s</gloss>, giving the most to things grasped through the sense<gloss>t</gloss>, used reason as a side-path, as it were; both against reason itself<gloss>o</gloss> and against the phenomenon<gloss>u</gloss>. Against reason itself, because they do not fit numbers to the differences of sounds<gloss>p</gloss>—these being the images of the ratios<gloss>s</gloss>—but to their intervals. And against the phenomenon, because they apply divisions<gloss>x</gloss> to these sensory agreements in inappropriate ways<gloss>v</gloss>. Each of these will be clear from the things to be brought forward, if first the things contributing to the subsequent sequence receive a certain definition.

---

<note>The following are textual variants and scholarly notes from the original editor, comparing different Greek manuscripts (A, B, C, D, etc.).</note>

<header>Chapter 3. HARMONICS BOOK I.</header>
<sig>C 3</sig>

# CHAPTER III.
## 3.

### How High Pitch and Low Pitch are constituted in sounds.
### How the Sharpness and Heaviness of sounds are established.

---

Since the difference in sounds (as in all other things) consists of both **Quality** and **Quantity**, and is established around **high pitches** <note>original: ὀξύτητας / acumen</note> and **low pitches** <note>original: βαρύτητας / gravitas</note>, it is not easy to declare immediately to which of these two categories the aforementioned should be assigned, until the causes of such occurrences have been examined. These causes seem to me to be somewhat common to the variations found in the other senses as well. For the sensations <note>original: πάθη / passiones</note> arising from them become different partly due to the force of the striker, partly due to the physical compositions of both the body being struck and the medium through which the strike occurs, and further due to the shape of the struck object in relation to the starting point of the motion. For clearly, while other factors remain the same, each of the mentioned elements produces something peculiar regarding the sensation, which differs according to its specific manner.

Now, regarding sounds: the difference arising from the compositions of the struck body would either not occur at all, or at least would not be perceptible, because the variation of the air <note>the medium of sound</note> is so constituted in relation to human sense. However, the difference arising from the force of the striker would be the cause of **magnitude** <note>volume</note> only, and not of high or low pitch. For we see no such alteration in the sounds of the same things when people speak more quietly, for instance, or more loudly; or again when they blow or strike more gently, or more vehemently and robustly; rather, the more forceful strike only results in a greater sound, and the weaker in a lesser one.

The variation resulting from the instruments through which the strikes occur takes certain qualities from the primary conditions of the body (namely, those by which each thing is either dense or thin, or thick or smooth, or rough); and also from their **shapes**. (For what is there in common between the most passive senses—I mean smells, tastes, and colors—and the reporting of a strike?) By means of shapes, in those things capable of receiving them (such as tongues and mouths), a configuration is produced, acting like certain laws imposed upon sounds. From these are formed crashes, thuds, voices, clangs, and countless such things; while we ourselves imitate each of these configurations, because man possesses a governing mind that is most rational and most skillful. Through...

---
