<summary>This chapter investigates whether pitch (high/low) is a matter of quality or quantity, concluding that the force of a strike affects volume (magnitude) rather than pitch, while the shape and material of the instrument define the character of the sound.</summary>
<keywords>pitch, high pitch, low pitch, acoustics, percussion, magnitude, sound quality, vocal anatomy, harmonics</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of sound from Chapter 3 of Ptolemy's Harmonics, specifically addressing how the material density and tension of an object affect its pitch.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 3.

As for the quality of smoothness or roughness, this likewise produces only that distinction by which certain sounds are called by the same name, "smooth" or "rough"; for these are also qualities properly so called. Through the quality of rarity or density, and thickness or thinness, we likewise call other sounds by the names "loose" or "compact," and also "thick" or "slender." Furthermore, we consider here the qualities of Deepness <note>original: *Gravitatis*; low pitch</note> and Sharpness <note>original: *Acuminis*; high pitch</note>; because each of the aforementioned conditions, although it is a quality, arises from the **Quantity** of the substance. For that which has more substance in an equal volume is denser (or more compact). And (among things of similar constitution) that which has more substance in an equal length is thicker. Now, the things that produce a sharper sound are the more compact and the more slender; while those that produce a deeper sound are the rarer and the thicker. (Indeed, even in other matters, something is said to be sharper because it is more slender: just as something is blunter because it is thicker.) Moreover, they strike more forcefully—both the more slender things, because they can penetrate more quickly; and the more compact things, because they have more mass.

Hence it is that bronze produces a sharper sound than wood; and a string (*made of gut*) sharper than flax: because they are more compact. Among pieces of bronze of equal density and equal length, that which is more slender produces the sharper sound. Among strings of equal density and equal length, the one that is finer. And hollow things produce a sharper sound than solid ones. Likewise, among windpipes <note>original: *Arteriarum*; referring to the anatomical or musical pipes</note>, those that are denser and smaller are of a sharper tone. Yet none of these things acts properly because it is compact or slender, but because it is **Tense** <note>original: *Tensum*; under tension</note>: since things that are so constituted tend to be more capable of tension. That which is more tense (in its striking) is also more vehement; and this is more forceful; and this, finally, is sharper. 

And therefore, it is clear that by whatever other reason a thing becomes more tense, it emits a sharper sound; for instance, because it is harder; or because it is absolutely greater, or prevailing; namely, when the unity [of one quality] prevails over the other ratio by an excess (*or preponderance*) in two things, both of which possess the power to produce such an effect. For example, when bronze emits a sharper sound than lead, it is because it is harder by a greater proportion than the lead is denser than the bronze. Likewise, even if a piece of bronze is larger and thicker, it emits a sharper sound than a smaller and more slender one, provided that the ratio of its magnitude is greater than the ratio of its thickness. For sound is a certain continuous tension of the air, passing from that air which is contained within the things making the stroke to the air which is outside. And therefore, by whatever reason any of the things making the stroke is more tense, it produces a result that is both faster and sharper.

<column-break/>

<note>The following is the original Greek text of the passage translated above.</note>

...or the quality of smoothness or roughness, again only according to which some are synonymously called smooth or rough; because these are qualities in the proper sense. But through the quality of rarity or density, and thickness or thinness, according to which again we synonymously call certain sounds loose, and thick or slender. And in the case of deepness and sharpness; because each of the stated constitutions, being a quality, arises from the quantity of the substance. For the denser is that which has more substance in an equal volume. And the thicker, of those similarly constituted, is that which has more substance in an equal length. And the things effective of sharper sounds are the denser and the more slender; of the deeper, the rarer and the thicker. Indeed, in other things, the sharper is said to be so by being more slender, just as the blunter by being thicker. For they strike more collectively, the more slender on account of being able to penetrate more quickly; the denser on account of being more so. And because of this, bronze makes a sharper sound than wood; and a string than flax; for they are denser. Of bronzes of similar density and equal length, the more slender; and of strings of similar density and equal length, the thinner. And hollow things more than solid. And again of the pipes themselves, the denser and more slender are sharper-toned. But each of these is not so because of the density or slenderness itself properly; but because of the tension; because it happens to such things to be more tense; and the more tense, in the strikes, becomes more vehement; and this, more collective; and this, sharper. Wherefore even if it be more tense in another way, such as being harder, or being generally greater, it makes a sharper sound; the [one ratio] prevailing, in those cases where both possess the power of producing it in some way, according to the other ratio of excess; as, when bronze makes a sharper sound than lead; since there is more hardness in it than there is density in the other. And again, if the bronze happens to be larger and thicker, it makes a sharper sound than the smaller and more slender; whenever the ratio according to magnitude is greater than that according to thickness. For sound is a certain continuous tension of the air, reaching from that which is enclosed by the things making the strikes to that which is outside; and because of this, according to whatever power each of the things through which the strikes occur is more tense, it completes it more quickly and sharply.

<summary>This page explains the physical causes of pitch, arguing that while density and thinness contribute to a "sharp" (high) sound, the primary underlying cause is the tension of the material and the air it displaces. It uses comparisons between materials like bronze, wood, and gut strings to illustrate how hardness and magnitude interact to determine tone.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Harmonics, acoustics, pitch, density, tension, bronze, music theory, ancient physics</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 3 of Book I regarding the physical causes of pitch, specifically focusing on the relationship between distance and sound.</meta>

# Chapter 3. HARMONICS BOOK I.

Through these compositions—the dense and the rare, the thin and the thick, the stretched and the slack—it appears that the difference between high and low sounds is a kind of quality, and even more so, it arises from the inequality of the distances between the object being struck and the object striking. For it is most clearly established by the quantity of these distances. From smaller distances, high pitch follows because of the forceful impact resulting from proximity; from larger distances, low pitch follows because of the relaxation <note>original: *laxitatem* / *eklysin*</note> resulting from the distance. Thus, sounds correspond to distances in a reciprocal or inverse ratio. For as the greater distance from the source is to the lesser, so is the sound produced from the lesser distance to that produced from the greater. Just as in the case of weights on a balance: as the greater distance of the hanging weight is to the lesser, so is the momentum at the lesser distance to that at the greater.

That this is manifest is easily observed in sounds produced by a certain length, such as those of **Strings**, **Flutes** <note>original: *tibiarum* / *aulon*, referring to reed instruments like the aulos</note>, and **Windpipes** <note>original: *arteriarum* / *arterion*, referring to the trachea</note>. For they become higher in every case (all other things being equal) in the case of **Strings**, if they are taken at smaller distances between the bridges <note>original: *hypagogion*, the movable bridges used to change string length</note> than at larger ones; and in **Flutes**, if the air breaks out through the holes closer to the mouthpiece <note>original: *hypholmion*, the socket or "receptacle" of the reed</note> (that is, the striking source) than through those further away; and in the **Windpipe**, for those who have the beginning of the percussion higher up and near the part being struck, rather than from the depths.

For the workings of the windpipe resemble a certain natural Flute, differing in this one thing only: in **Flutes**, while the location of the striker remains fixed (namely, in the mouth of the player), the location of the part being struck (at the holes) moves to a position closer or further from the striker through the artifice of the holes. In the **Windpipe**, however, it is the reverse: while the location of the part being struck (the air) remains fixed, the location of the striker moves to a closer or further distance from the part being struck. This occurs as the governing faculties within us <note>the *hegemonikon* or "ruling part" of the soul</note>, through the music implanted in us by nature (both wonderfully and effortlessly), find and employ the locations in the windpipe—acting like a movable bridge—from which the distances to the external air, proportional to their own excesses, produce the differences in sounds.

->CHAPTER<-

<summary>Ptolemy explains that pitch is determined by the distance between the source of a sound and the point of impact, using strings, flutes, and the human windpipe as primary examples of this inverse relationship. He highlights the biological "artistry" of the human throat, which acts like a musical instrument by shifting the point of sound production.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of sound, specifically focusing on the classification of pitches and the limits of human hearing. It follows the previous section which established that high and low pitch are forms of quantity.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 4.

### CHAP. IV.

### 4.

->On Sounds, and their Differences.<-

->On Sounds, and the difference within them.<-

In what manner, therefore, the High Pitch <note>original: *Acumen* / ὀξύτης</note> and Low Pitch <note>original: *Gravitas* / βαρύτης</note> of sound are constituted, and that their form is a certain Quantity <note>original: *Quantitas* / ποσότης</note>; let this much be declared for now. Furthermore, it should be observed that their increases (as with physical magnitudes) are, in potential, infinite; but in actuality, they are finite. Of these, there are two limits: one proper to the sounds themselves, and the other to the sense of hearing; and the latter is greater than the former. For since there is a great diversity in the conditions of things that produce sound: even if the distances from the lowest to the highest for individual things do not differ by any notable excess, nevertheless their extremes often differ greatly: for some, both extremes tend toward the Low; for others, toward the High. The hearing, however, perceives both the lowest of the lower sounds and the highest of the higher, as far as we know how to increase such distances in the construction of instruments. Since these things are so, we must next define them; of sounds, some are Equal-toned <note>original: *Æquitonos* / ἰσότονοι; having a steady pitch</note>; others are Unequal-toned <note>original: *Inæquitonos* / ἀνισότονοι</note>. Equal-toned are those that are unvarying in their tone: Unequal-toned are those that vary their tone. For Tone <note>original: *Tonus* / τόνος; here meaning "tension" or "pitch-holding"</note> in this sense is like a common genus of High and Low; taken as a single form of tension; like a boundary of the beginning and the end. Of the Unequal-toned; some are continuous, others are discrete. Continuous are those that do not have manifest places of transition in either direction; or in which no part remains at an equal pitch for a perceptible interval: (such as happens in the colors of a Rainbow <note>original: *Iris*</note>.) Such are those heard in the very acts of tightening and loosening [strings], while they are still in motion: and likewise, ending in the low, the lowing of Oxen; and in the high, the howling of Wolves. Discrete sounds, however, are those that have manifest places of transition; or when their parts remain at an equal pitch for a perceptible interval: (just as in the distinct placement of colors that are unmixed and unconfused.) But those former (continuous) sounds are foreign to the science of Harmonics; inasmuch as they do not suggest any single, identical thing, so as to be grasped by definition or proportion...

<column-break/>

How then high pitch is constituted <gloss>b)</gloss> of sound, and low pitch <gloss>c)</gloss> and, that some quantity <gloss>d)</gloss> is their form <gloss>e)</gloss> of them <gloss>f)</gloss> let it be sketched through these things. And let it be further understood, that also the <gloss>g)</gloss> increases of them, in potential, happen to be infinite; but in actuality, finite; just as also the <gloss>h)</gloss> magnitudes. And that there are two limits <gloss>h)</gloss> of these: the one proper to the sounds <gloss>i)</gloss> themselves; the other to the hearing; and this one again <gloss>k)</gloss> is greater than that. For while the producers <gloss>l)</gloss> of the sounds <gloss>l)</gloss> vary greatly according to their compositions; even if the distances of each, from its <gloss>m)</gloss> lowest to the highest, differ by nothing notable <gloss>n)</gloss> yet <gloss>o)</gloss> their limits will differ <gloss>p)</gloss> frequently in many ways. For some, both <gloss>q)</gloss> being toward the lower; for others, toward the higher; but the hearing perceives both the lowest of the lower <gloss>r)</gloss>, and the highest of the higher; as far as we may devise <gloss>s)</gloss> such distances in instrumental increases. These things then being so; it must be defined next that, of sounds, some are equal-toned; others are unequal-toned. Equal-toned indeed, are those standing <gloss>u)</gloss> unvarying according to some tone <gloss>t)</gloss>; and those [unequal] are varying. For the so-called <gloss>u)</gloss> tone, would be a common genus <gloss>x)</gloss> of high pitch, and of low pitch; taken according to one form <gloss>y)</gloss> of tension; as the limit or end and <gloss>z)</gloss> of the beginning. Of the unequal-toned; some are continuous; others, discrete. Continuous indeed, are those having the places <gloss>a)</gloss> of the <gloss>b)</gloss> transitions imperceptible <gloss>c)</gloss> or, in which <gloss>d)</gloss> not even one part <gloss>e)</gloss> is equal-toned for a perceptible interval; such as the colors of the Rainbow <gloss>f)</gloss> experience. And such are those in the tightenings themselves, or the <gloss>g)</gloss> loosenings, while still moving <gloss>h)</gloss>, sounded together. And again, toward the lower, the bellowings <gloss>i)</gloss> of trumpets <note>The Latin text translates this as "oxen," but the Greek *bykanismoi* refers to trumpet-calls or horn-sounds</note>; and toward the higher, the howlings <gloss>k)</gloss> of wolves. Discrete are those having the places <gloss>l)</gloss> of transitions manifest <gloss>m)</gloss> or when the parts of them remain <gloss>n)</gloss> equal-toned for a perceptible distance; as in the different side-by-side

placement of unmixed and unconfused colors. But those [continuous] ones are foreign to Harmonics; in no way suggesting anything one and the same, so as to be able to be grasped by definition, nor by ratio...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory, specifically defining the nature of a musical note (phthongos) and beginning a new chapter on the Pythagorean approach to harmonies.</meta>

# Chapter 5. BOOK I OF THE HARMONICS

...be able to be grasped <note>original: "comprehendi possint"</note> (contrary to what is characteristic of the sciences). These notes, however, are distinct and peculiar to this study; they are defined by the boundaries of the intervals and compared to one another according to the order of their differences. And indeed, such things may now be called **Notes** <note>original: "Phthongos"</note>, because a note is a sound maintaining one and the same pitch. Therefore, each note taken alone is without ratio <note>original: "ἄλογος" (alogos); meaning it has no mathematical relationship until compared to another</note>, being as it is indistinguishable from itself. For ratio belongs to things related to one another, and exists between at least two primary terms. But according to their comparison <note>original: "παραβολὴν" (parabolēn)</note> with one another, when they are of unequal pitch, they create a certain ratio, which is evaluated from the quotient of the difference. Among these, then, there appears both the **Concordant** <note>original: "ἐμμελὲς" (emmeleis); literally "in-tune" or suitable for melody</note> and the **Discordant**. Concordant notes are those which, when joined together, happen to be pleasant to the ear; Discordant are those which are not so. Furthermore, they say that **Consonants** <note>original: "Συμφώνους" (Symphonous)</note>—taking their name from the most beautiful of sounds or voices <note>original: "Phone"</note>—are those which produce a uniform perception in the ears; and **Dissonants** are those which do not.

---

### 5.
Concerning the hypotheses of the harmonies as they are received by the Pythagoreans.

### CHAPTER V.
**On the traditions of the Pythagoreans regarding the Hypotheses of Consonances.**

---

The sense of hearing perceives those Consonances which are called the **Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note> and the **Fifth** <note>original: "Dia-pente"</note>; the difference between which is called a **Tone**. Also the **Octave** <note>original: "Diapason"</note>, and further the Octave-plus-Fourth, the Octave-plus-Fifth, and the Double Octave. As for those intervals beyond these, let them be set aside for our present purpose. However, the reasoning of the Pythagoreans excludes only one of these—the Octave-plus-Fourth—from the number of consonances, following the particular hypotheses they have established for themselves.

---

<margin>
r. GIO. 1st. SD. lacks "de". s. S. lacks "men". t. GIO. "of equal tone," S. "equal-toned." u. A. "but a note" K. "diphthong," S. "that it is indifferent or we might call such things." x. S. "is." y. M. lacks "without ratio." z. C. "unequal tones." a. M. "to itself." b. "Difference" and "excess" <note>original: "ὑπεροχὴ & διαφορὰ"</note> are here generally taken for the same thing: they signify for the most part (though not always) not that difference which we seek by Subtraction, but that by Division; that is, not by *how much*, but in *what way* they differ when compared; (therefore I prefer to render this by "Quotient" rather than "Quantity"); for example, how many times one is of the other (double, triple, one-and-a-half, etc.), which thus indicates the Ratio of the things compared to each other. These terms are used indifferently for either extreme; for by however many times one sound is Sharper, by that many times the other is Deeper; indeed, by however many times this one is Greater, that one is Smaller. (I advise this lest the reader be surprised to encounter a "difference" of a smaller thing; as in, "the difference of the Fourth in relation to the Octave.") The same is indicated by the word "comparison" <note>original: "παραβολὴ"</note> which occurs almost constantly for collation or comparison; although in a looser sense it is said of any comparison, it is usually used technically among Mathematicians for Division, or what is like it; and a magnitude is said to be "applied" <note>applied/compared: "παραβάλλεσθαι"</note> to another magnitude, as a number is divided by a number: so that "comparison" and "division" are almost the same thing. I thought this should be noted for the better understanding of those places where these words occur. c. "Concinnum" and "inconcinnum" <note>Concordant/Discordant</note>: I translate these as "suitable for song" and "unsuitable": not because I think those words are derived from "singing," but because more suitable words do not occur to me; and I find these very words used in this sense by others. d. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean musical theory, specifically how the leaders of that school mapped mathematical ratios to musical intervals. It follows the previous page's introduction to the relationship between numbers and sounds.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 5.

The leaders of this sect <note>The Pythagoreans</note> established these principles, relying on the following considerations. Since they made the very foundation of their method this: to compare equal numbers to sounds of the same pitch <note>unisons</note>, and unequal numbers to sounds of unequal pitch; they proceed further as follows. Just as among sounds of unequal pitch, there are two primary species compared to one another: the Consonant and the Dissonant; and the Consonant is the more beautiful. Similarly, among unequal numbers, there are two primary differences of ratios: one which is called *Superpartient* <note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, e.g., 5:3</note>, which is as number to number; the other is the *Superparticular* <note>A ratio of the form (n+1):n, like 3:2</note> and the *Multiple* <note>A ratio of the form nx:x, like 2:1</note>. This latter group is better than the former because of the simplicity of the comparison: (Since therein, in the Superparticulars, the excess is a single part; and in the Multiples, the smaller number itself is a single or aliquot part of the larger.) Therefore, fitting the Superparticular and Multiple ratios to the consonances, they adapt the *Dia-pason* <note>The Octave</note> consonances to the Double ratio <note>2:1</note>; and the *Dia-pente* <note>The Fifth</note> to the sesquialter <note>3:2</note>; and the *Dia-tessaron* <note>The Fourth</note> to the sesquitertial <note>4:3</note>; (namely, insofar as they treat the matter in a more logical or rational way.) Since the *Dia-pason* is the most beautiful of Consonances, and the Double is the best of ratios: the former (*consonance*) as being closest to the same pitch; the latter (*ratio*) as being the only one which makes the excess equal to that which is exceeded <note>In 2:1, the difference is 1, which equals the smaller number</note>. Likewise, because the *Dia-pason* is composed of the two immediately following and primary consonances, the *Dia-pente* and *Dia-tessaron*; and the Double ratio is composed of the two immediately following and primary superparticulars, namely the sesquialter and the sesquitertial; and here, the sesquialter ratio is greater than the sesqui-

# Chapter 5. HARMONICS BOOK I.

->**Diagrams of Musical Ratios**<-

| Left Diagram | Right Diagram |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Octave & Fifth** (Dia-pason & dia-pente) <br> Ratio 3/1 = 2/1 x 3/2 | **Double Octave** (Dis-dia-pason) <br> Ratio 4/1 = 2/1 x 2/1 |
| 1 ——— 2 ——— 3 <br> α ——— β ——— γ | 1 ——— 2 ——— 4 <br> α ——— β ——— δ |
| **Triple** (1 to 3) <br> **Double** (1 to 2) <br> **Sesquialter** (2 to 3) | **Quadruple** (1 to 4) <br> **Double** (1 to 2) <br> **Double** (2 to 4) |
| Octave 2/1; Fifth 3/2 | Octave 2/1; Octave 2/1 |

The ratio **n** of **eight** to **three** <note>original: "η πρὸς γ"</note> is neither **o** **superparticular** <term>Superparticular: A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, e.g., 3:2 or 4:3</term> nor **p** **multiple** <term>Multiple: A ratio where the larger number is a clean multiple of the smaller, e.g., 2:1 or 3:1</term>. In a more linear fashion, proceeding forward, they arrive at the same conclusion in this way:

->**Diagram: Octave & Fourth**<-
**Ratio 8/3 = 2/1 x 4/3**
Numbers: 3 (γ), 6 (ζ), 8 (η)
Intervals: 3 to 8 (8:3 ratio); 3 to 6 (**Double**); 6 to 8 (**Sesquitertian** <note>4:3 ratio</note>)
Labels: Octave 2/1 and Fourth 4/3 <note>The OCR text incorrectly says "dia-pente" (Fifth) here, but the math 6:8 describes a Fourth.</note>

For let there be, **r** they say, a Fifth <note>Dia-pente</note> interval **AB**, and following this another Fifth **BC**; so that **AC** is a Double Fifth. And since the Double Fifth is a dissonant interval, therefore **t** **AC** is not in a multiple ratio; thus neither is **AB** a multiple (*since a multiple taken twice will also be a multiple*). But it is consonant: therefore the Fifth is **u** Superparticular. In the same **x** way, they show that the Fourth <note>Dia-tessaron</note> **y** is also superparticular, but smaller **z** than the **a** Fifth.

->**Diagrams of Proportional Segments**<-
(Left: Vertical ratios 9, 12, 16. Right: Vertical alignments for points A, B, C with ratios 8:4, 7:8, 15:16)

Furthermore, **b** they say, let **AB** be an Octave <note>Dia-pason</note>, and following this another Octave **BC**; so that **d** **AC** becomes a Double Octave <note>Dis-dia-pason</note>. **e** Since, then, the Double Octave is a consonant interval, **g** **AC** must therefore be either superparticular or multiple. But it is not superparticular; **h** for **i** a **mean proportional** <note>original: "ὃ ἀνάλογον" - a middle value that links the two ratios</note> fell in the middle <note>Superparticular ratios cannot be divided into two equal rational ratios</note>. Therefore **AC** is a multiple. Thus **AB** is also a multiple. Therefore the Octave interval is a multiple.

It is obvious from these things that the Octave is a **Double** ratio <note>2:1</note>; and from the others, that the Fifth is **Sesquialter** <note>3:2</note> and the Fourth is **Sesquitertian** <note>4:3</note>. Since **k** among the multiples, **l** **only** the Double ratio is composed of the two largest superparticulars <note>3/2 x 4/3 = 2/1</note>; so that ratios composed of any two other superparticulars **m** will be smaller than the Double; yet no multiple ratio is smaller than the Double. Likewise, following these things, it is shown that the ratio of a **Tone** **n** is **Sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note>; and they show that a half-tone is...

# CHAPTER VI.

# 6.

->**That the Pythagoreans reasoned incorrectly**<-
->**concerning the Consonances.**<-

->**Ὅτι μὴ δεόντως ὑπολογήσ’ οἱ Πυθα-**<-
->**γόρειοι, τὰ περὶ ª τὰς συμφωνίας.**<-

**S**uch, then, being the hypothesis of the Pythagoreans regarding the consonances: the <term>Octave</term><note>original: Dia-pason (8th)</note> and the <term>Fourth</term><note>original: dia-tessaron (4th)</note>, being an entirely manifest consonance, proves the error of the reasoning they applied to it. For (universally) the Octave consonance—because the sounds that produce it are so similar in power that they are like a single sound—when added to any of the remaining intervals, preserves the form of that interval undisturbed. (Just as, for example, the number ten relates to other numbers smaller than itself.) Now indeed, if one of those intervals is taken in the same direction from both outer limits of the Octave (say, either lower than both, or higher than both): as it relates to the nearer limit, so it will be judged to relate to the more remote; since the latter has the same power as the former. Moreover, the <term>Fifth</term><note>original: Dia-pente (5th)</note> and the Fourth are sung as consonances, both by themselves, in the position they hold relative to the nearer sound of the Octave, and also the Fourth-plus-Octave, and likewise the Fifth-

**Τ**οιαύτης δὴ τυγχάνουσης... <note>The Greek text mirrors the Latin translation provided above, discussing the relationship between the Diapason (Octave), Diapente (Fifth), and Diatessaron (Fourth).</note>

<image-desc>Two diagrams illustrating musical ratios. Left diagram (Latin): Brackets connecting numbers 3, 4, and 8. Right diagram (Greek): Brackets connecting numbers 1, 2, and 3. The interval between 1 and 2 is "Octave" (Diapason), and between 2 and 3 is "Fifth" (Diapente). The overall bracket is labeled "Octave plus Fifth."</image-desc>

plus-Octave, in the position they hold relative to the more remote sound. Therefore, they ought to produce the same perception to the ears: the Fourth-plus-Octave as the Fourth alone, and the Fifth-plus-Octave as the Fifth alone. And from this it follows entirely that, because the Fifth is consonant, the Octave-plus-Fifth is also consonant; and because the Fourth is conso-

<summary>Ptolemy critiques the Pythagorean system for failing to recognize the Octave-plus-Fourth as a consonance, arguing that because the Octave acts as a mathematical identity, any interval added to it should retain its character.</summary>

# Chapter 6. HARMONICS BOOK I.

-> [It is necessary] that the octave-plus-fourth <note>original: *dia-pason & dia-tessaron*</note> also be consonant; and that it holds the same relationship in the perception of the octave-plus-fifth <note>original: *dia-pente & dia-pason*</note> compared to the octave-plus-fourth, as the fifth alone holds to the fourth alone, following what is grasped from clear experience. However, a certain difficulty <note>original: *aporia*</note> falls upon them: why they attribute consonances only to these *Superparticular and Multiple* ratios <note>The Pythagoreans believed only ratios like 2:1 (multiple) or 3:2 (superparticular) could be harmonious</note> (namely, doubles, triples, quadruples, sesquialters, and sesquitertians) and not to others—for example, to *sesquiquartans* <note>5:4 ratio</note> or *quintuples* <note>5:1 ratio</note>—even though the form of the latter is the same as the former. And furthermore, why they make a selection of consonances in the specific manner they choose. From both [perspectives], certainly, <-

---

<image-desc>A diagram showing the ratios of the primary musical intervals.
Top row: Numbers 4, 3, 2, 1.
The first bracket connects 4 and 3: "Fourth" <note>Greek: *dia tessaron*</note> with the ratio 4:3.
The second bracket connects 3 and 2: "Fifth" <note>Greek: *dia pente*</note> with the ratio 3:2.
The third bracket connects 2 and 1: "Octave" <note>Greek: *dia pason*</note> with the ratio 2:1.</image-desc>

---

taking the first numbers (*that is, the smallest*) which produce those ratios, they subtract a unit from each for the sake of the "likeness" <note>original: *homoiotētos*</note> of the pair, and substituting the remaining numbers as the "unlikeness"; whichever of these results in a smaller sum, they claim to be more consonant. This is entirely ridiculous. For that ratio is not unique to the first numbers that produce it, but belongs to all numbers that hold the same relationship to one another. Thus, if the same were done with those, the sums of the "unlikenesses" would be constituted sometimes as the smallest and sometimes as the largest for the very same ratios. But (as it would seem a more appropriate method for this undertaking),

2. Numbers 2 and 4: "Octave" <note>ratio 2:4</note>.
3. Numbers 3 and 6: "Octave" <note>ratio 3:6</note>.</image-desc>

---

if we were to substitute the same number for the smaller terms of every ratio—for example, the number six <note>original: *senarius*</note>—and subtracting its equals from the larger terms, we compared the remainders (instead of the "likeness") as containing the "unlikenesses": according to the ratio...

<note>Critical Apparatus / Footnotes</note>

<margin>The translator notes that the Greek term *homoiotēs* (likeness) likely refers to the two units subtracted from the primary ratio (e.g., in a 4:3 ratio, subtracting 1 from each leaves 3 and 2). The sum of these remainders was used by some theorists to rank how "sweet" a sound was. Ptolemy finds this mathematical juggling arbitrary.</margin>

<summary>Ptolemy critiques the Pythagorean method of using arbitrary mathematical subtractions to rank musical consonances, arguing that harmony should be based on the ratio itself rather than specific sets of numbers.</summary>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 6.

by the **Double** ratio <note>original: "ratione Dupla"; the 2:1 ratio of the Octave</note>, there will likewise be six remaining; for the **One-and-a-half** <note>original: "Sesquialtera"; the 3:2 ratio of the Fifth</note>, three; for the **One-and-a-third** <note>original: "Sesquitertia"; the 4:3 ratio of the Fourth</note>, two. And so, for the more consonant intervals, the "unpaired" remainders are larger.

<note>original Greek: τὰ μὲν τ' διπλάσιον... The Greek text mirrors the Latin above, arguing that the mathematical method used by some theorists leads to the paradox where more harmonious sounds appear to have "more" leftover parts than less harmonious ones.</note>

---

<image-desc>A diagram of musical ratios.
- A bracket labeled 5 and 6 connects 12 and 6: **Octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν / Dia-pason"</note> 2/1 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 3 connects 9 and 6: **Fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε / Dia-pente"</note> 3/2 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 2 connects 8 and 6: **Fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσάρων / Dia-tessaron"</note> 4/3 ratio.
</image-desc>

But indeed, following their own method, it will be demonstrated that after the interval of the **Octave**, the **Octave-plus-Fifth** <note>original: "Dia-pason & dia-pente"; a 12th or 3:1 ratio</note> is more consonant than all remaining consonances: because here two remainders are unpaired; whereas in all others there are more;

<note>original Greek: δέ, ἡ κατὰ τ' αὐτῶν ἔφοδον... Ptolemy argues that if we judge "purity" by the number of remaining units in a ratio, the Octave-plus-Fifth (3:1) would wrongly be considered more consonant than the simple Fifth (3:2).</note>

---

<image-desc>A second diagram of ratios.
- A bracket labeled 5 connects 4 and 3: **Fourth** <note>original: "διὰ τεσσ. / Dia-tessaron"</note> 4/3 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 3 connects 3 and 2: **Fifth** <note>original: "διὰ πέντε / Dia-pente"</note> 3/2 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 1 connects 2 and 1: **Octave** <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν / Dia-pason"</note> 2/1 ratio.
</image-desc>

---

<image-desc>A third diagram of compound intervals.
- A bracket labeled 9 connects 8 and 3: **Octave-plus-Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-pas. & dia-tess."</note> 8/3 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 2 connects 3 and 1: **Octave-plus-Fifth** <note>original: "Dia-pas. & dia-pente"</note> 3/1 ratio.
- A bracket labeled 3 connects 4 and 1: **Double Octave** <note>original: "δὶς διὰ πασῶν / Dif-dia-pason"</note> 4/1 ratio.
</image-desc>

such as three, both in the **Fifth** and in the **Double Octave**. Yet either of these is most evidently more consonant than the **Octave-plus-Fifth**. And indeed, concerning the **Fifth**, it is entirely clear; since the **Fifth** is a simpler and less composite interval than the **Octave-plus-Fifth**, and is, as it were, of a purer consonance. The **Double Octave**, moreover, stands in relation to the **Octave-plus-Fifth** (that is, the **Quadruple** ratio to the **Triple**) just as the **Octave** alone stands to the **Fifth** alone (that is, as the **Double** ratio to the **One-and-a-half**): For if, from the same number, one takes both the Triple and the Quadruple, and then the One-and-a-half and the Double: they will produce a **One-and-a-third** <note>original: "sesquialteram" in Latin text, but "ἐπίτριτον" (4/3) in Greek and logic</note> ratio, both when the Quadruple is compared to the Triple, and the Double to the One-and-a-half. Therefore, by as much as the **Octave** is more consonant than the **Fifth**, by so much will the **Double Octave** be more consonant than the **Octave-plus-Fifth**.

<note>original Greek: δὶς διὰ πασῶν... Ptolemy uses mathematical proportions to show that the Double Octave (4:1) relates to the Octave-plus-Fifth (3:1) in the same 4:3 ratio that the Octave (2:1) relates to the Fifth (3:2).</note>

---

-> <term>One-and-a-third</term> <note>original: "ἐπίτριτος"</note> $\frac{4}{3}$ <-
-> <term>One-and-a-half</term> <note>original: "ἡμιόλιος"</note> $\frac{3}{2}$ <-

<image-desc>A mathematical table showing the relationship between ratios.
It demonstrates that 8:6 (from a base of 2) is the same 4:3 ratio as 4:3.
Labels: **Quadruple** <note>4/1</note>, **Triple** <note>3/1</note>, **Double** <note>2/1</note>, **One-and-a-half** <note>3/2</note>.
</image-desc>

*e* I replace "in which" with "these things" as found in all Codices. *f* GI "of them". *g* SV lacks "the Octave". *t* Namely, for the number 2, both the quadruple to the triple (8 to 6) and the double to the one-and-a-half (4 to 3) are in the **One-and-a-third** ratio. In these diagrams, we use Brackets instead of the complicated Semicircles used by others; since they show the same thing and are more convenient for the typesetters.

<summary>Ptolemy critiques musical theorists who rank consonances by mathematical remainders, arguing that their logic incorrectly suggests the Octave-plus-Fifth is more harmonious than the simple Fifth or the Double Octave. He uses proportional ratios (4:3) to prove the Double Octave's superior consonance.</summary>

# Ch. 7. HARMONICS BOOK I. 15

## 7.

## How the ratios of concordant intervals might be more soundly defined.

## CHAPTER VII.

## In what manner the ratios of Consonances are more rightly defined.

It would therefore be necessary <note>original: "Δεῖ οὖν ἂν εἴη"</note> not to attribute such errors to the power of reason itself, but to those who have not applied it as they ought. One should instead attempt to grasp the truth in a more natural way <note>original: "Φυσικώτερον" - referring to the physical or natural properties of sound</note>, having first divided distinct sounds of different pitch into three species. First in rank, for the sake of excellence, is the class of **unisons** <note>original: "ὁμοφώνων" - literally 'same-sounding'</note>; second, that of **consonances** <note>original: "συμφώνων" - 'sounding together'</note>; and third, that of **melodic intervals** <note>original: "ἐμμελῶν" - 'in-tune' or suitable for melody</note>. For the *octave* <note>term: "διὰ πασῶν" (dia pason) - the interval of an eighth</note> and the *double octave* clearly differ from the other consonances, just as those consonances differ from the melodic intervals; thus it would be more appropriate to call these "unisons." Let us define unisons as those which, by a coincidence of sound, produce the perception of a single note to the ears, such as the octave and those composed from it. Consonances are those which most closely approach the unisons, such as the *fifth* <note>term: "διὰ πέντε" (dia pente) - the interval of a fifth</note> and the *fourth* <note>term: "διὰ τεσσάρων" (dia tessaron) - the interval of a fourth</note>, and those composed of these and the unisons. Melodic intervals are those which most closely approach the consonances, such as the whole-tones and others of that kind. In this way, it will be preserved how unisons are composed of consonances, and consonances of melodic intervals.

These things having been defined, we must proceed to the ratio consistent with them, taking the same starting principle as the **Pythagoreans** <note>The followers of Pythagoras who believed the universe was governed by numbers</note>: namely, that we assign equal numbers to sounds of the same pitch, and unequal numbers to sounds of different pitch, as this is self-evident. Since it follows from this principle that the differences of sounds of different pitch should be measured by their proximity to equality, it is immediately clear that the **double ratio** (2:1) is closest to equality, having an excess equal to the value that is exceeded. Among the unisons, the octave is the most singular and beautiful; therefore, the double ratio fits it. To the double octave, clearly, the "twice-double" or **quadruple ratio** (4:1) applies, and so on for any others measured by the octave and the double ratio. Furthermore, after the double ratios, those that divide these most nearly in two would be closer to equality: namely, the **sesquialter** (3:2) <note>The ratio for the musical fifth</note> and the **sesquitertia** (4:3) <note>The ratio for the musical fourth</note>; for "nearly in two" is close to being divided into two equal parts. After the unisons, the primary consonances...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of musical intervals from Chapter 7 of Ptolemy's Harmonics, specifically focusing on the classification of ratios into Unisons, Consonants, and Concinnous (melodic) intervals.</meta>

# 16 CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 7.

The primary <note>Latin: *primarii*</note> ones are those which divide the **Octave** <note>original: *Dia-pason*</note> into two nearly equal parts; namely the **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> and the **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note>. Therefore, the Fifth must likewise be placed in the **Sesquialter** ratio <note>The ratio 3:2</note>; and the Fourth in the **Sesquitertial** ratio <note>The ratio 4:3</note>. Truly, the Secondary Consonants are those which are formed from the composition of either of the primary ones with the first of the Unisons; namely the **Octave-plus-Fifth**, in the ratio composed of the double and the sesquialter, which is **Triple** <note>3:1</note>; and the **Octave-plus-Fourth**, in the ratio composed of the double and the sesquitertial, which is the ratio of **Eight to Three** <note>8:3</note>. (For nothing shall disturb us regarding the fact that this is neither a superparticular nor a multiple ratio; since we have previously supposed no such restriction.) After the sesquitertial ratio, those which most nearly approach equality are those which compose it, having commensurable excesses; namely, the superparticulars smaller than it. After the Consonants, the next in rank are the **Concinnous** <note>original: *Concinni* / *emmeleis*; these are "melodic" intervals that are not fully consonant but fit well in a scale</note>; such as the **Tone**, and whatever others compose the smallest of the consonances. Thus, to these must be assigned the superparticular ratios that follow after the sesquitertial. And, among these, those which make divisions nearly in half are (for the same reason) more concinnous; and those whose differences contain larger unit parts (or aliquots) of the terms exceeded: as they approach more closely to equality: such as (most of all) the **half-part** <note>1/2</note>: and, after this, the **third-part** <note>1/3</note>; and the others in their proper order. To speak summarily: of these (Sounds,) the **Unisons** are the first multiple and those measured by it; the **Consonants** are the first two superparticulars and those composed of these with the Unisons; and finally the **Concinnous** are the superparticulars after the sesquitertial. Therefore, what the proper ratio of each Unison and Consonant is has already been stated. Of the Concinnous, it is demonstrated at the same time that the ratio of the **Tone** is **Sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note>; (on account of the excess of the first two superparticular ratios, and of the two...

<column-break/>

...sounds, those which divide the **Octave** <note>original: *dia pason*</note> nearly in two; that is, the **Fifth** <note>original: *dia pente*</note> and the **Fourth** <note>original: *dia tessaron*</note>. So that the Fifth is placed according to the **sesquialter** <note>original: *hemiolios*</note> ratio; and the Fourth according to the **sesquitertial** <note>original: *epitritos*</note>. Secondary are those according to the composition of each of the first with the first of the **unisons** <note>original: *homophonoi*</note>; the Octave-plus-Fifth, according to the ratio composed of the double and sesquialter, the **triple**; and the Octave-plus-Fourth, according to the ratio composed of the double and sesquitertial, that of **eight to three**. For now nothing strange—it being neither superparticular nor multiple—will discourage us; for we have presupposed no such thing. Next, after the sesquitertial ratio, are those which become closer to equality, which compose it in commensurable excesses; that is, the superparticulars smaller than it. After the **consonants** <note>original: *symphonoi*</note>, following in virtue, are the **melodic** <note>original: *emmeleis*</note>; such as the **tone**, and as many as compose the smallest of the consonances. So that to these are applied the superparticular ratios under the sesquitertial. And among these, those making the divisions nearly in two would be more melodic, for the same reason; and those whose differences contain larger simple parts of the things exceeded; because these are closer to equality, just as the half is more than all, then the third; and each of the following. To speak briefly: from these, the **unisons** would be the first multiple and those measured by it; the **consonants**, the first two of the superparticulars, and those composed of them and the unisons; the **melodic**, those after the sesquitertial of the superparticulars. Of the unisons and the consonants, the specific ratio of each has been told; of the melodic, the **tonal** ratio, being **sesquioctave** <note>original: *epiogdoos*</note>, is demonstrated from this, through the excess of the first two superparticulars...

### 8.
### In what manner the ratios of the concords shall be shown indubitably by means of the monochord rule.

### C A P. VIII.
### In what way the Ratios of Consonances are shown indubitably, through the monochord Rule <note>The "Canon" or monochord was a single-stringed instrument used to measure mathematical intervals in music.</note>.

Let us, therefore, omit the attempt to demonstrate our proposition by means of flutes <note>original: "αὐλῶν", often translated as oboe-like pipes</note> or pan-pipes <note>original: "συρίγγων"</note>, or by weights hung from strings; for such demonstrations cannot reach the highest level of accuracy, but rather provide grounds for detraction to those who attempt them. For in the case of flutes and pipes, besides the fact that their irregularity is difficult to correct, even the endpoints to which the lengths must be compared are established in a sort of indefinite deception; and because, in general, most wind instruments have a certain disorder added to them from the force of the breath. 

And in the case of weights hung from strings, since the strings themselves are not preserved entirely identical to one another—when it is a difficult task to find even a single string that is perfectly uniform in itself—it will no longer be possible to apply the ratios of the weights to the sounds produced by them. This is because thicker or thinner strings, under the same tension, produce sharper sounds. Even before this, even if one assumes everything else is possible, and that the length of the strings is equal, a greater weight, by its greater tension, will increase the stretching of the string attached to it and make it denser; so that because of this, a certain difference in the sounds occurs beyond the ratio of the weights. 

Similar things happen in the comparison of sounds made by striking; such as those we perceive through hammers or discs of unequal weight, or those from bowls, both empty and full. It is altogether laborious to maintain, in all these cases, an identicality in materials and shapes. But a string stretched upon the so-called rule <note>The "Canon"</note> will show us the ratios of the concords more accurately and more readily. However, it must not be given just any tension...

<meta>Catchword: but,</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 8.

...but, after an initial examination has been held, so that there might be no inequality in its construction: then also, with the endpoints placed in their due positions; so that the endpoints of the *Apopsalmata* <note>original: "Apopsalmatum (seu Finitorum)". These are the "plucked" or "sounding" lengths of the string.</note> in the Rules (by which the total length is determined) may have suitable and conspicuous starting points. Let it therefore be understood | but, first, after [considering] the reflection <note>original: "anaklasin". Likely referring to the bending or tensioning of the string.</note> regarding the irregularity that will exist in the construction; then also with the ends taking the proper position; so that the ends of the sounding lengths within them, by which the whole length is defined, may have their own clear starting points. Let there be understood:

->Harmonic Canon.<-

<image-desc>A technical diagram representing a "Canon Harmonicus" (Harmonic Canon). It features a horizontal base line ABCD (marked with Greek letters alpha, beta, gamma, delta) with various points F, H, K, L. Above this is a chord AEGD (alpha, epsilon, eta, delta). Two semicircular structures, referred to as "magades" (bridges), are shown supporting the chord at points E and G, with their centers at F and H respectively. Vertical dashed lines show the perpendicular relationship between the bridges and the base canon. Small movable bridges are also indicated by letters KK and LL (kappa, kappa and lambda, lambda).</image-desc>

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **ABCD**, The Straight Line of the Canon. | **$\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$**, the straight line of the canon. |
| **AEGD**, The String. | **$\alpha\epsilon\eta\delta$**, the string. |
| **AE**, **GD**, The Attachments. | **$\alpha\epsilon$**, **$\eta\delta$**, the attachments. |
| **EB**, **GC**; Perpendiculars of the Bridges. | **$\epsilon\beta$**, **$\eta\gamma$**, perpendiculars of the bridges. |
| **KK**, **LL**, Movable Bridges. | **$\kappa\kappa$**, **$\lambda\lambda$**, movable bridges. |
| **BKLC**, The Small Canon, or divided Rule. | **$\beta\kappa\lambda\gamma$**, the small canon. |

The Canon follows the straight line ABCD; and at its ends are the Bridges <note>original: "Magades sive Ponticuli". These are the supports that lift the string.</note>, which are entirely equal and similar; presenting spherical surfaces (as much as possible) placed under the strings: for instance BE, around the center F of said surface; and CG, similarly around the center H. And with points E and G taken at the midpoints of the curved surfaces; let the Bridges have such a position that the straight lines drawn from the midpoints E and G and the centers F and H, namely EBF and CGH, are perpendicular to the straight line ABCD. Now indeed if, from points A and D, we stretch a corresponding string, such as AEGD: this EG will be parallel to ABCD itself (on account of the equal height of the Bridges); and it will have, at points E and G, the beginnings of the Sounding Lengths or *Apopsalmata*. | The Canon along the $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$ straight line; and bridges at its ends, equal and similar in every way; making the surfaces under the string as spherical as possible; both $\beta\epsilon$, around the center $\zeta$ of the said surface; and $\gamma\eta$, similarly around the center $\theta$. And with the points $\epsilon\eta$ taken according to the bisections of the curved surfaces; let the bridges have such a position that those [lines] extended through the bisections $\epsilon\eta$ and the centers $\zeta$ and $\theta$, namely $\epsilon\beta\zeta$ and $\eta\gamma\theta$, are perpendicular to $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$. If therefore we stretch a symmetrical string from $\alpha$ and $\delta$, such as $\alpha\epsilon\eta\delta$; then $\epsilon\eta$ will be parallel to $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$, because the bridges have equal height; and it will receive, at points $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, the beginnings of the sounding lengths.

*z* K. is missing *a*. A C. <unclear>foundation-like</unclear>, Eastern MSS often confuse $\upsilon$ and $\beta$. *b* CV. there. *c* S. let him understand. C. I will understand. *d* S. lacks "to it". *e* S. —tes. *f* V. upon. *g* C. "with strings" (which I prefer, because of the single string here,) the rest "with the strings." *h* ABC. yet. *i* S. lacks $\zeta$. *k* AB. indeed. *l* ABCSV. lacks "the". *m* ABCV. $\epsilon\zeta\beta$ and $\eta\theta\gamma$, and so below. *n* AB. lacks "the". *o* S. and according to. *p* ABV. lacks "the". *q* GISOV. the. *r* O. lacks "will be". *s* AB. under the. Regarding the diagram set forth here; it must be noted that the Bridges placed on both sides (in all manuscripts) are designated by semicircles, standing upon the straight line $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$ (or parallel to it, beyond or on this side of the centers); as in Chap. 16. book 2. as if showing segments of a whole Sphere, of which these would be the sections through the axis. In place of these I substitute certain quadrants, suggesting segments of a Hemisphere, as being more suited to Ptolemy's purpose: both because there appears to be hardly any use for the portions within the summits $\epsilon\eta$; and because they would be inconvenient: For although I would wish the spheres to touch the straight line only at the single points $\epsilon\eta$; nevertheless the string $\epsilon\eta$, moved by striking, will touch some notable but uncertain part of that same spherical surface; whence the points of the sounding lengths $\epsilon\eta$, which terminate the length, would be rendered uncertain: but also $\epsilon\zeta$ and $\eta\theta$ themselves (perpendicular to the centers) will not be seen by this reasoning. I would rather have thought those semicircles were originally intended, not to designate semicircles in the $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$ plane, but semicircles standing transversely to the straight line $\alpha\delta$ at right angles; whose centers would likewise be the same $\zeta\theta$ points; and $\epsilon\eta$ the middle <term>summits</term> <note>original: "koruphomata"</note> (or curvatures) of the same; whence $\epsilon\beta\zeta$ and $\eta\gamma\theta$, (if the centers are below the $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$ line,) or $\epsilon\zeta\beta$ and $\eta\theta\gamma$ (if the centers are above the same line, with straight lines dropped from the ends of the semicircle or circular arc to the subject plane $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$, for the manuscripts vary in this designation,) would be perpendiculars through the centers. But, from book 2 and 3, it seems the Semicircle should rather have been retained; and, by "spherical," "Cylindrical" should be understood. It is likewise doubtful whether (as it sounded) the Small Canon <note>original: "Canonium"</note> is to be fashioned separately and applied to this string as the matter requires (which seems to be the case if the fixed bridges are continued within points $\beta\gamma$); or (which I would rather have thought) $\beta\gamma$ itself (placed under the string $\epsilon\eta$) is this Small Canon; which certainly, divided in the proper ratio, would show those marked points at which a movable small bridge <note>original: "magadion"</note> standing upright would make the required sections in the string. Therefore, I have adapted our diagram to this supposition (as the better one). In the following diagram, however, instead of the spherical surface of the bridge, I think the cylindrical surface of an oblong bridge (cut by a plane through the axis) should be understood, upon which (placed in a side-by-side position) all the strings $\epsilon\eta$ rest. Unless rather all those strings are to be considered as one and the same (which is in the preceding diagram) divided in one way and then another, successively. *t* S. the point. *u* S. sounding---. <meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals using string lengths, transitioning into a critique of the Aristoxenian school of music theory.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 9.

<note>Ptolemy is describing the mathematical ratios of a monochord—a single-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals.</note>
...of four such parts as the segment KG is of three; the sounds produced at each end of them will create the **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note> consonance, due to the sesquitertial ratio <note>The ratio 4:3</note>. If, however, the segment EK is taken as three such parts as KG is of two, the sounds at each end will create the **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> consonance, due to the sesquialter ratio <note>The ratio 3:2</note>. Likewise, if the whole length is divided so that EK consists of two parts and KG of one such part, it will be the **Octave** <note>original: *Unisonum Dia-pason*</note> concord, according to the double ratio <note>The ratio 2:1</note>. If it is set so that EK is eight parts and KG is three, it will be the **Octave-plus-Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-pason & dia-tessaron*</note> consonance, in the ratio of eight to three. If EK is three parts and KG is one, it will be the **Octave-plus-Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente & dia-pason*</note> consonance, in the triple ratio <note>The ratio 3:1</note>. Finally, if EK is four parts and KG is one, it will be the **Double Octave** <note>original: *Di-dia-pason*</note> concord, according to the quadruple ratio <note>The ratio 4:1</note>.

<note>The following is the original Greek text of the passage translated above.</note>
...from a distance of four parts, of which KG is three; the notes at each end will produce the *fourth* consonance; through the sesquitertial ratio. Of such parts, if EK is taken as three, of which KG is two; the notes at each end will produce the *fifth* consonance; through the sesquialter ratio. And again, if the whole length is divided so that EK becomes two segments, and KG one of the same; it will be the *octave* concord; through the double ratio. If, however, EK is composed of eight segments, and KG of three of them; the *octave and fourth* will be the consonance; according to the eight to three ratio. If, however, EK is of three segments, and KG one of the same; the *fifth and octave* will be the consonance; according to the triple ratio. If, finally, EK is composed of four segments, and KG one of the same; it will be the *double octave* concord; through the quadruple ratio.

---

### CHAPTER IX.
### 9.

*That the Aristoxenians wrongly measure consonances by intervals, rather than by the notes themselves.*

*Ὅτι οὐ δεόντως οἱ Ἀριστοξένειοι, τοῖς διαστήμασι καὶ οὐ τοῖς φθόγγοις, παραμετροῦσι τὰς συμφωνίας.*

**I**n the meantime, based on what has been said, the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed music was governed by strict mathematical ratios.</note> are not to be blamed regarding their discovery of the ratios of consonances, for they are correct; (rather, they are blamed for the reasoning by which they departed from their purpose.) But the Aristoxenians <note>Followers of Aristoxenus, who argued that the human ear, not math, should be the judge of music.</note> are to be blamed: because they neither agreed with these facts, manifest as they are, nor, having rejected them, did they seek out sounder ones—even though they took it upon themselves to hand down the Theory of Music. For they themselves must admit that these experiences occur in the hearing because of the relationship that sounds have to one another. And likewise, that the perceptions of these same strings have definite and clearly identical differences. Yet, how the two notes that constitute each species of consonance relate to one another, they neither say nor investigate. Instead, (as if the notes themselves were not **real** <note>original: *reales* / *asomaton* (incorporeal)</note>, but the spaces lying between them were **real** <note>original: *realia* / *somaton* (bodies)</note>), they only compare the distances <note>intervals</note> of those species among themselves, so that they might at least appear to be doing something with number and proportion. This, however, is plainly the opposite of the truth.

---

<note>The following are the translator's critical notes on the Greek and Latin variants.</note>
z V. *which*. a AB. *at each*. V. *at each of them*. b C. *so*. c S. --- d <gloss>Note on the word "of".</gloss> e CGISOV. *lacks "as".* f AB. g. g C. *as if* twice. h AB. *lacks "me"*. CHAPTER 9. a OV. --- b V. *witness*. c O. *Pythagoreans*. d DM. *spoken*. e CSV. *fell away*. f S. *brought to*. g GIO. h *use*. i *of them*. b AB. *the*. S. *missing*. i AB. *two notes toward each other*. k S. *otherwise; not*. l CS. *of shadows*. <gloss>I render *soma* and *asomaton* as "Real" and "Non-real" (rather than "Body" and "Incorporeal"), for that is surely what Ptolemy intends here. (For the debate here is not whether it is a physical Body or an Accident, but whether it has a positive magnitude that ought to be measured by proportion: and so hereafter.) In this sense, it is no wonder if the name "Body" is perhaps even attributed to Spirits, insofar as it denotes Reality and is distinguished from the Non-real.</gloss> m O. *lacks "p"*.

# Chapter 9. HARMONICS BOOK I.
->21<-

<margin>n</margin> For first, they do not define <margin>o</margin> each of the species in this way, according to its own nature, for what it is. It is just as if, when someone asks **what is a tone**, we should say that it is the **difference between two sounds, containing a sesquioctave ratio** <note>original: "ἐπέχουσα ἐπόγδοον λόγον" / "rationem sesquioctavam". A sesquioctave ratio is 9:8, the mathematical definition of a whole tone.</note>. But <margin>p</margin> they immediately make reference <margin>q</margin> to something else among those things yet to be defined. It is just as when <margin>r</margin> they speak of the **Fourth** <term>Dia-tessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> and the **Fifth** <term>Dia-pente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> by their excess <note>The "excess" or difference between a Fifth and a Fourth is a Tone.</note>. And yet the sense of hearing, if it wishes to tune a tone, does not first require the **Fourth** or any of the others; rather, it is capable of establishing each of such differences by itself. And if we seek the magnitude of the said excess, they do not even show this <margin>s</margin> apart from another; but they might only <margin>u</margin> say, perhaps, that there are two of these <margin>x</margin> in what the **Fourth** <margin>y</margin> has as five; and of these again five <margin>z</margin> such as <margin>α</margin> the **Octave** <term>Dia-pason: the interval of a perfect octave</term> has as twelve; <margin>β</margin> and similarly for the rest; until they finally come back around to saying, <margin>γ</margin> for example, that the tonic distance is two. Furthermore, <margin>δ</margin> not even in this way

The interval will certainly be smaller...

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 10.

->**Mathematical Diagram of Harmonic Ratios**<-
<note>The diagram illustrates the "Pythagorean" division of the octave. The numbers represent string lengths or frequencies. 6 to 12 is an octave (2:1). 6 to 9 and 8 to 12 are fifths (3:2). 6 to 8 and 9 to 12 are fourths (4:3). The central gap between 8 and 9 represents a whole tone (9:8).</note>

[The diagram shows:]
**Higher Pitch** (A=6) <----------------> **Lower Pitch** (B=12)
Intervals:
A to B: **Octave** <note>original: *Dia-pason*</note> (Ratio 2:1)
A to C: **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> (Ratio 3:2)
D to B: **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> (Ratio 3:2)
A to D: **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note> (Ratio 4:3)
D to C: **Whole Tone** <note>original: *Tonus*</note> (Ratio 9:8)
C to B: **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note> (Ratio 4:3)

---

the interval AC [is smaller] than BD (because it falls within the higher tensions); but the excess BC (by which the **Octave** AB exceeds the **Fifth** AC) is greater than AD (by which the same **Octave** exceeds the **Fifth** BD). Indeed, it seems among the most absurd of things to claim that a ratio exists for the differences [between sounds] which cannot be shown through the very magnitudes that produce those differences; and yet to claim no ratio for the magnitudes themselves, from which those differences could be immediately derived. For if they should say that those comparisons do not belong to the differences in sounds: they cannot say to what others they might belong. For a bare distance, or mere quantity, is not a **Consonance** <term>Consonum: a harmonious sounding together</term> or a **Melodic Interval** <term>Concinnum: suitable for melody</term>; nor is it even a real thing. Such a thing is said of a solitary magnitude: but a **Comparison** <note>original: *Comparatio* / *parabole*</note> exists only between two things at minimum, and those unequal; namely, the sounds which produce the consonance and melody themselves. Therefore, they cannot say that these comparisons according to **Quantity** <note>original: *Quotum* / *poson*</note> belong to anything other than the sounds and their differences. Neither of these are made known by their [the Aristoxenians'] methods; yet they are, by nature, defined together and share a common ratio; according to which (being one and the same) it is shown how sounds relate to one another, and how they differ among themselves.

<margin>The Greek text below mirrors the Latin argument above, providing the original phrasing of Ptolemy's critique regarding the "absurdity" of the opposing school's logic.</margin>

->### CHAPTER X.<-

->**That they do not correctly define the *Fourth* Consonance as consisting of two and a half tones.**<-

->**Ὅτι οὐχ ὑγιῶς τὴν διὰ τεσσάρων συμφωνίαν, δύο καὶ ἡμίσεος ὑποτίθενται τόνων.**<-

**I**ndeed, they also err in the measurement of the smallest and first Consonance (called the **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note>); inasmuch as they compose it from two tones and a half. (From which it is gathered that the **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> consists of three tones and a half; the **Octave** <note>original: *Dia-pason*</note> of six tones; and each of the remaining intervals follows from these.) For **Ratio** <note>original: *Ratio* / *logos*</note> (which, in these most minute differences, is much more worthy of trust than the senses) demonstrates entirely that the matter is otherwise, as will become manifest. They certainly attempt to demonstrate their proposition in this manner: Let there be (they say) two sounds which, by the judgment of the ears, form a **Fourth** consonance...

And from A, let there be taken, toward the sharp <note>high pitch</note>, the **Ditonum** <note>an interval of two whole tones, roughly a major third</note> AC; and from B likewise, toward the flat <note>low pitch</note>, let the **Ditonum** BD be taken (<margin>all these by the judgment of the ears</margin>). <image-desc>A mathematical diagram of musical intervals. A top horizontal bracket spanning the width is labeled "Fourth (Dia-tessaron)" with the value "512" above it. Below this are five vertical lines corresponding to numbers in Arabic and Greek numerals: 2187, 2048, 1944, 1728, and 1536. to 1944. (For the number 2187 exceeds the number 2048 by more than a fifteenth part, but less than a fourteenth: while the number 2048 exceeds 1944 by more than a nineteenth part, but less than an eighteenth.) Therefore, that which is perceived within the **Fourth** along with the **Ditone** <note>Two whole tones</note> is the smaller portion of the third tone. From this it is gathered that the magnitude of the **Limma** is less than a **Semitone**: (and the whole **Fourth** is less than two and a half tones.) And this ratio of the **Limma**, 2048 to 1944, is the same as 256 to 243.

Now, one must not think that this contradiction happens through a fault of Reason or of Sensation, but rather from hypotheses established in different ways; and by the more recent [theorists] who, contrary to the judgment of both, misuse their evidence. For Sensation all but cries out; clearly and undoubtedly recognizing the **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> Consonance when, in the demonstration already set forth by the **Monochord** <note>A single-stringed instrument used for measuring musical intervals</note>, it is taken according to the sesquialter ratio <note>3:2</note>: and the **Fourth** when taken according to the sesquitertial <note>4:3</note>. But those men do not adhere to these confessions of the sense: (from which it follows entirely, both that the excess of the said consonances, which is one Tone, is in the Sesquioctave ratio; and that the **Fourth** consonance is less than two and a half tones.) Yet, while they utterly distrust sensation in these matters (the larger differences, I mean) which it is fit to judge; they meanwhile trust it in those smaller differences which it is not sufficient to judge: indeed, they attach judgments that are plainly contrary to those first and most important ones. Furthermore, we shall see hereafter the foolishness of their demonstration—

---

# Chapter 10. HARMONICS BOOK I. 25

Let us calculate the magnitude of the variation of the <term>limma</term> <note>The "remainder" or Pythagorean semitone (256:243)</note> compared to the <term>semitone</term> <note>The "half-tone" (hemitonium), which the author argues is not a perfect mathematical half of a whole tone</note>. For since neither the <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>The ratio 9:8, representing a whole tone</note> <margin>m</margin> nor any other <term>superparticular</term> <note>A ratio of the form (n+1):n</note> <margin>n</margin> ratio can be divided into two equal ratios <insert>expressible in whole numbers</insert>; <margin>o</margin> yet two nearly equal ratios <margin>p</margin> produce the sesquioctave, namely <margin>q</margin> the 17th <note>18:17</note> and the 18th <note>17:16</note>; the semitone would therefore be somewhere in the middle ratio between these; that is, greater than the 17th but smaller than the 16th. Now, of the number 243, the number 15 is a part greater than a 17th, but smaller than a 16th. <margin>s</margin> Thus, when these are added together, <margin>t</margin> 243 and 15, the semitone results in a ratio of approximately <margin>u</margin> 258 to 243. <margin>x</margin> But it was also demonstrated that the ratio of the limma is <margin>y</margin> 256 to 243. Therefore, the semitone will differ from the limma by the ratio of 258 to 256; which is <margin>z</margin> the 128th <margin>a</margin> <note>original: "ἑπτακοσιογδοηκοστοῦ" (780th) in Greek text, but the Latin and math clarify it as 128th (sesquicentesimavicesimaoctava)</note>.

---

->Semitone.<-
->Hemitonium.<-

<image-desc>A musical diagram showing the subdivision of a Hemitonium. Three values are marked on a horizontal line: 258, 256, and 243. Brackets beneath the line define the intervals: the span between 258 and 256 is labeled "Difference," while the span between 256 and 243 is labeled "Limma." The entire span from 258 to 243 represents the Semitone.</image-desc>

But this slight variation, they themselves would not say is possible to judge by the ears. If, therefore, it is possible <margin>a</margin> for the sense of hearing to be deceived by so much in a single instance; much more might it be deceived in the <margin>b</margin> accumulation of many successive perceptions; such as occurs in the demonstration they have set forth. For when the <term>fourth</term> <note>Diatessaron (4:3)</note> <margin>d</margin> is taken three times, and the <margin>f</margin> <margin>g</margin> <term>ditone</term> <note>A major third (81:64)</note> twice, in <margin>h</margin> different positions; it is not easy for them to produce even a single ditone accurately. For they would more likely produce a whole tone than a ditone; since <margin>d</margin> the tone itself <margin>e</margin> is <margin>f</margin> <margin>g</margin> melodic and in a sesquioctave ratio; but the <margin>h</margin> uncompounded ditone is unmelodic; being in the ratio <margin>h</margin> of 81 to 64. Those things <margin>i</margin> which are more <term>symmetrical</term> <note>commensurable or having simpler ratios</note> <margin>i</margin> are more easily grasped by the senses.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. XI.

## C H A P. XI.
## 11.

->*How it may be demonstrated, even to the sense of hearing itself (by means of the Eight-stringed Rule), that the Octave is less than six Whole Tones.*<-

->*Πῶς ἂν καὶ διὰ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ᵃ ἐπιδειχθείη, τὸ διὰ πασῶν ἔλαττον ἑξ ᵇ τόνων, δι’ ᶜ ὀκταχόρδου κανόνος.*<-

**T**he proposition will be even more clearly refuted (beyond that well-known weakness of the ears) by the <term>Octave</term> <note>original: *Dia-pason*; the interval of an 8th</note> unison itself. For they <note>referring to the Aristoxenian school of music theorists</note> assert (following from the premise that the <term>Fourth</term> <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note> consonance consists of two and a half tones) that the Octave consists of Six Tones: because the Octave contains the Fourth twice, plus a Whole Tone. But if we were to command even the most skilled musician to produce six Whole Tones, placed continuously and independently (without simultaneously adding other previously tuned notes, lest he be led astray toward some other known consonance), the first note compared to the seventh will not produce an Octave. Now, if this happens not because of a weakness of the sense (as if he simply failed to take those tones exactly), but rather because that is the nature of the thing, then it must certainly be pronounced false that the Octave consonance consists of six tones. Or, if it were because the Sense cannot grasp Tones exactly, then it is much less to be trusted in the grasping of <term>Major Thirds</term> <note>original: *Ditonorum*; literally "two-tones"</note>, by the help of which they think they discover that the Fourth is two and a half tones. And this is most true: for not only is an Octave not produced, but neither is any other interval produced precisely to the magnitude of a difference repeated several times; whether the differences are already tuned, or (in whatever way) always repeated from the same ones. But if, in the same manner, continuously—

**Ε**ναργέστερον δ’ ἂν ἄρα ἐπελέγχοιτο τὸ προκείμενον... <note>The Greek text mirrors the Latin translation provided above, arguing that the ear cannot reliably stack six equal tones to reach a perfect octave.</note>

<meta>catchword: —pon (continuing the Greek word "manner")</meta>

---

<note>Footnotes and Critical Apparatus</note>

Cʜᴀᴘ. XI. **a** S. demonstrated. **b** GI. tones. **c** CK. under the string. **d** C. lacks the clause "following... tones." **e** S. [variation in suffix]. **f** C. will make. **g** So GISO. (and C. in text), but ABD. (and C. in margin) "enclosed together" (also omitting "indeed"). **h** C. "this," others "it would be." **i** V. (separated) "the man would appear." **k** GIO. "to be of tones." **l** V. "the." **m** AB. "of them." **n** AB. "yet." **o** ABCV. "they think" (referring to the Senses); others "they think" (referring to persons). **p** CS. [variation in suffix]. **q** GIVO. "And it is." **r** *Most true* <note>original: *Ἀληθέστερον*</note>, I interpret as "most true"; since for Ptolemy (as for others), the Comparative is frequently used with the sense of a Superlative: namely, he affirms this is most true, that an Octave is not constituted by six tones. If anyone feels it should be taken in a comparative sense (as if Ptolemy preferred the latter part of the previous disjunction as "truer"), the syntax can bear this; but reason dissuades it, since in Ptolemy's opinion (which he follows here) the first part is truer; namely, that it happens not so much from the weakness of sense as from the nature of the thing (that six tones taken continuously do not yield an Octave) because an Octave does not consist of six tones. **s** CVMD. Through. **t** GIOV. yet. **u** MDV. upon all. **x** This sense is somewhat obscure; but I believe Ptolemy means this: from a Tone repeated several times (which is the difference between a Fifth and a Fourth) there is not constituted (precisely) either an octave or any other musical interval (perhaps he means to except the fifth, octave, or other multiples), no musical interval (tone, fourth, fifth, etc.) repeated identically several times will make a [consonant] musical interval; (for a double-fourth, double-fifth, and others of that sort are, in Ptolemy's opinion, *unmelodic* <note>original: *ἐκμελῆ*</note>, and even the "ditone" taken in this sense—that is, two 9:8 ratios—is unmelodic; for the *melodic* <note>original: *ἐμμελές*</note> ditone is composed of a 9:8 and a 10:9 ratio). Ptolemy wishes to insinuate here that a larger interval is composed not from the same smaller intervals repeated, but from dissimilar ones placed continuously; as, in the words immediately following, an Octave is from a Fourth and a Fifth. In codex S. the whole clause reads: "for the middle does not become the octave, nor any other dense magnitude, the difference is entirely of the same things being tuned, yet always upon the same." As if he meant: the latter part of the disjunction should be judged truer (that the sense cannot precisely reach minute differences), for from a tone taken six times continuously, there will neither be an Octave, nor any one magnitude always the same (but now a little larger, now a little smaller). But even so the words are not clear enough; and I prefer the former reading because of the following clause. **y** GIO.

<summary>Ptolemy argues that the musical Octave is mathematically smaller than six whole tones, asserting that human hearing is an unreliable judge for stacking identical intervals.</summary>
