# CHAPTER II. OF HARMONICS. BOOK I.

<margin>I.</margin> ...of them, in succession; <note>original: "ἐφεξῆς"</note> the **fourth** <note>Greek: *dia tessaron*</note> and the **fifth** <note>Greek: *dia pente*</note> combined; the outer notes will produce the **octave** <note>Greek: *dia pason*</note>; because the extremes of these are more consonant. By the ratio, if six successive **tones** <note>a "tone" here is the interval of a 9:8 ratio</note> are taken, the outer notes will produce a magnitude somewhat **greater** than the octave; and always by that same **excess**; namely, double the amount by which the **remainder** <note>term: *Limma* — the small interval left over when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth</note> falls short of the **semitone**; which is calculated to be very nearly in the **sixty-fourth** ratio <note>original: "ἐξηξηκοσοτετάρτῳ λόγῳ"</note>; following consistently from the initial hypotheses.

This should be easy for us to observe: by attaching seven other strings to that one on the **monochord** <note>term: *Canon* — a single-stringed instrument used for measuring musical intervals</note>, using a similar test and arrangement. For if we tune the eight notes to the same pitch, with the lengths of the strings exactly equal, as A B C D E F G H; then, by applying the bridge <note>term: *Subductorium* — a movable bridge placed under the strings to change their vibrating length</note>, divided into six successive **nine-to-eight** <note>term: *sesquioctava* — the ratio 9:8, which defines a whole tone</note> ratios, let us move, for each note, the bridge to its proper section; so that the distance AK is a 9:8 ratio to the distance BL, and this likewise to CM, and this to DN, and this to EX, and this to FO, and this finally to GP; and further, let AK be in a **double ratio** <note>2:1 ratio, the mathematical definition of an octave</note> to HR. These notes (AK and HR) will indeed exhibit a precise **octave** unison; however, the note PG will be slightly (but always by the same amount) **sharper** than RH.

-> [Diagram of the Canon] <-
<note>The diagram illustrates eight strings of equal length, with movable bridges (K through R) placed at mathematically calculated points to demonstrate the cumulative difference between six whole tones and one octave.</note>

That these strings behave the same way, even if they are many, as if they were one and the same—provided they are tuned to the same pitch at equal lengths—will be made manifest from this: Since, in these matters, there are **three causes** of the difference between **high pitch** <note>sharpness</note> and **low pitch** <note>gravity</note>: one in the **density** of the strings; another in the **circumference** (or the mass of the string's thickness); and the third in the **length**, or the distance between the fixed points. The sound becomes **sharper** when produced by a string that is denser, or thinner, or of a **shorter length**...

---

<margin>Notes on the Greek and Latin variants:</margin>
<gloss>γ: GIO reads "the fourth to the fifth."</gloss>
<gloss>δ: AB note: For the Semitone to the Limma (as shown before) is as 258 to 256, or 129 to 128...</gloss>
<gloss>σ: The diagram in all manuscripts has one continuous straight line cutting all strings; this does not agree with Ptolemy's intent, as such a line would not cut the strings in the proper proportion... we have restored the proper section to each string.</gloss>

<summary>Ptolemy demonstrates through a multi-stringed canon that six whole tones exceed a perfect octave by a small mathematical margin (the Pythagorean comma). He also begins explaining the three physical properties that determine a string's pitch: density, thickness, and length.</summary>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. II.

the distance is smaller; but if we substitute **tension** in place of density (as that which strengthens and hardens, whereby **Strings** are rendered more similar to those at smaller distances), it is manifest that (other things being assumed equal) as the greater tension is to the lesser, so is the sound produced by the greater to that produced by the lesser. Furthermore, as the greater circuit <note>original: *circuitus* / *periochē*; referring to the circumference or thickness of the string</note> is to the lesser, so is the sound produced in the lesser to that in the greater. 

I say now (these things being so) that for dissimilar strings, when they are made equal in tone <note>original: *æquitonæ* / *isotonoi*; having the same pitch</note> at equal distances, that which the sound lacks due to the greater circuit is compensated by that which is redundant due to the greater tension. And altogether, the ratio of the greater circuit to the lesser becomes the same as the ratio of the greater tension to the lesser. 

For let there be, at equal lengths, sounds A and B of equal tone; and let the circuit of A be greater than that of B. It is manifest that the tension of B is less than that of A. Let another string C be taken at the same length, which has the same circuit as B, but the same tension as A. Since, therefore, C differs from B only in tension, it will be that as the tension in C is to the tension in B, so is the sound of C to the sound of B. Again, since C differs from A only in circuit, it will be that as the circuit of A is to the circuit of C, so is the sound of C to the sound of A. But the sound of C has the same ratio to each, namely to A and B (since the sounds of A and B are indeed equal). Therefore, as the tension of C is to the tension of B, so is the circuit of A to the circuit of C. And it is as the tension of C is to the tension of B, so is the tension of A to the tension of B (for the tensions of A and C are equal); and as the circuit of A is to the circuit of C, so is the circuit of A to the circuit of B (for the circuits of B and C are equal).

<note>The following Greek text is the original version of the Latin passage translated above.</note>

...instead of density, tension; for it strengthens and <margin>d</margin> hardens; whence <margin>e</margin> they are more similar in form to those in smaller distances. It is clear that, other things being assumed the same, as the greater tension is to the lesser, so is the sound according to the greater to the sound according to the lesser; <margin>f</margin> as <margin>g</margin> the greater circuit is to the lesser, so is the sound according to the lesser to the sound according to the greater. I say then, these things being so, that of dissimilar strings, when they are made equal in tone at equal distances, <margin>h, i</margin> the sound lacking <margin>k</margin> due to the greater circuit is compensated by the sound exceeding <margin>l</margin> due to the greater tension; <margin>m</margin> it happens entirely that the <margin>n</margin> ratio of the greater circuit to the lesser is the same as that of the greater tension to the lesser. For let there be <margin>p</margin>, in equal lengths, <margin>q</margin> two sounds of equal tone, <margin>r</margin> α and β. And let the circuit <margin>s</margin> of α be greater than that of β. And <margin>t</margin> it is clear that <margin>u, x, z</margin> the tension <margin>a</margin> of β is less than that of α. Let <margin>c</margin> another be taken, in equal length, γ; having a circuit equal to β, but <margin>f</margin> a tension equal <margin>g</margin> to α. Since then γ differs from β only in tension, it will be as the <margin>k</margin> tension of γ is to the tension of β, so the sound of γ is to the sound of β. Again, since γ differs from α only in circuit, it will be as the circuit of α is to the circuit of γ, <margin>k</margin> so the sound of γ is to the sound of α. But the sound of γ has the same ratio to each <margin>x</margin> of the sounds α and β; <margin>b</margin> for α and β are equal. As therefore the tension of γ is to that of β, so the circuit of α is to that of γ. And it is as the tension of γ is to that of β, so the <margin>c</margin> tension of α is to that of β; for the <margin>d</margin> tensions of α and γ are equal; and as the circuit of α is to that of γ, so the circuit of α is to that of β; for the circuits of β and γ are equal.

<image-desc>A logical diagram comparing three notes (A/α, C/γ, B/β) and their properties of circuitus (periochē) and tenſio (tasis). 
- Line A (α) is labeled with "greater circuit" and "greater tension".
- Line C (γ) is bracketed on the left with B (β) as "lesser circuit" and on the right with A (α) as "greater tension".

->Synopsis of the Demonstration.<-

| Given Data. | Demonstrated Facts. |
| :--- | :--- |
| *Length* $\alpha = l \beta = l \gamma$ | $l \gamma . l \beta :: \int \gamma . \int \beta$. <note>Ratio of lengths to sounds</note> |
| *Sound ..* $\alpha = \int \beta$ | $c \alpha . c \gamma :: \int \gamma . (\int \alpha =) \int \beta$. <note>Ratio of circuits to sounds</note> |
| *Circuit* $\gamma = c \beta < c \alpha$ | $l \gamma (= l \alpha) . l \beta :: c \alpha . c \gamma (= c \beta.)$ |
| *Tension ...* $\gamma = \tau \alpha > \tau \beta$ | $\tau \alpha . \tau \beta :: c \alpha . c \beta$. <note>The final proof: Tension ratio equals Circuit ratio</note> |

---

<summary>Ptolemy demonstrates through a logical proof that if two strings of different thickness (circuit) produce the same pitch, the ratio of their tensions must be inversely proportional to the ratio of their thicknesses. He uses a third hypothetical string (C/γ) to bridge the comparison between the two primary strings.</summary>

# Chapter 12. HARMONICS BOOK I.

And so, as the tension of A is to the tension of B, so is the circumference <note>original: *periochē* / *circuitus*. In this context, it refers to the vibration or the spatial cycle of the string.</note> of A to the circumference of B. This would happen to them equally if they were entirely unchanging and did not differ from one another. Furthermore, in these circumstances—take for example AB and CD—let us make the distances unequal by diminishing one of them, say to CE. It will follow that as the distance AB is to the distance CE, so the sound of CE is to the sound of AB. For since the distance CD is to the distance CE as the sound of CE is to the sound of CD; and since the distance AB is equal to CD, and the sound of AB is equal to the sound of CD; it follows then that as the distance AB is to the distance CE, so the sound of CE is to the sound of AB.

## 12.

On the division of the <term>genera</term> <note>The "genera" (classes) of scales in ancient Greek music: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</note> according to <term>Aristoxenus</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle, famous for his writings on music theory which emphasized human hearing over pure mathematical ratios.</note>, and on the individual tetrachords.

## CHAPTER XII.

On the division of the Genera according to Aristoxenus, and of the Tetrachords in each.

Regarding the greater differences in sounds, let these things be defined by us. We must now pass on to the smaller ones, namely those that measure the first <term>consonance</term> <note>A "consonance" is a combination of notes that sounds pleasing or stable.</note>. These arise from the <term>Fourth</term> <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*. An interval spanning four notes of a scale.</note> being divided into three ratios, in a manner following the previously defined ways. This is so that the first <term>unison</term> <note>original: *Dia-pason*. Here used to mean the Octave, which the author describes as a "single" sound composed of two primary consonances.</note>, being a unity, is composed of two primary consonances; and the first consonance (the Fourth) is composed of three <term>melodic intervals</term> <note>original: *emmeleiai*. Notes or intervals suitable for melody.</note>, reaching the number that limits its proportion. It happens that the division of the Fourth itself is not always the same everywhere, but is constituted now in one way, now in another. While the two outer sounds remain fixed so that they preserve the proposed consonance (for which reason they call them "Standing" notes), the two intermediate notes are moved so that they create unequal intervals between the sounds within it. It is called...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory from the previous section, specifically focusing on the classification of musical "Genera" or types of scales.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 12.

Therefore, such a motion is a **Change according to Genus.**<sup>k</sup> A **Genus** <note>original: *Genus*</note> in Harmony is, essentially, the specific relationship to one another of the sounds that compose the **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> consonance.<sup>m</sup> The first distribution of the Genera is into **Two** parts;<sup>n</sup> insofar as one is **Softer** and the other is **More Intense**. The Softer is that which exhibits a mode of constriction:<sup>s</sup> the More Intense is that which is more relaxed. (In the former, the intermediate sounds cling closer to the lowest note, and are thus "softer" or lower; in the latter, they are further from it, and are thus "more intense" or higher.)

The second distribution is into **three** parts:<sup>t</sup> a third being interposed, as it were, in the middle of the two already mentioned. And this (interjected) one is called the **Chromatic Genus**: of the remaining two, the softer is called the **Enharmonic**; the more intense, the **Diatonic**.<sup>u</sup>

It is a property of the Enharmonic and Chromatic genera to be what is called <term>Dense</term> <note>original: *Spissum* / *Pyknon*; a grouping of small intervals at the bottom of a tetrachord</note>: (this occurs when the two ratios at the bottom, when added together, are smaller than the single remaining ratio.) The property of the Diatonic is what is called <term>Non-dense</term> <note>original: *Non-spissum* / *Apyknon*</note>; (namely, when no single ratio of the three is greater than the other two added together.)

Of these very Genera, the "Moderns" make many more distributions: but we, for the present, will describe those belonging to **Aristoxenus**;<sup>c</sup> which are as follows. He divides the **Tone** <note>a whole step</note> sometimes into two, sometimes into three, sometimes into four, and sometimes into eight equal parts. 

*   A fourth part of a tone he calls an **Enharmonic Diesis**:<sup>d</sup> <note>a "diesis" is a microtonal interval</note>
*   A third part, a **Diesis of Soft Chromatic**:<sup>e</sup>
*   A fourth plus an eighth part, a **Diesis of Sesquialter Chromatic**:<sup>f</sup> <note>also known as Hemiolic Chromatic</note>
*   The **Semitone** <note>half-tone</note> he makes common to both the Tonic Chromatic and the Diatonic genera.

From these, they establish **Six Differences** (or species) of (pure) Genera: One of the **Enharmonic**;<sup>g</sup> three of the **Chromatic** (namely, Soft, Sesquialter, and Tonic); and the remaining two of the **Diatonic** (namely, Soft and Intense).

In the **Enharmonic Genus**, the interval at the very bottom (or the Following one), as well as the middle one, each makes an Enharmonic Diesis;<sup>h</sup> and the remainder (which is called the Leading one) consists of two Tones. (For example: if the number 24 is assigned to a tone; each interval of the "Dense" portion makes 6; while the remainder makes 48.)

In the **Soft Chromatic**;<sup>i</sup> of the intervals constituting the "Dense" portion, each makes a third of a tone;<sup>k</sup> while the remainder makes one tone plus a half and a third:<sup>l</sup> (For example; each of the former is 8;<sup>m</sup> while the latter is 44.)<sup>n</sup>

In the **Hemiolic**<sup>o</sup> (or Sesquialter) **Chromatic**; of the intervals of the "Dense" portion, each makes a fourth and an eighth part of one tone;<sup>p</sup> while the remainder makes one tone plus a half and a fourth;<sup>q</sup>

<column-break/>

[The Greek text below mirrors the Latin above, providing the original terminology of Ptolemy.]

...such a motion, a change according to genus.<sup>k</sup> And genus,<sup>l</sup> in harmony, is a certain relationship to one another of those notes completing the consonance of the **fourth**.<sup>m</sup> Of the genera, the first difference is as if into two;<sup>n</sup> according to the softer and according to the more intense.<sup>o</sup> The softer is that which is more constricting<sup>q</sup> of character; the more intense, that which is more diffusive.<sup>s</sup> The second is as if into three;<sup>t</sup> a third being placed somehow between the two mentioned.<sup>u</sup> And this is called **Chromatic**. Of the rest, the softer of them is **Enharmonic**; the more intense, **Diatonic**. A property of the Enharmonic and the Chromatic is that called <term>Dense</term> <note>original: *Pyknon*</note>; when the two ratios at the lowest part, taken together, are less than the remaining two. Of the Diatonic, that called <term>Non-dense</term>;<sup>x</sup> when not one of the three ratios becomes greater than the other two combined.<sup>z</sup> The moderns make even more differences of these. But we, at present, will outline the **Aristoxenian**<sup>c</sup> systems, which are thus. He divides the tone sometimes into two equal parts; sometimes into three; sometimes into four; sometimes into eight. And the fourth part he calls an **enharmonic diesis**;<sup>d</sup> the third, a **diesis of soft chromatic**;<sup>e</sup> the fourth and eighth, a **diesis of hemiolic chromatic**;<sup>f</sup> and the semitone he makes common to the chromatic and diatonic genera. From which he establishes six differences<sup>g</sup> of the unmixed genera: One of the enharmonic; three of the chromatic: soft, hemiolic, and tonic; and the remaining two of the diatonic: soft and intense. In the enharmonic genus: the one at the lowest, and the following interval, and the middle, each makes an enharmonic diesis;<sup>h</sup> the remainder and leading one, two tones. For example, if the number for the tone is 24; each of the intervals of the "dense" is 6;<sup>i</sup> the remainder 48. In the soft chromatic,<sup>k</sup> each of the intervals of the "dense" makes a third of a tone;<sup>l</sup> the remainder, one and a half and a third;<sup>m</sup> for example, each of those is 8; this one 44.<sup>n</sup> In the hemiolic chromatic;<sup>o</sup> of the intervals of the "dense," each makes a fourth and an eighth of a tone;<sup>p</sup> the remainder, one and a half and a fourth;<sup>q</sup>

...fourths: <note>original: "quartæ"</note> (Namely; each of those is 9; but this one is 42.) In the Tonic Chroma; of the two intervals in the "thickened" cluster <term>Pyknon: a structural grouping of three notes in the lower part of a tetrachord</term>, it makes each a half-tone; but the remaining interval is one tone and a half: (Namely; each of those is 12; but this one is 36.) In the remaining two genera, which are not "thickened"; the following interval in both retains the half-tone: Of those which follow in succession; in the Soft Diatonic, it makes the middle interval a half plus a quarter of a tone; but the preceding one is a tone and a quarter; (namely, 12, and 18, and 30:) In the Intense Diatonic; the following is a half-tone: of the remaining two (the middle and the preceding), each is a full tone; (namely, 12, and 24, and 24) as the numbers are set out below.

# CHAPTER XIII.

## Concerning the division of the Genera and Tetrachords according to Archytas.

**H**e <note>Aristoxenus</note> therefore, as is evident here and everywhere, cared nothing for mathematical ratio; but defined the Genera by the intervals lying between the sounds alone, and not by the ratios of the sounds to one another. Regarding the causes of the differences as non-causes and as nothing, and taking only the outer limits, he applied his comparisons to incorporeal and empty spaces. For this reason, he thinks nothing of dividing musical melodies in half almost everywhere; even though superparticular ratios <term>Superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is one greater than the denominator, like 3:2 or 4:3</term> by no means allow for such a thing. But Archytas of Tarentum, who among the Pythagoreans was most diligent in musical matters, attempts to preserve what is consistent with Ratio: not only in the Consonances, but also in the divisions of the Tetrachords: (As if the nature of melodic sounds were inherent...)

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 13.

...the commensuration of the excesses <note>original: *excessuum Commensuratio*; referring to the mathematical balance between the intervals</note> would be appropriate.) However, although he has set this as his entire goal, he is clearly found to err from it in certain respects. In most cases, he adheres to it, yet in such a way that he manifestly departs from those things which are directly perceived by the senses: as we shall soon see in the division of the tetrachords according to him. He establishes three <term>Genera</term> <note>The three "styles" or "families" of Greek melody</note>: the **Enharmonic**, the **Chromatic**, and the **Diatonic**. He institutes the division of each in the following manner. He establishes the following ratio as the same in all three Genera: namely, the <term>sesquivigesimaseptima</term> <note>The ratio 28:27</note>. The middle ratio, however, he sets in the **Enharmonic** as the <term>sesquitrigesimaquinta</term> <note>36:35</note>; and in the **Diatonic** as the <term>sesquiseptima</term> <note>8:7</note>. From this it is gathered that in the **Enharmonic** genus, the leading ratio is the <term>sesquiquarta</term> <note>5:4</note>; and in the **Diatonic**, the <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>9:8</note>. 

But, in the **Chromatic** genus, he takes the second note from the highest and derives it from the one that holds the same position in the **Diatonic**: for he says that the second note from the highest in the **Chromatic** genus has the same ratio to the similar note in the **Diatonic** as 256 has to 243. Therefore, these three tetrachords are established according to the ratios set forth in these smallest numbers. If we set the highest notes of the tetrachords at 1512, and the lowest (in the <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>4:3</note> ratio) at 2016: these will make a <term>sesquivigesimaseptima</term> ratio to 1944; and there will be just as many, in the three genera, for the second notes from the lowest. Of the second notes from the highest: that of the **Enharmonic** genus is 1890 (since these make a <term>sesquitrigesimaquinta</term> ratio to 1944, and a <term>sesquiquarta</term> to 1512); but of the **Diatonic** genus, the same is 1701 (for these make a <term>sesquiseptima</term> ratio to 1944, and a <term>sesquioctava</term> to 1512); and of the **Chromatic** genus, the same will be 1792 (since these have the same ratio to 1701 as 256 has to 243). The exposition of these same numbers is provided below, arranged as follows.

<note>The following Greek text is the original source for the Latin/English translation provided above, detailing the mathematical ratios of the three musical genera.</note>

| | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | |
| | 1890 | 1890 | 1792 | 1792 | 1701 | 1701 | |
| | | at $\frac{1}{35}$ | | | | at $\frac{1}{7}$ | |
| | 1944 | 1944 | 1944 | 1944 | 1944 | 1944 | |
| | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | | at $\frac{1}{27}$ | |
| Following toward the Low. | 2016 | 2016 | 2016 | 2016 | 2016 | 2016 | *In Grave, consequens.* |

Chapter 14. BOOK I OF THE HARMONICS.

14.
### Proof that neither of the divisions preserves that which is truly melodic.

### CHAPTER XIV.
### Demonstration that neither of these divisions retains that which is truly harmonious.

Contrary indeed to his own <sup>a</sup> proposition, as <sup>b</sup> we said, the chromatic tetrachord <note>A four-note scale segment</note> was constructed by him. For the number 1792 makes a ratio that is neither superparticular <note>A ratio of the form (n+1)/n, like 3/2 or 4/3, which Greeks considered more "musical"</note> to 1512, nor to 1944. Furthermore, it is contrary to the <sup>c</sup> evidence of the senses; both the chromatic and the enharmonic. For we perceive the ratio following the usual chromatic to be larger than the 27th part <note>original: "ἐπικατογδοηκοστοῦ" / "sesquivigesimaseptimam"</note>. And again, he assumes the following ratio in the enharmonic—which appears much smaller than in the other similar <sup>g</sup> genera—to be equal to them. And in addition to these, <sup>h</sup> by placing it <sup>i</sup> smaller than the middle, in a ratio of 36 to 35 <note>original: "ἐπιτριακοσπέμπτῳ"</note>, such a thing becomes everywhere manifestly unmelodic; insofar as the magnitude at the lowest note would be established as <sup>j</sup> greater than the middle. These things indeed seem to bring <sup>k</sup> reproach upon the Rational Criterion; because, when the <sup>m</sup> division of the Canon <note>The Monochord, a single-stringed instrument used for measuring intervals</note> is made according to the ratios set forth by those <sup>l</sup> who defined it, it does not preserve the melodic quality. For <sup>o</sup> most of the underlying ratios, and those fashioned by almost all the other *Pythagoreans*, do not fit the <sup>p</sup> agreed-upon characters <sup>q</sup> in melodies. It <sup>r</sup> also seems that the <sup>s</sup> number of genera, according to <sup>t</sup> Archytas, is deficient in measurement; for he assumes not only the enharmonic itself, but also <sup>u</sup> the chromatic and the diatonic, to each be of a <sup>v</sup> single form <note>Archytas only provided one mathematical model for each, whereas musical practice had many variations</note>. But according to Aristoxenus, the number is excessive in the chromatic, <sup>x</sup> where the quarter-tones <note>dieses</note> of the "soft" and "hemiolic" types <sup>y</sup> differ by only a <sup>z</sup> twenty-fourth part of a tone, so as to produce no noteworthy variation in kind to the sense of hearing; or <sup>a</sup> in the diatonic, where more melodies clearly appear; <sup>b</sup> as can be seen from what will be demonstrated immediately. <sup>c</sup> Furthermore, these men do not act soundly in that, in the "dense" passages <note>pykna: the closely packed lower intervals of a tetrachord</note>, they <sup>d</sup> make two magnitudes equal to one another; whereas the middle is everywhere perceived as <sup>e</sup> larger. And again, the intervals adjacent to the lowest note in both the <sup>f</sup> diatonic and the <sup>g</sup> tonic chromatic <term>toniaei <gloss>τονιαίου</gloss></term> <note>A specific "flavor" of the chromatic scale</note> are established as larger in the chromatic.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 15.

## CHAP. XV.

### On such a division of the Tetrachords according to Genus, which is consistent both with Reason and with Observations.

### On the division of the tetrachords according to genus, following what is reasonable and what is apparent. <note>original: "Περὶ τῆς κατὰ τὸ εὔλογον καὶ τὸ φαινόμενον τῶν κατὰ γένος τετραχόρδων διαιρέσεως."</note>

**C**OME now; since the primary genera of the tetrachords have not been divided by those [previous theorists] in a way that satisfies the senses: Let us ourselves endeavor here also to preserve that which is consistent both with the hypotheses of **Harmonious Intervals** <term>Concinnitatum: from 'concinnitas', referring to musically pleasing or "apt" intervals</term> and with the **Phenomena** <note>The "Phenomena" refers to observed musical reality or what is heard by the ear.</note>; following the first suggestions that are natural to the nature of divisions. We assume as a *Lemma* <term>Lemma: a subsidiary proposition used to prove a larger point</term> (both from the hypothesis originally laid down and from reason) this principle common to all genera: Namely, that **Sounds**, even in Tetrachords, when placed in succession, always create **super-particular ratios** <term>Super-particular: a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator (e.g., 3:2, 4:3)</term> between one another; specifically those which look toward the sections of a whole into two or three near-equal parts. By such sections, the excesses of the primary consonances were determined; and these proceeded as far as the number three, as that which sufficed for completing all distances. For from the **Octave** <term>Dia-pason</term> Unison, and the double ratio (in which the excess of the outer terms is established as equal to the term that is exceeded), there was taken—in moving away from equality by way of Diminution—both the **sesquialtera** ratio (which is the Consonance of the **Fifth** <term>Dia-pente</term>), in which the excess of the outer terms contains a half part of the term exceeded; and the **sesquitertia** ratio (which is the consonance of the **Fourth** <term>Dia-tessaron</term>), in which the excess of the outer terms contains a third part of the term exceeded. But in moving away from equality by way of Increase; from there was taken both the **triple** ratio (which is the consonance of the **Octave and a Fifth**), in which the excess of the outer terms is double that which is exceeded (which is the opposite of the half part); and the **quadruple** ratio (which is the Unison of the **Double Octave** <term>Dis-dia-pason</term>), in which the excess of the outer terms makes three times that which is exceeded (which is likewise the opposite of the third part). From the sense...

---

<note>
CHAP. 15. a S. [reads] "of the". b C. "and here". c S. lacks "to preserve". d S. "to the". e CKSV. lack "and". f DM. lack "the". g "Quantities" <note>original: "Πηλικότητων"</note> (understand: of the notes or sounds in relation to each other) I translate here as quantities (as it is usually explained by others, though I wouldn't say how accurately). He hints at either the quotients or the denominators or exponents of the ratios (to be investigated by division) by which it is indicated how great or how much any ratio is, or how the preceding term relates to the following (for instance, how many times it is, or what part, etc.) as in the definition of Ratio in Euclid, Book 5, Def 3 (which many do not weigh accurately enough), where "their quantity in relation to each other is a certain quality of relation." It is that qualitative Relation (or Habit) between them (that is, how this relates to that) which is according to the Quotient: (for instance, how many times, or what part, etc. unless we prefer to say 'how-many-fold' and 'how great a part'). And I think this is to be understood wherever the same word 'quantity' <note>original: "πηλικότης"</note> recurs. h S. "moving". i CKV. lack "the", which is however emphatic here; namely, that not only the sounds of the Consonances (which everyone posits) but also the continuous sounds in the division of the tetrachord (which not everyone observed) are to be established in a super-particular ratio. Ptolemy religiously observes this in all cases, except in the Ditonic Diatonic, which he adds to the remaining genera at the end of the following chapter; at least to be endured for a special reason. k S. "such as". l S. "were completed". m C. adds here "by a cutting from the equal." KV. "by a cutting from the equal." which, I believe, crept into the text from a marginal note. n S. "uniting". o S. "years". p V. "contains". q C. lacks the clause "and the sesquitertia -- of the exceeded." but it is supplied in the margin by a more recent hand. r GI. "sesquitertia" [accusative], the rest correctly "sesquitertia" [genitive]. s GIO. "to increase". t S. [illegible]. u S. "makes two". v CS. [variant spelling]. y S. lacks "three".
</note>

<meta>continuity: catchword "z S." refers to a manuscript variant on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Ptolemy introduces his method for dividing the tetrachord, arguing that successive notes must follow "super-particular" ratios (like 3:2 or 4:3) to be mathematically and harmonically sound. He bases these divisions on the same principles that define the primary musical intervals like the octave, fifth, and fourth.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, tetrachord, musical ratios, super-particular, octave, fifth, fourth, harmonics, Greek music theory</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of harmonic ratios and the division of the tetrachord, specifically focusing on the sesquitertian ratio (4:3) which defines the interval of a fourth.</meta>

# Chapter 15. HARMONICS BOOK I.

...according to the evidence of the senses, we take as a common principle <note>original: "pro Lemmate sumimus," a lemma is a preliminary proposition</note> for all genera alike: that of the three following magnitudes, the last is always smaller than either of the remaining two. Specifically: for those genera that have a **dense** <term>Spissum: a "dense" cluster of three notes (pyknon) where the sum of the two smaller intervals is less than the remaining large interval</term> arrangement, the two intervals at the lowest pitch, when taken together, are smaller than the one at the highest pitch. But for those that do not have a dense arrangement, no single magnitude is greater than the other two combined.

These things being presupposed, we first divide the **sesquitertian** ratio <note>The ratio 4:3, representing the musical interval of a fourth</note> (which belongs to the consonance of the **Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note>), as often as possible, into two **superparticular** ratios <term>Superparticular: a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator (e.g., 5:4, 6:5)</term>. This can be done in only three ways. This is achieved by taking the three superparticulars that follow it in sequence: the **sesquiquartal** (5:4), the **sesquiquintal** (6:5), and the **sesquisextal** (7:6). For the sesquitertian is completed by adding the **sesquififteenth** (16:15) to the sesquiquartal; the **sesquinonal** (10:9) to the sesquiquintal; and the **sesquiseptimal** (8:7) to the sesquisextal. Beyond these, we will not find the sesquitertian composed of any other two superparticulars.

---

->**Sesquitertian (4:3)**<-

<image-desc>Three mathematical diagrams illustrating the division of the sesquitertian ratio (4:3). Each diagram contains three numbers and labels for their constituent superparticular ratios.
1. The first shows the numbers 60, 75, and 80. Above them are labels "5:4" and "16:15". Below is the equation 5/4 x 16/15 = 4/3.
2. The second shows the numbers 45, 54, and 60. Above are labels "6:5" and "10:9". Below is the equation 6/5 x 10/9 = 4/3.
3. The third shows the numbers 6, 7, and 8. Above are labels "7:6" and "8:7". Below is the equation 7/6 x 8/7 = 4/3.
Curved lines connect the numbers to represent the proportions described by the ratios.</image-desc>

---

ratio. Now, regarding those genera that contain a **dense** cluster: since in these, the leading ratios are greater than the other two combined, he assigns the larger ratios of the set pairings—namely the **sesquiquartal**, the **sesquiquintal**, and the **sesquisextal**—to their leading ratios. The remaining smaller ratios—namely the **sesquififteenth**, the **sesquinonal**, and the **sesquiseptimal**—he assigns to the sum of the other two intervals. The division of each of these occurs according to the two following ratios, by taking their divisions into three... <margin>The sense is somewhat obscure. Why, for instance, would he divide this ratio into three, when only two are required to complete the three ratios of the Tetrachord? He could have divided the ratio into two nearly equal superparticulars by doubling the numbers... but he triples them instead... I do not see the reason, unless it is so that the following of the three is notably smaller than either of the others (as he presupposed), which otherwise would have been nearly equal to the middle one.</margin> ...namely of the Tetrachord...

---

# Chapter 15. - OF HARMONICS. BOOK I.

By tripling <note>original: "triplicando"</note>, we will have 21 and 24: and between these, the intermediate numbers (with equal differences) are 22 and 23. Since 23 does not form a superparticular ratio <note>A ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, e.g., 3/2 or 4/3</note> with either extreme, but only 22 does; namely, with 24 it forms a *sesquiduodecimal* <note>13/12 ratio</note>; and, with 21, a *sesquivigesimaprima* <note>22/21 ratio</note>: Here, therefore, to the *sesquisexta* <note>7/6 ratio</note>, the *sesquiundecima* <note>12/11 ratio</note> must be joined next; and the *sesquivigesimaprima* <note>22/21 ratio</note> will occupy the following place.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of musical tunings from Chapter 15 of Ptolemy’s Harmonics. The previous discussion established the ratios for "dense" (spissum) genera; here, the author transitions to "non-dense" genera, specifically the Soft Diatonic.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Cap. 15.

...of the seconds from the leaders, 132,825 and 127,512 and 123,970; of the thirds, 138,600 and 136,620 and 135,240. Just as the attached descriptions hold. <note>These large numbers represent string lengths or monochord divisions in "myriads" (units of 10,000) to express precise musical ratios.</note>

As for those genera which do not have a **Dense** <term>Spissum: a tuning where the sum of the two smaller intervals is less than the remaining interval</term> arrangement: since it is consistent with what was defined before, we should, conversely, place the **Smaller ratios** (of those arising from the first divisions) in the preceding positions; and divide their joined **larger ratios**, in the same manner, into the two following intervals. 

Now, the **fifteenth-part** <term>sesquidecimaquinta: the ratio 16:15</term> was found to be entirely unsuitable to hold the preceding <insert>first</insert> position. (For if we triple the numbers 4 and 5, which contain the remaining **fourth-part** <term>sesquiquarta: 5:4</term>, so that they make 12 and 15; and the intermediate numbers falling between them with equal differences are 13 and 14: then 13 will not make a **superparticular** <term>a ratio of the form (n+1):n</term> ratio with either extreme; but 14, with 12, makes a **sixth-part** <term>sesquisexta: 7:6</term>; and with 15, a **fourteenth-part** <term>sesquidecimaquarta: 15:14</term>. It is not permitted to place either of these in the following position, as they would be larger than the one in the preceding position—namely, the fifteenth-part—which contradicts both the evidence of the senses and the ratio established at the start.)

However, when the **seventh-part** <term>sesquiseptima: 8:7</term> is arranged in the preceding position; the first numbers (6 and 7) containing the remainder (the **sixth-part** <term>7:6</term>), when similarly tripled, will make 18 and 21: with the means 19 and 20 intervening with equal differences. Since 19 again does not make a superparticular ratio with either extreme, but 20 with 18 makes a **ninth-part** <term>sesquinona: 10:9</term>, and with 21 a **twentieth-part** <term>sesquivigesima: 21:20</term>; the larger of these (the ninth-part) must likewise be joined next to the seventh-part, and the smaller (the twentieth-part) will occupy the following place. And in the same way, if the **ninth-part** is placed in the preceding position; if the numbers 5 and 6 containing the remainder (the **fifth-part** <term>6:5</term>) are tripled, they make 15 and 18; with the means 16 and 17 falling between them: 17 will not make a superparticular ratio with either extreme, but 16 with 18 makes an **eighth-part** <term>sesquioctavam: 9:8</term>, which to...

<column-break/>

...specifically of the seconds from the leaders, both that of 132,825 and that of 127,512; and that of 123,970. Of the thirds, both that of 138,600 and that of 136,620, and that of 135,240. As the diagrams have it. In the case of the **non-dense** <note>Greek: ἀπύκνων (apyknon)</note> genera, it being consistent with the things previously determined, one should, conversely, place the smaller of the ratios resulting from the first division of the **fourth** <term>epitritos: the 4:3 ratio of a perfect fourth</term> in the leading positions; and the larger of the pairs remaining from them, in the same way, into the two following [intervals]. The **fifteenth-part** <note>original: ἐπιπεντεκαιδέκατος (16:15)</note> was found not to be able to hold the leading position. For if we triple the numbers containing the remaining **fourth-part** <note>original: ἐπιτέταρτον (5:4)</note>, that is 4 and 5, so they make 12 and 15, the means falling in equal excesses are 13 and 14. Now 13 will [not] make a superparticular ratio with either of the extremes, but 14 with 12 makes a **sixth-part** <note>original: ἐπίεκτον (7:6)</note>, and with 15 a **fourteenth-part** <note>original: ἐπιτεσσαρεσκαιδέκατον (15:14)</note>. Neither of these can be placed in the following position because it would be larger than the leading one, that is, the fifteenth-part; which is contrary to the evidence of the senses and the original ratio. But with the **seventh-part** <note>original: ἐπιεβδόμου (8:7)</note> being stationed in the leading position, the first numbers containing the remainder and the **sixth-part**, 6 and 7, being tripled likewise, will make 18 and 21, with the means taken in equal excesses being 19 and 20. The 19 again will [not] make a superparticular ratio with either extreme, but 20 with 18 makes a **ninth-part** <note>original: ἐπίενατον (10:9)</note>, and with 21 a **twentieth-part** <note>original: ἐπιεικοστόν (21:20)</note>. Of these, likewise, the larger and ninth-part will be joined to the seventh-part; and the smaller and twentieth-part will fill the following position. According to these same steps, also for the **ninth-part** stationed in the leading position; if the numbers containing the remainder and the **fifth-part**, 5 and 6, being tripled, make 15 and 18, with the means falling in equal excesses being 16 and 17. The 17 will [not] make a superparticular ratio with either extreme; but 16 with 18 makes an **eighth-part** <note>original: ἐπόγδοον (9:8)</note>...

<summary>This page details the mathematical derivation of the "Soft Diatonic" musical scale, explaining why certain ratios like 16:15 are rejected in favor of the 8:7, 10:9, and 21:20 sequence. Ptolemy uses a method of tripling numbers to find harmonic means that result in "superparticular" ratios suitable for tuning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ratios and the division of the tetrachord into specific diatonic scales, following the mathematical principles of Claudius Ptolemy.</meta>

### Intense Diatonic
->Intense Diatonic<-
->on the 4/3 ratio<- <note>original: "ἐπὶ γ'". This refers to the sesquitertial ratio of the perfect fourth.</note>

[Mathematical diagram of the Intense Diatonic scale: A bracket spans four values: 72, 80, 90, and 96. The ratios between them are 10:9 (between 72 and 80), 9:8 (between 80 and 90), and 16:15 (between 90 and 96). The summary equation shows how these combine to form the 4:3 ratio of the fourth.]

...with the 15, a <term>sesquidecimanquinta</term> <note>A ratio of 16:15, often called a "minor semitone".</note>. Thus, the larger ratio (which is the <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>A 9:8 ratio, the standard whole tone.</note>) is joined to the neighboring <term>sesquinona</term> <note>A 10:9 ratio, or "minor tone".</note>; and the remaining ratio (the 16:15) fits into the following place. But before all these, the <term>sesquioctava</term> ratio was found by itself to contain the Tone (from the difference between the first two consonances <note>The difference between a perfect fifth (3:2) and a perfect fourth (4:3) is a 9:8 tone.</note>). It is both reasonable and necessary that this ratio should hold the leading position, with those most closely related to it being joined together; since none of the <term>superparticular</term> <note>Ratios of the form (n+1)/n.</note> ratios will complete the <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>The 4:3 ratio of the fourth.</note> when added to it. Indeed, the <term>sesquinona</term> (10:9) has already been joined to it in the division just set forth; but the <term>sesquiseptima</term> <note>An 8:7 ratio.</note> has not yet. Therefore, we shall join this 8:7 ratio to it in the middle position, and we shall assign the remainder of the 4:3 ratio—which is the <term>sesquivigesimaseptima</term> <note>A 28:27 ratio.</note>—to the following place. And here again—

### Middle Soft Diatonic
->Middle Soft Diatonic<-

...the proper values of the second [notes] from the preceding ones are 576, 567, and 560; and of the third [notes], 640, 648, and 630. These are as shown in the attached descriptions. That the aforementioned divisions of the genera are not only consistent with Reason <note>Latin: *Rationi*; Greek: *λόγῳ*</note>, but are also in agreement with the senses, may again be perceived from the eight-stringed rule <term>Octachord canon: a one-stringed or multi-stringed instrument used to demonstrate mathematical musical intervals</term> containing the octave <note>Latin: *Dia-pason*</note>. The sounds, as we said in Chapter 8, must be accurately examined, both regarding the uniformity of the strings and the equality of the tones. For when the sections of the rules employed are made according to the ratios required by each genus, and the movable bridges <term>Magadiis: small wooden blocks used to change the vibrating length of a string</term> are placed accordingly, the octave will be so perfectly tuned <note>Latin: *aptatum*; Greek: *ἡρμοσμένον*</note> that there will be nothing to disturb even those most skilled in music. Rather, in this arrangement of tuning, we may admire nature: for according to this, Reason fashions and shapes the differences that preserve the melody, while Hearing obeys Reason as much as possible, being led by the order derived from it and recognizing in each part that which is uniquely pleasing. Those who have been authors of the part to be rejected <note>Referring to rival theorists like Aristoxenus</note> were neither able to approach the divisions according to reason by themselves, nor did they deign to discover those revealed by the senses.

<margin>
* [The total is] 672; specifically, the differences from the leading [notes] are 576, 567, and 560; and of the third [notes], 640, 648, and 630. As the diagrams show. That the proposed divisions of the genera are not only reasonable but also consistent with the senses; it is possible to understand this again from the eight-stringed rule containing the octave, the notes being made accurate, as we said, according to the uniformity of the strings and the equality of tension. For by the divisions made of the rules, following the ratios of each genus, and with the underlying bridges being set accordingly, the octave will be so tuned that not even the most musical would be confused; but let us admire nature in the arrangement of the tuning; on the one hand, reason fashions and shapes the differences that preserve the melody; on the other, hearing obeys reason as much as possible, being so disposed because of the order from it, and recognizing the appropriate quality in each of the fitting parts. But those who have failed in this part have been condemned, being neither able to hit upon the reasonable divisions by themselves, nor having deigned to accept those shown by the senses.

# Chapter 16. HARMONICS BOOK I.

## 15'.
## How many <gloss>a</gloss> are the genera most familiar to the hearing <gloss>b</gloss>, and which ones are they.

INDEED, of the genera already set forth <gloss>c</gloss>, we find that all the Diatonic <gloss>d</gloss> types are familiar to the ear; but this is no longer the case for either the Enharmonic or the "soft" <term>Chromatic</term> <gloss>e</gloss> <note>The Chromatic genus uses smaller intervals than the Diatonic, often sounding "colored" or "shaded" to ancient ears.</note>. This is because the ears do not at all <gloss>f</gloss> delight in modes that are excessively limp or dissolved in character <gloss>g</gloss>; rather, it is sufficient <gloss>h</gloss> for them, in the transition toward the soft <gloss>i</gloss>, to reach as far as the "intense" <term>Chroma</term> <gloss>k</gloss>. For the "density" <note>original: pyknon; a structural grouping of the two smallest intervals in a tetrachord</note> by which the nature of the "soft" is distinguished from the "intense" marks the limit of the genus: beginning from here on the path toward the softer, and ending <gloss>l</gloss> here again on the path toward the more intense.

## CHAPTER XVI.
## How many genera are more agreeable to the ears, and which ones they are.

BUT, of the genera already explained; we find by experience that all the Diatonic ones are agreeable to the ears: but not equally so the Enharmonic, or the soft Chromatic; because the ears are not greatly delighted by modes that are very loose: since they consider it enough, in proceeding toward the soft, to at least arrive at the Intense Chroma. For the "density" (by which the nature of the Soft and the Intense is, as it were, distinguished) is bounded in this genus. Beginning from here, in the transition to the softer; and likewise ending there, in the transition to the more intense. Furthermore, as for the—

->[Mathematical Diagram]<-
<note>This diagram shows the division of a perfect fourth (4:3 ratio) into two smaller ratios. The top bracket represents the whole interval (4/3). It is divided into a 7/6 ratio (sesquisexta) and an 8/7 ratio (sesquiseptima). The calculation at the bottom proves that 7/6 multiplied by 8/7 equals 8/6, which simplifies to 4/3.</note>

—division of the <gloss>m</gloss> whole tetrachord into two ratios, it is taken, in this genus, from those ratios which most nearly approach equality and are next to one another; namely, the sesquisexta <note>the ratio 7:6</note> and the sesquiseptima <note>the ratio 8:7</note>; <gloss>n</gloss> which divide the whole excess of the outer notes nearly in two. <gloss>o</gloss> Therefore, for the reasons stated, this division appears most useful to the hearing; and it suggests to us yet another genus: for those hastening from that melody <gloss>p</gloss> established <gloss>q</gloss> according to equalities, and considering <gloss>r</gloss> if there might be any pleasing composition of the fourth <note>original: dia-tessaron</note> by dividing it from the start into three ratios that are as nearly equal as possible, with equal differences. For such a genus is composed of the sesquinona <gloss>s</gloss> ratio <note>10:9</note>, the sesquidecima <gloss>t</gloss> <note>11:10</note>, and the sesquiundecima <gloss>u</gloss> <note>12:11</note>; the first numbers <gloss>v</gloss> indicating the fourth <note>the ratio 4:3</note> having been tripled in the same way, producing the numbers <gloss>x</gloss> 9, 10, 11, and 12 in continuous sequence...

<summary>This page discusses the auditory preference for Diatonic and "Intense" Chromatic scales over softer, more "dissolved" musical genera. It provides a mathematical proof for dividing a perfect fourth into nearly equal ratios (7:6 and 8:7, or 10:9, 11:10, and 12:11) to create pleasing musical intervals.</summary>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 16.

->The Even Diatonic<-
<note>original: "Διάτονον ὁμαλόν" / "Diatonum Æquabile". This refers to a tuning system where the intervals are made as equal as possible using arithmetic ratios.</note>

<image-desc>
A musical diagram representing the "Even Diatonic." It illustrates ratios within a tetrachord (a four-note scale segment) and a fifth (Dia-pente).
- A group of brackets covers the numerical progression 9, 10, 11, 12. Above these are the Greek markers for "on the 9th" (1 1/9), "on the 10th" (1 1/10), and "on the 11th" (1 1/11). A bracket spanning 9 to 12 is labeled "epitritos" (the 4:3 ratio) and "1 1/3".
- A larger bracket spanning 8 to 12 includes a leading ratio of 1 1/8 (epogdoos). This larger span is labeled "Dia-pente" (a perfect fifth), "hemiolion" (the 3:2 ratio), and "1 1/2".
- Mathematical equations are shown below: 10/9 x 11/10 x 12/11 = 4/3 and 9/8 x 10/9 x 11/10 x 12/11 = 3/2.
</image-desc>

...continue following those already set forth. And with the larger ratios placed first here in the same way, a tetrachord is produced that is not unlike the Intense Diatonic <note>The "Intense Diatonic" was the standard tuning most similar to our modern major scale</note>, yet it is even more "even" than that one: both by itself, and even more so if the Fifth <term>Dia-pente: a perfect fifth</term> is completed. Indeed, if the Disjunction <insert>or Disjunctive Tone</insert> <note>The whole tone used to separate two tetrachords to form an octave</note> making a 9:8 ratio <term>sesquioctavam: a ratio of 1 1/8</term> is joined to the preceding sound, it will effect that particular quality of equality, no longer through only three increases, but through four; with the ratios contained in continuous succession from the 9:8 ratio to the

the successive ratios [of] those called out. With the larger ratios placed first here as well, a tetrachord is produced, not unlike the Intense Diatonic, but even more so in its own character of equality, and also in its completion. For the disjunction <margin>d. Disjunction is that disjunctive tone which, placed between two disjoined Fourths, completes (with them) the Octave.</margin> joined to the leading sound, holding the place of the middle ratio, no longer works the character of equality around only the three excesses, but also upon the fourth, being contained by the successive ratios from the 9:8 ratio <term>epogdoos</term> up to the 11th—

->The Octave<-
<note>original: "Διπλάσιον" / "Dia-pason". Literally "through all [strings]," meaning the full octave scale.</note>

<image-desc>
A complex diagram of the "Octave" scale across the numerical values 18, 20, 22, 24, 27, 30, 33, 36. 
- The span from 18 to 24 and 27 to 36 is marked as "Dia-tessaron" (a perfect fourth) with sub-ratios 1 1/9, 1 1/10, 1 1/11.
- Between 24 and 27 is the "Tone" (1 1/8 ratio).
- The span from 18 to 27 is marked "Dia-pente" (a perfect fifth) with a ratio of 1 1/2.
- The entire span from 18 to 36 is labeled "Dia-pason" and "Double" (2:1 ratio).
- A mathematical proof is provided at the bottom: 10/9 x 11/10 x 12/11 (= 4/3) x 9/8 x 10/9 x 11/10 x 12/11 (= 4/3) = 2/1.
</image-desc>

—eleventh. Therefore, these first numbers—18, 20, 22, 24, 27, 30, 33, 36—produce an Octave of this kind, with the disjunction placed in the middle. If the division of equal-toned strings <margin>* Grammatical rules regarding accents teach that Genitive Plurals of the second declension have a circumflex on the last syllable... but all the manuscript codices from which we publish these have the common consensus of [the other form], which I therefore follow. Since this occurs often, let this be a single warning.</margin> is taken according to these numbers, a character will appear that is somewhat more strange and, as it were, rustic; but otherwise quite pleasing, and even more adapted to the ears, so that it by no means deserves to be held in contempt.

—tenth. Therefore, the first of the numbers, 18, 20, 22, 24, 27, 30, 33, 36, produce such an octave, with the disjunction placed in the middle. When the division through them is taken, upon the equal-tones, a character somewhat more foreign and rustic will be manifest, but otherwise pleasing, and more practiced by the ears; so that it should not be rightly overlooked.

<margin>

...partly because the melody <gloss>a</gloss> is unique <gloss>b</gloss>, and partly because of its fixed <gloss>c</gloss> division; for <gloss>d</gloss> even if it is sung by itself, it causes no <gloss>e</gloss> offense to the <gloss>f</gloss> sense of hearing. This <gloss>g</gloss> is a quality that belongs almost <gloss>h</gloss> exclusively to the Middle of the Diatonics; whereas the others, though by <gloss>i</gloss> themselves they are joined <gloss>j</gloss> with some violence <note>original: βίᾳ, implying a forced or unnatural tuning</note>, are able to proceed when <gloss>k</gloss> mixed with the aforementioned Diatonic. This occurs when the softer parts of it are taken according to the lower <gloss>l</gloss> tetrachords of the Disjunctions, and the <gloss>m</gloss> more intense parts according to the higher ones. Let this <gloss>n</gloss> genus be called the **Equal Diatonic** <gloss>o</gloss>, named after <gloss>p</gloss> its accidental property.

Regarding the <gloss>q</gloss> distinction of the <gloss>r</gloss> other familiar tunings: the <gloss>s</gloss> Middle <gloss>t</gloss> Tonic <gloss>u</gloss> Diatonic, when examined by itself and unmixed, will harmonize <gloss>v</gloss> with the "Solid" tunings on the lyra, and on the cithara with the <gloss>w</gloss> tunings of the *Tritae* and <gloss>x</gloss> *Hyperteropa* <note>technical names for specific string positions or modes</note>. The <gloss>y</gloss> aforementioned <gloss>z</gloss> mixture of the Intense Chromatic with it corresponds to the "Soft" tunings on the lyra, and the "Tropical" <note>modes involving "turning" or change</note> on the cithara <gloss>a</gloss>. The <gloss>b</gloss> mixture of the Soft Diatonic with the Tonic corresponds to the "Mutable" characters <gloss>c</gloss> on the cithara's *Parypate* <note>the "next-to-lowest" string</note>. The <gloss>d</gloss> mixture of the Intense Diatonic with the Tonic corresponds to the "Mutable" characters which the cithara-players call **Lydian** and **Iastian** <gloss>e</gloss> <note>also known as Ionian</note>.

Furthermore, following the demonstrated <gloss>f</gloss> Intense Diatonic, just as one might <gloss>g</gloss> observe from a <gloss>h</gloss> comparison of its <gloss>i</gloss> proper ratios, they have introduced <gloss>k</gloss> another <gloss>l</gloss> genus, which is nearby but <gloss>m</gloss> otherwise ready at hand. It makes <gloss>n</gloss> the two leading intervals <gloss>o</gloss> into what they themselves consider a semitone; but as Reason <note>mathematical logic</note> proves, it is actually what is called the **Limma** <note>original: λεῖμμα, the "remnant" or Pythagorean semitone</note>. This <gloss>p</gloss> serves their purpose well enough, because there is no significant difference <gloss>m</gloss> between <gloss>r</gloss> the sesquioctave ratio <note>9:8, a whole tone</note> and the sesquinona <note>10:9, a minor whole tone</note> in the leading positions, nor <gloss>t</gloss> in the following positions between the sesquidecima-fifth <note>16:15</note> and the Limma <note>256:243</note>.

For if we take the number 72 <gloss>u</gloss>, the sesquinona and <gloss>v</gloss> sesquioctave <gloss>w</gloss> applied <gloss>x</gloss> to it will produce <gloss>y</gloss> the <gloss>z</gloss> number 81 in the latter case, and <gloss>a</gloss> the number 80 <gloss>b</gloss> in the former. Thus the sesquioctave is the <gloss>d</gloss> **sesquioctogefimus** <note>81:80 ratio, also known as the Syntonic Comma</note> of the sesquinona. This is the same ratio as that of the **Ditone** <gloss>f</gloss> (that is, two sesquioctaves) to the sesquiquarta <note>5:4, a major third</note>, which is the leader <gloss>h</gloss> in the Enharmonic genus. For relative to the number 64, the sesquiquarta makes 80, while the <gloss>i</gloss> double sesquioctave <gloss>k</gloss> makes <gloss>l</gloss> 81. Similarly <gloss>y</gloss>, since the ratio of the Limma is 256 to <gloss>z</gloss> 243, and the <gloss>a</gloss> sesquidecima-fifth of 243 <gloss>b</gloss> is approximately 259;

It provides a mathematical proof using the number 72 to demonstrate the slight difference between various musical intervals like the sesquioctave and sesquinona.</summary>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 16.

very nearly; the ratio of the <term>sesquidecimiquinta</term><note>The ratio 16:15</note> to the <term>Limma</term><note>The "remainder" or Pythagorean semitone, 256:243</note> will be that which is 259 to 256 very nearly; and it is altogether the same as the <term>sesquioctogesima</term><note>The ratio 81:80, also known as the Syntonic Comma</note>. And this happens because the <term>sesquiquarta</term> ratio <note>5:4, the major third</note> is equal to both the <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>9:8, the whole tone</note> and the <term>sesquinona</term> <note>10:9, the minor tone</note> combined (that is, $\frac{5}{4} = \frac{9}{8} \times \frac{10}{9}$). Therefore, in neither of the genres set forth does any notable offense occur if they are used interchangeably; for instance, in the <term>Intense Diatonic</term>, using the 9:8 ratio instead of the 10:9 in the preceding position, and the Limma instead of the 16:15 in the following position: and, in the <term>Enharmonic Genus</term>, using two 9:8 ratios instead of the 5:4 in the preceding position, and the Limma likewise instead of the 16:15 in both the following ratios combined. Likewise, let us also assume this genus: both because of the ease of transitions from the <term>tonal genus</term> to a mixture with it; and because the ratio of the Limma has a certain peculiar relationship to the <term>Fourth</term> <note>Diatessaron</note> and the <term>Tone</term>, above others which are not <term>superparticular</term><note>A ratio where the numerator is one greater than the denominator, like 9:8</note>; as that which necessarily follows from two 9:8 ratios falling within a 4:3 ratio. Moreover, the Limma can be taken both by itself and through Consonances, just like the Tone. The Tone is found from the excess of the first two consonances <note>the difference between a Fifth and a Fourth</note>; but the Limma from the difference of the <term>Ditone</term> <note>two major tones</note> from the consonance of the Fourth. Therefore, these first numbers produce this genus: 192, 216, 243, and 256. It may aptly be called "Ditonic," because it has the two preceding positions as Tones.

<margin>
<note>original Greek text of the passage above</note>
...15th is 259, nearly; there will be a ratio of the 16:15 to the limma, that of 259 to 256 nearly; which is entirely the same as the 81:80. This is because the 5:4 ratio is equal to the 9:8 and 10:9 combined. Therefore in neither of our set genres does any notable discord arise for those using them... in the intense diatonic... and in the enharmonic... let this genus be assumed by us... because of the readiness of the change... and because the ratio of the limma has a certain kinship to the fourth... it follows by necessity from two 9:8 ratios falling into the 4:3... the numbers are 192, 216, 243, and 256. It would reasonably be called "ditonic" because it has the leading two ratios as tones.
</margin>

## Ditonic Diatonic.
## Diatonum ditonicum.
## Ditone.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY'S HARMONICS, Chapter 16.

->**The Ditonic Diatonic. <note>original: "Διάτονον διτονιαῖον." This is the "Pythagorean" tuning of the diatonic scale, characterized by two equal whole tones and a small remainder.</note>**<-

<meta>This page begins Book II of Ptolemy’s Harmonics. It follows the conclusion of Book I, which established the mathematical ratios of intervals. Here, Ptolemy shifts from purely theoretical derivation to an empirical approach based on sensory perception.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
## HARMONICS
->BOOK TWO OF THREE.<-

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY
## HARMONICS
### THE SECOND BOOK (OUT OF THREE).

---

1. CHAPTER I.

How the ratios of our familiar musical scales may be grasped even through the sense of hearing.
->In what manner the Ratios of the Accustomed Genera are perceived by the Senses.<-

We might also grasp in another way the same symmetries of the scales that are familiar and easy for our hearing to handle. We shall not do as we did just now, deriving their differences from a logical principle <note>original: "tou eulogou" / "rationis normam"</note> and then bringing forward evidence from the observed phenomena by means of the monochord <note>term: Canon/Kanon; a single-stringed instrument used to measure mathematical intervals</note>. Instead, we shall do the reverse: first setting out how we organize tunings <note>original: "harmonias"</note> through the sense of hearing alone, and then demonstrating from those tunings the resulting ratios that are perceived in each genus <note>term: Genus; a classification of the four-stringed scale (tetrachord) based on the spacing of the inner notes</note> by the equalities or excesses of the sounds. Here too, we assume only those things agreed upon by everyone: that the concord of the fourth <note>term: Dia-tessaron; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> contains the sesquitertial ratio <note>note: 4:3 ratio</note>, <margin>b</margin> and that the whole tone <note>term: Tonus</note> is sesquioctave <note>note: 9:8 ratio</note>. Now, of the tetrachords played by the cithara players <note>term: Citharœdi; professional singers who accompanied themselves on the kithara</note>, let us first take the one from the *Nete* to the *Paramese* <note>note: These are the names of specific strings/notes in the Greek system; Nete is the highest, Paramese is the note below the disjunction</note>, the fourth called the "of the modes" <margin>c</margin> <note>note: Likely referring to a specific tuning style or "tropos"</note>; let it be represented by A B C D, with A assigned to the *Nete*. I say that this produces the tuning of the "Intense Chromatic" <margin>d</margin> genus previously set forth. And first, that the ratio of A to B is sesquisextal <note>note: 7:6 ratio</note>, and that of B to D is sesquiseptimal <note>note: 8:7 ratio</note>. (The ratios of B to C and C to D will be shown after these.) For both A-B and B-D will be found by the ear to produce a size greater than a whole tone; <margin>e</margin> that is, greater than the sesquioctave <margin>f</margin> ratio. And the ratio of the outer notes A to D is sesquitertial <note>4:3</note>.

---

<margin>CAP. I.</margin> 
<a> So most manuscripts; some lack "of the three." O. B. [reads] "His second book of Harmonics." 
<b> CV [manuscript] reads "simply agreed upon by all," which is more correct. 
<c> They seem to be called this because it often happens that the sounds of this Disjunct tetrachord are converted into the sounds of the Conjunct tetrachord (which our musicians do by placing a B-flat in B-fa-B-mi) and vice versa. 
<d> AB [manuscript] reads "sixths." 
<e> AB [manuscript] reads "ratios." 
<f> Numbers.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 1.

There are no other two <term>superparticular ratios</term> <note>original: "superparticulares"; ratios where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, such as 3/2 or 4/3</note> greater than a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>9:8 ratio, a whole tone</note> which, when combined, complete a <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>4:3 ratio, a perfect fourth</note>, except for the <term>sesquisexta</term> <note>7:6 ratio</note> and the <term>sesquiseptima</term> <note>8:7 ratio</note>. Therefore, of the ratios AB and BD, one will be a sesquisexta and the other a sesquiseptima. Now, let a note H be taken that is equal in pitch to B. And let another tetrachord EFGH be constructed (from H toward the higher pitch) similar to the tetrachord ABCD. It will be found that A is higher in pitch than F. Since B and H are equal in pitch, the ratio AB is therefore greater than FH. However, FH is set as equal to BD. Therefore, the ratio AB is greater than BD. Thus, AB will be a sesquisexta (7:6) and BD will be a sesquiseptima (8:7). Furthermore, while the tetrachord ABCD remains, let a note F be taken equal in pitch to B. With this established, let there be constructed from the <term>Paramese</term> <note>the "next-to-middle" note in the Greek system</note> a perfect fourth in the so-called <term>Epichromatic</term> genus of the "Solids" <note>referring to the "Solid" or "Dense" tetrachords</note>: such as EFGH, with E placed at the Paramese. In this, I say, is contained the genus of the **Tonic Diatonic**: namely, that the ratio of EF is a sesquioctave (9:8); and FG is a sesquiseptima (8:7); and GH is a <term>sesquivigesimaseptima</term> <note>28:27 ratio</note>. For EF produces precisely a whole tone; that is, a sesquioctave ratio. And by ear, G will be found to be equal in pitch to D. And so the ratio of FG...

Moreover, there are no other two ratios, greater than the sesquioctave (9:8), which complete the sesquitertia (4:3), except the sesquisexta (7:6) and the sesquiseptima (8:7). So that of the ratios AB and BD, one will be sesquisexta and the other sesquiseptima. Let H be taken equal in pitch to B; and let there be made from it toward the higher pitch a tetrachord EFGH similar to ABCD. Then A will be found higher than E; and since B and H are equal in pitch, the ratio AB is greater than EH. But EH <unclear>will be</unclear> the same as BD; therefore the ratio AB is greater than BD. Thus AB will be sesquisexta and BD sesquiseptima. Again, while the tetrachord ABCD remains, let Z be taken equal in pitch to B; and, with it standing, let there be made from the paramese the **Epichromatic of the Solids** perfect fourth, as EFGH, with E being ordered according to the paramese. I say that it contains the same genus as the **Tonic Diatonic**; and the ratio of EF is sesquioctave; that of FG is sesquiseptima; and that of GH is sesquivigesimaseptima (28:27). For EF produces exactly a tone; that is, a sesquioctave ratio; and Z will be found equal in pitch to D; so that the ratio FG...

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 1.

->**Soft Diatonic**<-

| Left Tetrachord (A-D) | Ratios | Right Tetrachord (E-H) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **A** (α) 101 1/8 | | **E** (ε) 90 |
| | 9/8 (sesquioctava) | |
| **B** (β) 113 3/4 | | **F** (ς) *lichanos* 102 6/7 |
| | 21/20 (sesquivigesima) <note>The diagram notes 1 1/21, but the text clarifies the ratios below.</note> | |
| **C** (γ) 117 5/12 | | **G** (ζ) *parhypate* 114 2/7 |
| | 28/27? <note>Original: 256/243 marked near segment</note> | |
| **D** (δ) 120 | | **H** (η) *hypate* 120 |

<column-break/>

it remains, however, that the ratio of **FG** is a *sesquinona* <note>9:8 ratio</note>; and **GH** a *sesquivigesima* <note>21:20 ratio</note>. Now therefore, it will be perceived (by *ear*) that **C** is slightly sharper than **G**: and thus the ratio of **GH** will be smaller than **CD**; that is, smaller than a *sesquidecimaoctava* <note>19:18 ratio</note>. Therefore the ratio of **FH** is a *sesquioctava* <note>9:8</note>. And so the ratio of these **FG** will be smaller than a *sesquioctava*. And the ratio of **FH** is a *sesquisexta* <note>7:6 ratio</note>; (since **EF** is a *sesquiseptima* <note>8:7 ratio</note>.) Moreover, no other two (*superparticular*) ratios <note>Ratios in the form (n+1)/n</note> complete a *sesquisexta* where one is smaller than a *sesquioctava* and the other smaller than a *sesquidecimaoctava*, except for the *sesquinona* <note>10:9</note> and the *sesquivigesima* <note>21:20</note>. And **GH** is smaller than a *sesquidecimaoctava*. This, therefore, will be the *sesquivigesima*: and the remaining **FG** the *sesquinona*. Finally; while the **EFGH** tetrachord remains, let **C** be made equal in tone to **G**: and, with this standing, let the **ABCD** *Diatessaron* <note>An interval of a fourth</note> be fitted, of the first-placed Chromatic genus, with **A** likewise placed at the sharpest point; so that the ratio of **BD** is a *sesquiseptima*. It must be shown that the ratio of **BC** is a *sesquiundecima* <note>12:11</note>, and **CD** a *sesquivigesima-prima* <note>22:21</note>. It will certainly be perceived (by *ear*) that **D** is a little sharper than **H**; and thus the ratio of **CD** will be smaller than **GH**, that is, smaller than a *sesquivigesima*. But **B** is sensibly deeper <note>lower in pitch</note> than **F**; and thus the ratio **BC** will be smaller than the ratio **FG**; that is, than a *sesquinona*. No other *two* ratios complete a *sesquiseptima* of which one is smaller than a *sesquinona* and the other smaller than a *sesquivigesima*, except the *sesquiundecima* and the *sesquivigesimaprima*. And the ratio of **CD** is smaller than a *sesquivigesima*. This, therefore, is the *sesquivigesimaprima*: and thus the remaining **BC** is the *sesquiundecima*. Which was the thing to be demonstrated.

<note>The following is the original Greek text of the passage translated above.</note>
It must be shown, then, that the ratio of ςζ becomes 9:8, and that of ζη 21:20... <note>The Greek text mirrors the Latin/English proof regarding the division of the tetrachord into specific mathematical ratios.</note>

# HARMONICS BOOK II.

<margin>Chapter 1.</margin>

->2.<-
->On the use <gloss>a</gloss> of the rule according to the instrument called the Helicon.<-

->CHAPTER II.<-
->On the use of the Rule according to the instrument called the Helicon.<-

**T**he differences regarding the <term>tetrachords</term> <note>A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth.</note> according to their genera have thus been established by us in these ways, through the examination and comparison of unequal-toned sounds. However, the application of the <term>diapason</term> <note>The interval of an octave.</note> to the eight-stringed rule might also occur in another way, according to the instrument called the **Helicon**. This was constructed by students of the mathematical sciences to demonstrate the ratios found in musical harmonies in a visible way. <gloss>d</gloss> For let a square be set out, such as **ABCD** <note>The text uses both Greek letters α β γ δ and Latin A B C D for the diagram points.</note>. And, having bisected lines **AB** and **BD** at points **E** and **F**, let the straight lines **AF** and **BGC** be joined. It is then necessary that a line **EHK** be drawn through point **E** parallel to **AC**, <gloss>e</gloss> and through point **G**, the line **LGM** <gloss>f</gloss>. From this it follows that **AC** is double each of the lines **BF** and **FD**. <gloss>g</gloss> Since **BD** is also double <gloss>h</gloss> each of these, it is four times the length of **EH** <gloss>i</gloss>. Thus **AC** is four times the length of **EH**, while it is in a <term>sesquitertial</term> ratio <note>A ratio of 4:3, representing a perfect fourth.</note> to the remaining part **HK** <gloss>k</gloss>. It is also demonstrated that **MG** <gloss>l</gloss> is double **LG**; since as **DC** is to **CM**, so is **DB** to **GM**; and as **BA** is to **AL**, <gloss>m</gloss> which is again as **DC** is to **CM**, so is **BF** to **LG**. <gloss>n</gloss> Because of this, as **BD** is to **GM**, so is **BF** to **LG**; <gloss>o</gloss> and alternately, as **BD** is to **BF**, so is **MG** to **LG**. Therefore, **AC** is in a <term>sesquialter</term> ratio <note>A ratio of 3:2, representing a perfect fifth.</note> to **GM**, and a triple ratio to **GL**. Thus, if four strings of equal tension are arranged according to these positions, <gloss>p</gloss> namely those of **AC**, **EK**, and **LM**, and...

And with the straight lines BD in their positions: and with the rules <note>original: "canonio"</note> placed beneath them in the position of the straight line FGHA; and with the numbers aptly fitted; namely, 12 for the straight line AC; 9 for HK; 8 for GM; 6 for both BF and FD; likewise 4 for LG; and 3 for EH: All the **Consonances** <note>musical intervals that sound harmonious together</note> and the **Tone** will be produced. Furthermore; according to this very Instrument, if we set out a Parallelogram in any way, such as ABCD; (whether it be a square, or any other parallelogram;) and we understand AB and CD to be at the **String-Ends** <note>original: "Apopsalmata"; the nuts or bridges that define the vibrating length of a string</note> or Finishers (by which the lengths of the strings are bounded on both sides;) and AC BD, at the extreme sounds of the Octave itself: Then, (with DE extended equal to CD itself,) let us divide (in place of the rules) the side CD, according to the ratios proper to each of the genera <note>the different types of musical scales: diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic</note>; with the higher-pitched end placed toward E; and through these sections thus made, let us stretch strings parallel to AC, and equal in tone to one another: and, this finally being done, let us place a common **movable bridge** <note>original: "subductorium"; Greek: "hypagogeus"</note> beneath the strings, in the position of the straight line which joins points A and E; such as AFE: We shall again produce the lengths of the strings in the same ratios; which therefore will receive the test of the ratios
