# Chapter 2. HARMONICS BOOK II.

| | |
|---|---|
| <margin>p. 2.</margin> ...according to the reflection of the ratios <note>original: "λόγων ἀνάκλασιν"</note> for each of the genera <term>genera: the three types of Greek scales—Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic</term>. Since, just as the lines taken from point **E** toward **CD** relate to one another, so also will the lines drawn from their endpoints parallel to **AC** up to **AF** relate to one another. For example, as **EC** is to **ED**, so is **CA** to **DF**. These will therefore produce the octave <note>original: "διὰ πασῶν" / "Dia-pason"</note>, because their ratio is double <note>2:1</note>. If we then further mark off from **CD** the segment **CG**, being a fourth part of **EC**, and **CH**, being a third part of the same; and if we set up strings at **G** and **H**, such as **GKL** and **HMN**, tuned to the same pitch as those originally placed; then **AC** becomes a sesquitertial ratio <note>4:3, a perfect fourth</note> to **GK**, and a sesquialter ratio <note>3:2, a perfect fifth</note> to **HM**. Likewise, **HM** will be sesquitertial to **DF**, and **GK** sesquialter to it; and **GK** will produce a sesquioctave ratio <note>9:8, a whole tone</note> to **HM**. These will also produce harmonies <note>original: "συμφωνίας"</note> with one another consistent with those ratios. A similar result will follow for the middle sections taken between the two tetrachords, according to the proper ratios under examination. Now, the first method, compared to this one, is more convenient in that it does not require changing the distances of the strings from one another. But this second method has an advantage over the first: it uses a common bridge <note>original: "ὑπαγωγεῖα" / "subductoria"</note>, a single one placed in the same position. Furthermore, it can make the entire tone sharper while preserving the specific character of the genus (since, for example, as **CA** is to **FOD**, so is **XC** to **OD**, and similarly for the rest). On the other hand, the first method is more laborious than this second one because it requires moving more bridges for each tuning. This second method, however, requires moving entire strings; and the transitions between systems are accomplished not by equal distances, but by distances that often differ greatly. <margin>similarly also for the others</margin> | Since, as the straight lines (taken from E) on CD relate to each other, so will those drawn from their ends to AF, parallel to AC, relate to each other. For instance, as EC to ED, so CA to DF: which therefore will show the Octave, since they are in a double ratio. If we then take away from CD the part CG, a fourth part of the line EC; and CH, a third part of the same: and at G and H we place strings, as GKL and HMN, of equal tone to those first placed; so that AC becomes sesquitertia to GK, and sesquialtera to HM; and likewise HM sesquitertia to DF, and GK sesquialtera; and likewise GK sesquioctava to HM: These will also make among themselves harmonies agreeable to those ratios. And the same will follow in the middle sections of the two tetrachords, according to the due ratios of the things examined. But that former method, compared to this, has this advantage: it is more ready, because it has no need to change the distances of the strings from each other. This one, over that, has this: that it has a common bridge, a single one, and placed in the same position; and it makes the whole tone sharper, while its generic property remains; (Since, for example, as CA is to FOD, so XC is to OD; and similarly in the rest.) Again, that former method is more troublesome than this second one, because it is necessary to move more bridges in each fitting: but this one over that, because it is necessary to transfer whole strings; and also that the distances of the strings (not equal indeed, but) often differing much, must be passed over in performing the touches. |

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 3.

A dissertation on the System; first, we must define those differences of the primary Consonances which are (as they say) according to **Species**, which are as follows. Species is, in any case, the particular arrangement of the specific (*or characteristic*) ratios within their own boundaries in each Genere <note>Genus/Genera: the three types of ancient Greek scales: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic</note>. And those specific ratios in the **Fifth** <note>original: "Dia-pente"</note> and **Octave** <note>original: "Dia-pason"</note> are the Tonal and disjunctive ones; (*namely, the disjunctive tone:* <note>The whole tone that separates two tetrachords</note>) but in the **Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note>, they are the ratios of the two preceding sounds, which create the distinctions between the softer or more intense forms. (*Namely, in the former, from A to E; in the latter, from G to A.*) Moreover, let us call it the first species in all cases alike when that specific ratio occupies the leading (*that is, the highest*) place; (*for the leading place is surely the first:*) the Second, when it occupies the place second from the leader: the Third, when the third: and similarly in those following. And for that reason, there are as many species in each of the consonances as there are places for the ratios: Specifically, the **Fourth** has three species; the **Fifth**, four; the **Octave**, seven. And in
<margin>In the discussion of systems, one must further define the differences of the primary consonances according to what is called "species," which are as follows. Species is <note>d</note> a certain placement of the specific ratios within their proper limits according to each genus. These would be, in the Fifth and the Octave, the tonal and <note>e</note> disjunctive ones; but in the Fourth, those of the two <note>f</note> semitone sounds which produce the variations toward the softer or more intense. First, then, we call a species "first" generally whenever the specific ratio holds the leading place; because the leading is first. Second, when it holds the second from the leader; or third, when the third; and so on in order. Therefore there are as many species in each as there are places of the ratios: for the Fourth, three; for the Fifth, four; <note>g</note> for the Octave, seven. <note>*</note> And indeed it happens that for the...</margin>

---

->* In modern Music those Species are sung (from low to high) with these voices. I omit the lowest voice (from which one ascends to the high) in all of them, as it is not always fixed: for example, sometimes *mi*, sometimes *fa*, in the Fourth.<-

| sol | la | mi | la | fa | la | sol |

->And the sounds are { Fixed, *la, mi.*<-
->{ Movable, *fa, sol.*<-

For although in our Music, we who use only one single Genus; namely, as it is believed, the *Diatonic Ditonic*; (unless rather, as I would prefer, the *Intense Diatonic*; <note>Ptolemy's preferred tuning for the diatonic scale</note>) *fa* and *sol* are no less fixed than *la* and *mi*; yet in the Music of the Greeks, who varied the Genera (such as the Enharmonic, and various Chromatics and Diatonics), while the sounds we call *la* and *mi* remained, those we call *fa* and *sol* changed their places (according to the various Genera); being sometimes closer to and sometimes further from the standing *la* and *mi*.

---

<margin>
*d* "A certain placement," in the definition of Ratio, and likewise of Genus in Harmony, refers to how the sounds in a Tetrachord relate to one another (or what ratios or relations they have): but "a certain placement," in the definition of Species, refers to how they are situated: for example, which seat the ratio which he calls "Specific" or "Distinctive" occupies in any of the consonances. Moreover, that distinctive ratio is (as he defines it in the following words) that by which (in the high range) the voice *mi* is sung (in modern Music) for the Octave and Fifth; and the voice *la* for the Fourth. These Species will soon be set out. *e* Manuscript AB: "placement" incorrectly. *f* Manuscript AB: "I might say to you." *g* GIO: "indeed the Fourth." *h* GIO: "he says." *i* V. [variant ending].
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals and their species from the previous chapter, focusing on the mathematical and structural relationships between fixed notes.</meta>

### Chapter 3. HARMONICS BOOK II.

In the <term>Dia-tessaron</term> <note>the interval of a fourth</note>, it happens that only one species—specifically the first—is contained by <term>stable sounds</term> <note>original: "ἑστώτων φθόγγων" / "sonis stabilibus"; these are the fixed notes of the tetrachord that do not change regardless of the musical genus</note>. In the <term>Dia-pente</term> <note>the interval of a fifth</note>, there are only two: the first and the fourth. In the <term>Dia-pason</term> <note>the interval of an octave</note>, there are only three: the first, the fourth, and the seventh. 

For if we set out a Dia-tessaron, A B C D, and we understand A to be at the highest pitch <note>original: "ὀξύτατον" / "acutissimo"</note>; and to this we join another Dia-tessaron toward the grave <note>lower pitch</note>, having a similar arrangement, D E F G; and to this similarly a <term>Tone</term> <note>a whole step</note>, G H; and to this again a Dia-tessaron, H K L M; and finally to this another Dia-tessaron, M N X O. The <term>standing tones</term> <note>fixed notes</note> will be A, D, G, H, M, and O. 

Of the Dia-tessaron itself, the first species will be M O; the second, L X; the third, K N. And of these, only one (as is evident) is comprised of stable sounds, namely the first, M O. 

Of the Dia-pente, the first species will be G M; the second, F L; the third, E K; the fourth, D H. And only these among them (as is clear) are comprised of stable sounds: the first, G M, and the fourth, D H. 

Finally, of the Dia-pason, the first species will be G O; the second, F X; the third, E N; the fourth, D M; the fifth, C L; the sixth, B K; the seventh, A H. And likewise, of these, only those comprised of stable sounds are the first, G O; the fourth, D M; and the seventh, A H.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 4.

### CHAPTER IV.

->*Concerning the Perfect System; and that only the Double Octave is such.*<-

->4. Concerning the Perfect System, and that only <gloss>a</gloss> the double octave <note>original: "dis dia pason"</note> is of such a kind.<-

These things having been set forth; a System <note>term: System (Greek: *Systema*). In ancient music theory, this refers to a scale or a collection of intervals combined together.</note> is called, simply, a magnitude composed of Consonances: (just as a Consonance is a magnitude composed of Concinnous <note>term: Concinnous. Intervals that are "fit" or "suitable" for melody, even if they aren't primary consonances.</note> intervals:) and a System is, as it were, a consonance of consonances. Moreover, a Perfect System is said to be that which contains all the consonances, along with each of their own species <note>term: Species. The different ways an interval can be divided; for example, the different "shapes" of an octave depending on where the semitones fall.</note>. (For, universally, that is Perfect which contains all its own parts.) According to the first definition, therefore, the Octave <note>original: "Dia-pason"</note> is a System, (and this indeed was deemed sufficient by the ancients;) likewise the Octave-plus-Fourth <note>original: "Dia-pason & dia-tessaron"</note>; and likewise the Octave-plus-Fifth <note>original: "Dia-pason & dia-pente"</note>; and finally the Double Octave <note>original: "Dis-dia-pason"</note>. (For each of these is fashioned from Consonances; two, or more.) But according to the second definition, only the Double Octave will be a Perfect System. For in this alone are all the Consonances present, with their species set forth. And indeed, those Systems which are larger than it have nothing more beyond those things which are contained, potentially, within it: while those which are smaller than it fall short in some of the things which are within it. Whence it is clear, that the *System* composed of the Octave and the Fourth is not rightly called a Perfect System. For it will never contain the seven species of the Octave itself: and indeed, it does not always contain the four species of the Fifth. But in-

These things having been set forth, a System is called, simply, the magnitude composed of consonances; just as a Consonance is the <gloss>b</gloss> magnitude composed of concinnous intervals; and the system is <gloss>c</gloss> as it were a consonance of consonances. And a perfect system is said to be that which encompasses all the consonances, along with the species of each. Because that is perfect in general which encompasses all <gloss>d</gloss> its own parts. According to the first definition, then, the octave also becomes a system; for this seemed sufficient to the ancients; as well as the octave-plus-fourth, and the octave-plus-fifth, and the double octave. For each <gloss>e</gloss> of them is encompassed by consonances, two or more. But according to the <gloss>f</gloss> second, only the double octave would be a perfect system; for in it alone are all the consonances present, along with the species set out. And those <gloss>g</gloss> beyond it <gloss>f</gloss> would have nothing more than what is taken potentially in that one; while those <gloss>i</gloss> under it would lack something of what is in it. Whence it is not right to call the system composed of the octave and the fourth a perfect system; for it never encompasses the <gloss>k</gloss> seven species of the octave; and the four of the fifth, not always; but when it is so placed, as

-deed, when it obtains a placement of this kind, so that two conjoined tetrachords <note>term: Tetrachord. A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth.</note> are disjoined from a third by a disjunctive tone <note>original: "tono diazeuctico"</note>; it will contain four species of the Fifth: but of the seven of the Octave

<summary>Ptolemy defines a "Perfect System" in music as one that contains all possible consonances and their species, concluding that only the Double Octave (Dis-dia-pason) meets this criteria. He argues that smaller systems, like the Octave-plus-Fourth, are incomplete because they cannot represent every species of the primary intervals.</summary>

# BOOK II OF THE HARMONICS.
<sig>K</sig>

### Chapter 5.

...again four species, which are those closest to either of the two extremes. When, however, it happens to have such a position that the <term>tone</term><note>original: "tonus" or "tonos"; the standard whole-step interval</note> is at the end, and there are three <term>tetrachords</term><note>A scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</note> joined together, it will contain only one single species of either the <term>Dia-pente</term><note>The interval of a perfect fifth</note> or the <term>Dia-pason</term><note>The interval of an octave</note>—namely, either the first or the last of both. This can be seen in the preceding diagram; if one similarly attaches a single tetrachord to either of its ends. But truly, in the <term>Dis-dia-pason</term><note>The interval of a double octave</note>, when two octaves are established in the same direction and in a similar manner (in whatever way the beginning of the <term>disjunctions</term><note>The point where two tetrachords are separated by a whole tone rather than sharing a note</note> occurs), we shall find contained therein all the species of the octave itself, as well as those of the fifth and the fourth; and there is nothing further in those scales that exceed the double octave.

->V.<-
->CHAPTER V.<-

->How the names of the notes are understood in relation to both their position and their function.<-

How it happens, then, that the system of the octave and the fourth is incorrectly added to the double octave itself will be placed before our eyes in what follows. But in that which is truly the **Perfect System**, namely the double octave, which consists of fifteen notes (since one of them is common to both the lower and the higher octave, being the middle of them all), we assign names to them. Sometimes we do this simply because of their **position**, calling a note "higher" or "lower" in a general sense. Specifically, we call the **Mese** <note>original: "Mesen"; literally "the middle one"</note> that common note just mentioned, which joins the two octaves.

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 5.

The Octave <note>original: "Dia-pason"</note>: The **Acquired Note** <note>original: "Proslambanomenon"</note> indeed <margin>the acquired note <note>original: "ho proslambanomenos"</note></margin> is the lowest of all: and the **Last of the Excellent** <note>original: "Neten Hyperbolæon"</note> <margin>and the [last] of the excellent, the highest</margin> is the highest of all. Then, those which follow the Acquired Note toward the high pitch, up to the Middle Note <note>original: "Mesen"</note>; we call them: the **Highest of the highest** <note>original: "Hypaten hypaton"</note> <margin>of the highest</margin>, the **Next-to-the-highest of the highest** <note>original: "Parypaten hypaton"</note> <margin>and the next-to-the-highest of the highest</margin>, the **Index of the highest** <note>original: "Lichanon hypaton"</note> <margin>and the index of the highest</margin>, the **Highest of the middle** <note>original: "Hypaten meson"</note> <margin>and the highest of the middle</margin>, the **Next-to-the-highest of the middle** <note>original: "Parypaten meson"</note> <margin>and the next-to-the-highest of the middle</margin>, and the **Index of the middle** <note>original: "Lichanon meson"</note> <margin>and the index of the middle</margin>: Those, however, which similarly follow the Middle Note up to the Last of the Excellent, are the **Near-middle** <note>original: "Paramesen"</note>, the **Third of the disconnected** <note>original: "Triten diezeugmenon"</note>, the **Next-to-the-last of the disconnected** <note>original: "Paraneten diezeugmenon"</note> <margin>Those after the middle, likewise, up to the last of the excellent: the near-middle, and the third of the disconnected, and the next-to-the-last of the disconnected</margin>.

***

**g** *Proslambanomenos* (Acquired Note) almost always occurs in the masculine gender, as if the word "sound" <note>Greek: "phthongos"</note> or "tone" is understood; but the remaining names—*hypate, parypate, lichanos, mese, paramese, trite, paranete, nete*—are always feminine, as if the word "string" <note>Greek: "chorde"</note> is understood. Indeed, *hypate, parypate, nete, paranete* are in the form of substantives (by apposition), as is clear from the circumflex accent on the plural genitives; (understand the same for *lichanos*: which elsewhere, when taken for a finger, is feminine, with "finger" <note>Greek: "daktylos"</note> understood; but in Music, it is always feminine, with "string" understood). But *mese* and *trite* are in the form of adjectives. Whether this was done by design or by chance, I could hardly say. It is possible that those who added the *Proslambanomenon* to the number of strings (much later than most of the others) did not pay enough attention to the names previously given; and so (looking at the newly acquired sound) they attached a masculine name to it. However it may be, since the Ancients spoke thus, we follow them. **h** *Nete*, which is also sometimes written *nate*, is the *newest* or *last* (of that particular tetrachord), or even the *lowest* (in physical position on the instrument); thus *nete synemmenon*, *nete diezeugmenon*, *nete hyperbolæon*; to which *hypate* ("highest") is opposed. **i** <gloss>between</gloss> **k** *Hypate* means "highest." The Ancients seem (contrary to what we do, who consider High pitch as "top" and Low pitch as "bottom") to have placed the Low pitch at the top and the High pitch at the bottom. Thus they called the lowest string of a tetrachord *hypate* (as if the "highest" string of that group), such as the *hypate hypaton* and *hypate meson*. When they only had three tetrachords joined together, they called the highest *nete* (the "last" or "bottom-most"). Later, when they began to interpose a "disjunctive tone," they called this "last" tetrachord, now separated from the others, the *nete diezeugmenon* (of the disconnected) to distinguish it from the *nete synemmenon* (of the connected). Finally, adding yet another tetrachord toward the high pitch, they called this the *nete hyperbolæon* (of the excellent). **l** *Parypate* means "next to the highest" or "sub-highest"; just as *paranete* is "next to the last" or "penultimate." Others (which I approve of less) translate *hypate* and *parypate* as "principal" and "near-principal." **m** *Lichanos* means "index [finger]"; the second sound in a tetrachord from the highest, or third from the lowest: so called (I believe) because from this string an "indication" <note>term: "indicium" - a sign or marker</note> is taken regarding the Genre (Diatonic, Chromatic, or Enharmonic), whether it is "Tense" or "Soft." The closer this sound is to the highest, the more "Tense" the genre; the further away, the "Softer" (that is, lower) the sound. Ptolemy hinted at this in Chapter 3. **n** <margin>The text mistakenly reads *parypate* here.</margin> **o** *Trite* means "third"; namely, from the highest string of that tetrachord, or *Nete*. It serves the same function as *parypate* does elsewhere. If one wonders why the names vary across tetrachords: I judge the occasion was this; in individual tetrachords, the lowest was called *hypate* and the highest *nete*. But in connected tetrachords, where the highest of one was the same as the lowest of the next (e.g., the *nete* of the highest was the same as the *hypate* of the middle), they didn't want one string to have two names. They kept *hypate meson* and dropped *nete hypaton*. Because they dropped the "last" name, they also had to drop the "next-to-last" (*parypate*) name and used *lichanos* instead. Conversely, in the higher tetrachords, they preferred to keep the name *nete* ("last"), so they used "third" (*trite*) and "next-to-last" (*paranete*) to avoid confusion. **p** <margin>Manuscripts A, B, G, I, O omit the letter 'e'.</margin>

<summary>This page provides a detailed etymological and structural breakdown of the names of the strings in the Greek musical system, explaining why certain notes are named "highest" or "index" based on their position and function within tetrachords.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, music theory, tetrachord, Hypate, Nete, Lichanon, Proslambanomenos, Greek pitch notation, etymology of musical terms</keywords>

# Chapter 5. HARMONICS BOOK II.

...and the *nete diezeugmenon* <note>the last of the disjoined notes</note>, and the *trite hyperbolaeon* <note>the third of the extreme notes</note>, and the *paranete hyperbolaeon* <note>the penultimate of the extreme notes</note> ^α^. Sometimes, however, according to their **power** <note>Greek: *dynamis*; the functional role of a note in a scale</note>, or their relative state ^β^—that is, how they relate to one another—we apply these names to the positions they occupy in what is called the **Immutable System** of the double octave <note>Greek: *dis-dia-pason*</note>. Then, having made the designations of both positions and powers common to it (as, for example, in the Dorian mode), we transfer these same powers to other positions.

For indeed, of the two disjunctive tones <note>the whole tones that separate tetrachords</note> in the double octave, if we take one away from the note that is the *mese* <note>middle note</note> by position ^δ^ and add to it, on either side, two sets of joined tetrachords ^u^ (making four in total), and finally add the other of those two tones to the remaining and lowest ^y^ interval ^z^: we then call the lower note of the higher disjunction the *mese* by power ^a^ ^b^, and the higher note the *paramese*. Furthermore, we call the lower note ^θ^ of the lower disjunction the *proslambanomenos* <note>the "added note" at the bottom of the scale</note> (which is also the *nete hyperbolaeon*), and the higher note the *hypate hypaton* ^r^.

Next, we call the note common to the two lower joined tetrachords—specifically those following the lower disjunction ^c^—the *hypate meson*. We call the note common to the two higher joined tetrachords—those following the higher disjunction ^d^—the *nete diezeugmenon*. Again, we call the note that is second from the lowest of the tetrachord immediately following the lower disjunction the *parypate hypaton* ^e^, and the third note from it the *lichanos hypaton* ^f^. Likewise, the *parypate meson* is the second from the lowest of the tetrachord preceding the higher disjunction, and the *lichanos meson* is the third ^g^.

Then, the *trite diezeugmenon* is the second from the lowest of the tetrachord following the higher disjunction, and the *paranete diezeugmenon* is the third. Similarly, the *trite hyperbolaeon* is the second from the lowest of the tetrachord preceding the lower disjunction (namely, that which lies next to the *nete hyperbolaeon* or *proslambanomenos*), and the *paranete hyperbolaeon* is the third from it. And indeed, according to these names of the **powers** alone, the notes may be properly called—during the changing of the genera—some "standing" <note>fixed notes that do not change pitch when the scale genus changes from Diatonic to Chromatic or Enharmonic</note>, while others...

<margin>
α <gloss>Note: "hyperbolon" is a mistake for "hyperbolaeon." The tetrachord of the *hyperbolaeon* is so called as if it were "Extravagant" or "exceeding the limits." Since there were previously three tetrachords (the highest, middle, and lowest), and this last tetrachord was sometimes joined to the others and sometimes separated by a disjunctive tone, this additional tetrachord added outside the last ones is rightly called "Extravagant" or "Exceeding." Or even (taking this excess in a positive sense) as "Excellent" or "Surpassing," in which sense I see it understood by Latin writers. It appears that before this tetrachord was added (for they are not all from the same era), the way of speaking began to change; so that what was previously said of the Low was now said of the High (and thus this highest of all tetrachords was called "surpassing" or "super-eminent"). This is even clearer from the names of the Tones to be handed down in Chapter 7, etc. Yet the same authors render *hypaton* as "principal," as if this were a mark of Dignity, not of Location; so that they held now the Low, now the High, to be more worthy.</gloss>
r <gloss>GIO: "or again hypatolaion" is added, which is missing in AB V; in C it is struck out as redundant, since a name had already been given to this string above.</gloss>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 5.

namely, the <term>Proslambanomenos</term><note>The "acquired" or lowest note of the Greek scale.</note>, the <term>Hypate hypaton</term><note>The "highest of the highest" (referring to the highest string on the lyre, which produced the lowest pitch).</note>, the <term>Hypate meson</term><note>The "highest of the middle" notes.</note>, the <term>Mese</term><note>The "middle" note, the central anchor of the system.</note>, the <term>Paramese</term><note>The note "next to the middle."</note>, the <term>Nete diezeugmenon</term><note>The "lowest" (highest pitch) of the disjoined tetrachord.</note>, and the <term>Nete hyperbolaeon</term><note>The "lowest" (highest pitch) of the extreme notes.</note>; (which is one and the same in quality with the Proslambanomenos:) the rest are **Movable**. For when the **Functions** <note>original: "Potestatibus" / "δυνάμεων" — referring to the musical role a note plays regardless of its absolute pitch.</note> are transferred by position, the boundaries of the Fixed and Movable notes no longer coincide with the same places as before. It is evident, moreover, that the first species of the <term>Octave</term><note>original: "Dia-pason"</note> (in the aforementioned system, called the **Immutable**), for the reasons stated, <note>The following is a Greek repetition of the preceding Latin text:</note> in the proposed system called [the immutable], the acquired note, the highest of the highest, the highest of the middle, the middle, the next-to-middle, the lowest of the disjoined, and the lowest of the extreme—being one and the same as the acquired note— <margin>h</margin> are fixed; but the others are **movable**. For when the functions are transferred by position, the boundaries of the fixed or <margin>i</margin> movable notes no longer fit the same places. It is clear, then, that the first species of the octave, in the proposed system called "immutable" for the reason mentioned,

The assigned letters **α α**, indicate (in the natural position, which is the same as the **Dorian Mode**) the first species of the Octave; **β β**, the second; **γ γ**, the third; etc. These letters (in the Manuscript Codices) are bound to each other by interlocking semicircular arcs. I have added, in the margin, the names of the strings as they are commonly called nowadays <note>Referring to the 17th-century Guidonian hexachord system like "A re", "B mi", etc.</note>.

Our musicians indicate this System by omitting the **B-flat** in the "b fa ♮ mi" <note>The author is comparing the Greek system to the contemporary "natural" scale without accidentals.</note>. Which we sing with these voices: *la, mi fa sol la fa sol la, mi fa sol la fa sol la.*

<note>The image shows a 15-note scale using square notation, mapping the ancient Greek notes to the solfège syllables mentioned above, with Greek letters underneath to mark the intervals.</note>

<margin>b GIVO.</margin> <note>Likely a reference to a specific manuscript or editor.</note> **h. i** These are terms or attributes. Specifically, he means this: When, for example, the **Mese** by function (which in the Dorian Mode is also the Mese by position) is moved, say, to the position of the **Paramese** (as occurs in the Phrygian Mode); then that which is the Mese by position becomes the **Lichanos Meson** by function, and thus a movable sound; and similarly for the rest. Thus, in this matter, one should not look at what position each string holds, but what function it serves; by which it is determined whether it should be called fixed or movable.

...they encompass: first, the *Paramese* <note>the note above the middle</note> and the *Hypate hypaton* <note>the lowest note of the lowest tetrachord</note>; the second, the *Trite diezeugmenon* <insert>k</insert> and the *Parypate hypaton*; the third, the *Paranete diezeugmenon* and the *Lichanos hypaton*; the fourth, the *Nete diezeugmenon* and the *Hypate meson*; the fifth, the *Trite hyperbolaeon* and the *Parypate meson*; the sixth, the *Paranete hyperbolaeon* and the *Lichanos meson*; the <insert>m</insert> seventh, the *Nete hyperbolaeon* <insert>n</insert> or the *Proslambanomenos* <insert>o</insert> and the *Mese*. ## 6.
## How the Conjunct magnitude, formed from the octave and the fourth, obtained the reputation of a Perfect System.

## CHAPTER VI.
## In what manner the Conjunct magnitude, from the Octave and the Fourth, acquired the status of a Perfect System.

This system just described is also called "Disjunct," to distinguish it from the one taken according to the magnitude composed of the octave <term>Dia-pason</term> and the fourth <term>dia-tessaron</term>, which is called the "Conjunct" system <insert>b</insert>. It is so named because, instead of a disjunction <note>the whole-tone gap between the middle and the disjoined tetrachords</note>, it has another tetrachord joined to the *Mese* <note>the central note</note> toward the high pitch. This is itself called the "Conjunct" <term>synemmenon</term> tetrachord, named after this accidental feature, just as the "Disjunct" is. In this system, they <unclear>name</unclear> the note after the *Mese* <insert>c</insert> the *Trite synemmenon* (the third of the conjuncts); the next one, the *Paranete synemmenon* (the penultimate of the conjuncts); and the leader of the tetrachord, the innermost one, the *Nete synemmenon* (the last of the conjuncts). 

It seems, however, that this system was devised by the ancients for a different kind <insert>d</insert> of modulation <term>metabole</term>, so that it is a "Modulating" system compared to that "Immutable" one. For that system is not called "Immutable" <insert>e</insert> because it does not change according to genus—since that is common to all our systems—but <insert>f</insert> because of the power of the tone or pitch. There are two primary differences in modulations according to <insert>g</insert> what is called the "Tone": The first is that by which the whole melody is shifted to a sharper tension (pitch) or, conversely, to a deeper one, while maintaining the continuous sequence of the species throughout. The second is that by which the whole melody is not changed in tension, but only some parts <insert>h</insert> of it, departing from the original sequence. Therefore, this might be called a modulation of the melody <term>concentus</term> rather than a modulation of the tone. According to...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical systems and transitions (modulations) in the works of Claudius Ptolemy, specifically contrasting the "Immutatum" (unchanged) system with the "Conjunctum" (conjoined) system.</meta>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 6.

According to the former <note>referring to a simple change in pitch</note>, the essential character of the Melody is not changed, but rather the Pitch of the whole: but according to the latter <note>referring to modulation</note>, the Melody is indeed diverted from its own proper order; and the Tension <note>the stretching or tuning of the strings</note> is *varied*, not merely as tension, but for the sake of the Melody itself. And therefore, that former type does not suggest to the senses an imagination of diversity according to Function <note>original: "Potestatem" / "δύναμιν", referring to the internal relationship of notes</note>, by which the Character of the song might be moved; but only according to the Sharpness or Heaviness of the *whole*: This latter type, however, causes the sense to be struck, as if falling away from the accustomed and expected melody; when the proper course has proceeded for a great part, but then passes into another species; whether according to Genus <note>the division of the tetrachord, e.g., Diatonic or Chromatic</note>, or according to Tension. For example: when, from a continuous Diatonic, the genus declines somewhere into the Chromatic: Or, when from a melody which is accustomed to make transitions to the sounds of the Fifth <note>original: "Dia-pente"</note>...

<note>The following is the Greek source text for the passage above</note>
...for in that case, the melody is not changed, but the whole pitch; but according to this, the melody is turned aside from its proper arrangement; and the tension, not as tension, but as for the sake of the melody; whence, that former one does not produce in the senses a fantasy of difference according to the function, by which the character is moved, but only of that according to the sharper or heavier; but this one, as if to strike it, makes it leave the accustomed and expected melody, when the sequence has been continued for some time, but then shifts somewhere toward another species, either according to the genus, or according to the tension; as, when from a continuous Diatonic, the genus somewhere declines toward the chromatic; or when from a melody upon the concords of the fifth.

->*Systema Conjunctum.*<-

Our musicians indicate this System by placing a ♭ flat on *b fa b mi*; but not likewise on B *mi*. That is (in Ptolemy’s phrase) after the first eight notes have been sung (as in the first system) in the Dorian mode (*la, mi fa sol la, fa sol la,*), a transition is made for the three remaining notes (the clef being changed) into the Mixolydian; (so that for the third part one sings *fa sol la*; which, in the previous continuous system, would have been *mi fa sol*.)

<note>The musical notation shows a standard eight-note scale from A to A, followed by a transition to a "conjoined" tetrachord starting with a B-flat, illustrating how the melody shifts its internal structure.</note>

The Disjoined System, which preceded this, he calls <term>ametabolon</term> which I translate as **Unchanged** (or non-mutated) rather than "Immutable" (even though the latter seems to fit the Greek word more closely): He wishes to imply; not indeed that it *cannot* be changed, but that it is not yet changed—a perfect system—into another. But where, a change having been made, it passes into this Conjoined System; it is then called <term>metabolikon</term>, as that which (from a perfect System) **has become changed** into one less perfect.

# Chapter 6. HARMONICS BOOK II.

-> accustomed to making transitions harmoniously, a certain deviation occurs toward the **Fourth** <note>original: *Dia-tessaron*</note> as happens in the systems already set forth. For when the melody ascends to the **Middle** <note>original: *Mese*, the central pitch of the Greek scale</note>, whenever it does not proceed (as was customary) to the **Disjointed** <note>original: *Diezeugmenon*</note> tetrachord—which is at the interval of a **Fifth** <note>original: *Dia-pente*</note> from the **Middle** tetrachord—but instead, being drawn elsewhere, is caught up as if to be **Joined** <note>original: *Synemmenon*</note> to the **Middle** tetrachord; so that, instead of a Fifth, it creates a Fourth in relation to those notes preceding the Middle; a change and a deception of the senses occurs, the event turning out differently than was expected. And this change is indeed pleasing when the contraction is proportional and harmonious; but it is unpleasing when the opposite is true. Therefore, that change is most beautiful, and almost unique in its power, which is similar to the one previously mentioned, having an added transition in the **whole tone** <note>original: *tonus*</note> by which the Fifth differs from the Fourth. Indeed, that **disjunctive tone** <note>original: *tonus diazeucticus*</note>, because it is common to all types <note>original: *generum*</note>, can make the change conspicuous in all of them; and because it differs from the ratios within the tetrachords, it can alter the melody; and finally, because it is proportional (being the first of the harmonious intervals), it makes the digressions in the melody neither very large nor very small; either of which is difficult for the ears to judge. Therefore, three tetrachords are formed in continuous succession (according to what is characteristic of this change) by a partial mixture of two **Disjointed Systems**, which differ from each other in total by the magnitude of a Fourth. Since, however, the increase of **modes** <note>original: *tonorum*</note> had not proceeded that far among the ancients (for they knew only the **Dorian**, the **Phrygian**, and the **Lydian**, differing from each other by a single tone; so that they did not reach that which is a whole Fourth higher or lower); and thus they had no way by which, from Disjointed ones, they might make three continuous tetrachords: they encompassed this "Continuous" one under the name of a **System**, so that they might have the described change ready at hand. But, universally: in the Modes which differ from each other by a Fourth; whether of the tetrachords which precede a similar Disjunction in both, the tetrachord of the higher mode is joined in the high register to that which is of the lower; it will make, in the lower mode, three **Joined Tetrachords**, of which the one thus transferred is the highest: or if, of the tetrachords which follow a similar Disjunction in both, the tetrachord of the lower mode is joined in the low register to that of the higher... <-

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 6.

It is; likewise, in a higher pitch, it will produce three **Joined Tetrachords** <note>original: "tetrachorda conjuncta"; a tetrachord is a series of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</note>; of which, the one thus transferred becomes the lowest. For let there be, from the highest sound **A**, a tetrachord descending to the grave **AB**, and another joined to it **BC**, and following this, a **disjunctive tone** <note>original: "tonus diazeucticus"; a whole step that separates two tetrachords</note> **CD**: likewise, below that, two other joined tetrachords **DE** and **EF**. Now, let there be taken a disjunction **GH**, similar to **CD**, but higher by the interval of a **Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note>, and joined to it toward the grave, likewise two tetrachords **HK** and **KL**: And let there be a disjunction **MN**, similar to **CD**, but lower than that first one by a **Fourth**, and joined to it toward the acute <note>higher pitch</note>, two tetrachords **NX** and **XO**. Since, therefore, sound **H** is similar to **D**; it will be higher than it by a Fourth. Moreover, it is also higher than **K** by that same amount. Therefore, **D** and **K** are **Equal-Toned** <note>original: "Aequitoni"; having the same pitch or frequency</note>. It will therefore be possible for the tetrachord **KH** to be joined to **D** toward the acute; and so three continuous tetrachords are made in the tone **AF** (of which that one will be the highest), namely **FE**, **ED**, and **DH**. Likewise; since sound **N** is similar to **C**; it will be lower than it by a Fourth. Moreover, it is also lower than **X** by that same amount. Therefore, **C** and **X** are **Equal-Toned**. It will therefore be possible for the tetrachord **XN** to be joined to **C** toward the grave; and so three continuous tetrachords are made in the tone **AF** (of which that one will be the lowest), namely **AB**, **BC**, and **CN**. The diagram placed below follows.

<note>The following Greek text is a parallel version of the Latin above, describing the same musical geometry.</note>

...lower, it produces again in the higher pitch three joined tetrachords, of which the transferred one becomes the lowest. For let there be, from the highest sound **alpha**, the tetrachord toward the grave **alpha-beta**, and another joined to it **beta-gamma**, and the following disjunctive **gamma-delta**: and again under it another two joined tetrachords, **delta-epsilon** and **epsilon-zeta**. And let there be taken the higher by the interval of a fourth, the disjunction **eta-theta** similar to **gamma-delta**, and joined through it toward the grave two more tetrachords, **theta-kappa** and **kappa-lambda**: and the lower by the interval of a fourth than the first, the disjunction **mu-nu** similar to **gamma-delta**, and joined through it toward the acute two more tetrachords, **nu-xi** and **xi-omicron**: Since then the sound **eta** is similar to **delta**, it will be higher than it by a fourth: and **kappa** is also higher by the same; therefore **delta** and **kappa** are equal-toned. So that it will be possible to join to **delta**, toward the acute, the tetrachord **kappa-theta**, and to make three in succession in the **alpha-zeta** tone, of which it will be the highest, **zeta-epsilon**, **epsilon-delta**, and **delta-eta**. Again, since the sound **nu** is similar to **gamma**, it will be lower than it by a fourth: and **xi** is also lower by the same; therefore **gamma** and **xi** are equal-toned: so that it will be possible to join to **gamma** toward the grave the tetrachord **xi-nu**, of which it will be the lowest, **alpha-beta**, **beta-gamma**, and **gamma-nu**. The illustration follows.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical systems from Book II, Chapter 6, specifically focusing on the transition from the "Disjoint" and "Conjoined" systems to the theory of "Tones" or keys.</meta>

# 7.
## CHAPTER VII.

->On the changes according to the so-called ^a Tones.<-
<note>original: "Περὶ τῶν κατὰ τοὺς καλουμένους Τόνους μεταβολῶν" / "De mutationibus secundum (quos vocant) Tonos."</note>

That it is entirely superfluous to include the Conjoined System <note>The *Systema Conjunctum* was a specific Greek tetrachord arrangement</note> when the Perfect Disjoint Systems are already accepted—since the addition of the fourth ^b is already present, and because the Conjoined System does not possess the nature of a "Perfect" system as we have said ^d—let this now be made clear. We must further define the nature of the changes that occur throughout entire musical compositions; these we specifically call "Tones" <note>In this context, "Tones" refers to keys or modes of transposition (tonoi), not just individual intervals</note>, because these differences arise from the tension ^f of the voice. In potential, their number is infinite, just as the number of individual sounds ^h is infinite. For a "Tone" in this sense differs from a single sound only in that the Tone is composite, while the sound is incomposite—much like a line compared to a point. For nothing ^i prevents us from moving either a single point or an entire line into continuous locations. However, in actual practice and in relation to our perception, the number is finite, just as the number of usable sounds ^k is finite. Therefore, there are three defining rules ^m to be considered regarding Tones, just as there are for any of the concords. First, the rule by which the ratio of the outer tones is established. Second, the rule by which the number of tones between those extremes is established. Third, the rule by which the specific intervals between successive tones are established. Just as, for example, in the concord of the fourth <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note>: first, that the outer sounds create a 4:3 ratio <note>original: "sesquitertiam"</note>; second, that only three intervals compose the whole; and third, what the specific differences of those ratios are. Except that, while each of these rules has its own specific cause in the case of intervals, in the case of Tones, the remaining two rules follow the first one, relying on one and the same safeguard. Most people, being ignorant of the logical consequence of this, have set out these rules in different ways: some stopping short of the octave <note>original: "Dia-pason"</note>; others stopping exactly at it; and others going beyond it. These younger writers ^s almost always hunt for some kind of "progress" beyond what the ancients ^s achieved, though such progress is foreign to the nature and restoration of a well-tuned system. It is this nature alone which must necessarily limit the distance between the extreme tones; for neither the range of the human voice nor that of any instrument that produces sound can have one and the same limit. Indeed, we find that this arrangement of change according to Tone was not created for the sake of making voices higher or lower,

---

CHAPTER 7. a O. tones. b D. change--. c ABGIO. not. d GIO. we might say. e CV. happening. f C. lacks "something." g O. lacks "tone." h CV. thus. i ABV. preventing. O. punishing. k GIO. of the whole. l KO. two. m V. "as," which is entirely correct, but missing in the others. n AB. -tais. o GI. This contains a Scholium <note>A marginal or end-note by a commentator</note>: *It should be known that only up to this point have we found the commentary of Porphyry.* But in DM. it continues to the end of this Chapter. p V. they shall be. q ABGIO. smaller. r ABGI. larger; CKV. the larger. s AB. more anciently. CV. anciently. t CKVO. of those producing. u AB. sharper or deeper. x AB. lacks "voices."

<sig>L</sig>
<meta>catchword: tutionem,</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the theoretical definition of "Tones" (keys or modes) as transpositions of the musical system. It argues that while the number of possible pitches is mathematically infinite, the number of functional musical keys is finite and governed by three specific rules of proportion and range.</summary>
<keywords>Tones, Systema, Fourth, Transposition, Porphyry's Commentary, Concords, Octave, Musical Ratios</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 7 regarding the nature of "tones" (keys or modes) and their shifts, transitioning into Chapter 8 which defines the limits of these shifts within the octave.</meta>

# 66 CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Cap. 8.

<note>original: "tutionem" - continuing "restitutionem" from the previous page</note> restoration, (since for a difference of this kind, the tightening or loosening of whole instruments would suffice, while no change is made in the melody itself; just as the whole piece is performed similarly by performers endowed with deeper or higher voices:) but for this purpose; namely, so that, by one and the same voice, the same melody—begun now from lower and now from higher positions—might produce a certain **conversion of the mode** <note>Greek: *tropikēn tina tou ēthous* - a change in the character or emotional quality</note>: because the extremes of the voice do not coincide simultaneously with both extremes of the song during variations of the tones; but rather, in some cases, the limit of the voice ends before the limit of the song; in others, the limit of the song ends before that of the voice; so that the melody which, from the start, was suited to the span of the voice, now by falling short and now by overstepping during these mutations, presents to the ears the imagination of a different mode.

<note>The following is the original Greek text of the passage translated above.</note>
...since for such a difference the tightening of the whole instruments, or again their loosening, is sufficient, no change being effected in the melody, when the whole is performed similarly by deeper-voiced or higher-voiced contestants; but for the sake of the same melody, by the one voice, sometimes beginning from higher places, sometimes from lower, producing a certain turning of the character; by the fact that the limits of the voice no longer coincide with each of the limits of the melody in the exchanges of the tones; but always, in one direction, the limit of the voice stops before that of the melody; and in the opposite, the limit of the melody before that of the voice; so that the melody which originally fitted the span of the voice, in some ways falling short in the changes, and in others encompassing it, provides the ears with the fantasy of a different character.

---

### CHAPTER VIII.

### That the extremes of the Tones ought to be limited by the Octave itself.

### <note>original Greek: Ὅτι τῷ διὰ πασῶν ὁρίζεσθαι δεῖ τοὺς ἄκρους τῶν τόνων.</note>

Therefore, let the first and most important restoration of similarity in a tuning be in the first of the **unisons** <note>term: *unisonorum* - here referring to notes that sound "the same" though at different pitches, i.e., octaves</note>; that is, in the **Octave** <note>term: *Dia-pason* - the interval of an eighth</note>: since the sounds encompassing it (as we showed before) behave as if they were one. Likewise; just as the **consonances** <note>term: *consonantia* - harmonious intervals</note> which are composed with the Octave produce the same effect as they would if placed by themselves: so also any of the songs can similarly run its full course, whether in that distance alone which is from the first of the unisons; or in that which is composed of this and the Octave; taking the beginning from either of the extreme sounds. For which reason, in the permutations of the Tones, if ever we wish a Tone which is an Octave higher...

---

<margin>
<note>This long marginal note by the 17th-century editor (John Wallis) explains Ptolemy's theory using the "Guidonian Hand" and solmization system common in the Renaissance/Baroque periods.</note>
For example, (in modern Music,) if the first note of some song, as it is now sung, is *ut* in *Gam-ut* <note>The lowest note of the medieval hexachord system</note>, (so that *mi* is to be sung in *B mi* hard, that is, sharp; and again in *b fa b mi*; in which case, the note *b-flat* must not be assigned to the B-strings:) but I should wish that the same song be sung in a higher tone; say, one which is either a **Fourth** <note>term: *Dia-tessaron*</note>, or an **Octave and a Fourth** higher than the one set forth; (whereby the note *ut*, which was previously in *G-ut*, is now either in *C-fa-ut*, or in *c-sol-fa-ut*:) in either case, the same change of Keys must be made; namely, to each of the B-strings, the note *b-flat* must be assigned (whereby the note *mi*, which was previously sung in B, is now sung in E, everywhere). And the same must be done if I wish the Tone to be either a **Fifth** <note>term: *Dia-pente*</note>, or an **Octave and a Fifth** lower than the one set forth; whereby the note *ut*, which was previously in *Gam-ut*, is now either in *CC-fa-ut*, or in *CCC-fa-ut* (below *Gam-ut*); and thus *mi* is not in B, but in E. For of two sounds differing by an Octave, the intermediate sound which is a Fourth away from one will be a Fifth away from the other: for example, of the two, *C-fa-ut* and *CC-fa-ut*, the intermediate sound *Gam-ut*, which is a Fourth from the former, will be a Fifth from the latter; and so since *ut* is in C, *mi* will be in E, as before. But if I wish the tone to be an Octave higher or lower than the one set forth; so that the note *ut*, which had been in *Gam-ut*, is now in *G-sol-re-ut*; or vice versa: the same notation of Keys will remain as before, (because of *ut* being in each G, and thus *mi* in each B, as originally;) and it is (in power) the same Tone. Certainly, for Ptolemy, a change according to Tones is the same as a varied notation of Keys is for us.
</margin>

<summary>Ptolemy explains that shifting the "tone" (key) of a melody changes its emotional character because the melody interacts differently with the natural limits of the human voice. He establishes that these shifts should be measured within the span of an octave (Dia-pason).</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Harmonics, Octave, Dia-pason, music theory, modulation, solmization, hexachord, Greek music, Latin translation</keywords>

# Chapter 8. HARMONICS BOOK II.

<sup>k</sup> Or if we wish to substitute a lower <term>pitch</term><note>original: φθόγγων (phthongon). In Greek music theory, a phthongos is a single note of a definite pitch.</note>, we move none of the notes <sup>l</sup> (whereas in other changes we always move some); but the <sup>m</sup> <term>Tone</term><note>original: Tonus. Here referring to a specific musical key or mode.</note> <sup>n</sup> itself remains the same as it was at the beginning. <sup>o</sup> Consequently, the Tone which <sup>p</sup> differs by a <term>fourth</term><note>original: διὰ τεσσάρων (dia tessaron), literally "through four."</note> from the original is the same as that which differs by an <term>octave plus a fourth</term><note>original: διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ τεσσάρων (dia pason kai dia tessaron).</note> from that same original. The one <sup>q</sup> which differs by a <term>fifth</term><note>original: διὰ πέντε (dia pente), literally "through five."</note> from the original is the same as that which differs by an <term>octave plus a fifth</term><note>original: διὰ πασῶν καὶ διὰ πέντε (dia pason kai dia pente)</note> <sup>r</sup> from the same; and similarly for the others. Thus, those who define the outer limits of the Tones <sup>r</sup> within the span of an octave would not have fully restored the harmonic system; for there is always some Tone beyond them <sup>s</sup> unlike all the first ones. But <sup>t</sup> those who exceed <sup>u</sup> the octave <sup>p</sup> and go further away from the octave itself, <sup>v</sup> mistakenly assume a new Tone; <sup>x</sup> whereas these are always the same as those previously taken. Specifically, <sup>y</sup> within <sup>r</sup> the octave, those <sup>z</sup> which are equally distant from the octave are the same as those which are equally distant (in the same direction) from the original. Therefore, those who proceed only as far as the octave do not correctly <sup>a</sup> count the Tone that is an octave away from the original as a separate Tone. For they will be seen to have <sup>f</sup> committed the same error as those who overstep the established limit; the latter <sup>c</sup> err in many instances, while the former err in one. So that they might <sup>r</sup> justly be answered by those who are criticized for excess, that they themselves <sup>x</sup> provided the <sup>d</sup> cause <sup>r</sup> for the overstepping. For if a Tone is taken once that is the same as one of the preceding ones (such as the octave <sup>e</sup> being the same as the original), what <sup>f</sup> prevents one (they might say) from also adding those that follow the rest <sup>z</sup> in <sup>a</sup> proportion? And indeed, the fact that <sup>d</sup> the <term>powers</term><note>original: δυνάμεις (dynameis). In music theory, this refers to the function or "role" of a note within a scale.</note> within the octave should not be measured by the number of its terms, but by the number of <sup>e</sup> ratios that compose it, <sup>g</sup> we have a most fitting example from the <term>species</term><note>original: εἰδῶν (eidon). The different types of octave scales (modes).</note> contained within it. For we all assume <sup>h</sup> these to be only seven without variation, even though the notes that produce them happen to be eight. <sup>h</sup> No one would say that the octave taken from the lowest note (for example) toward the bass <sup>e</sup> produces a different species <sup>x, i</sup> than the first one taken in the same direction <sup>k</sup> from the highest note; <sup>d</sup> because (universally) whatever <sup>r</sup> begins in the same manner <sup>h</sup> from either of the two extremes of the octave produces the same power.

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical and functional identity of musical intervals across octaves, arguing that an octave plus a fifth functions the same as a simple fifth. It concludes that there are only seven species of the octave (modes) despite there being eight notes involved, because the eighth note repeats the "power" of the first.</summary>

# CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chap. 9.

### CHAPTER IX.

### 9.

### That only seven Tones should be proposed; which are exactly as many as the Species of the Octave itself.

### That it is necessary to set down only seven tones, equal in number to the species <gloss>a</gloss> of the octave.

Reason has led us to consider the number of Tones <note>original: "Tonorum"; here referring to the keys or modes of the musical system</note>. It would certainly be appropriate to make them as many as there are species of the Octave <note>original: "Dia-pason"</note>; for indeed, these are as many as the two primary Consonances combined, taken according to the requirement of each of their own Ratios: (*Namely, three in the Fourth <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note>, and four in the Fifth <note>original: "Dia-pente"</note>; as many as there are ratios for them; and indeed such in each case, for every Genus, as the nature of each genus requires;*) of which nature allows neither more nor fewer to be proposed. Therefore, just as if someone wished to make divisions into more parts—for instance, of the Fourth—than three; or indeed, the same number, but with the intervals <note>original: "excessibus"</note> taken at random; or even with determined intervals, but different from those taken according to the proper ratio (for each genus): the nature of reason, as well as Experience, would immediately cry out against it. Similarly, if any should propose that the tones contained within the Octave—which follow the nature of the consonances and have obtained their origin for the sake of those consonances (namely, so that entire Systems might have consonant differences)—are either more than seven (which is the number of both the Species and the Ratios of the Octave itself;) or that the intervals between them all are equal; it must not be conceded to them. For they do not have even a probable cause to assign; whether that the increments of all are equal (which, in Harmony, is found to be altogether unpleasant;) or that all their intervals between one another are, for example, Whole-tones <note>original: "Tonici"</note>, or even Semitones <note>original: "Hemitionia"</note>, or Quarter-tones <note>original: "Diesis"</note>; (from which suppositions they determine the number of Tones, according to the multitude of those intervals constituting the Octave:) For why should they make these intervals rather than others? since (according to them) Consonance could admit both these, and those, and many others; both in the Genera and in the ordering of distances. Nor can they say that this magnitude divides the Octave exactly, while that one does not; or that these members are (perhaps) even in number, while those are odd. On the contrary, if the Whole-tone divides the Octave into six equal parts; and the Semitone into twelve; and the third of a tone into eighteen; and the quarter into twenty-four: even so, none of these is free from fault.

The argument has led us to perceive the number of the tones. For it would be well to make them equal in number to the species of the octave; because there are just so many <gloss>b</gloss> as there are of both the first two consonances combined; taken according to what follows the ratios in each case; which Nature allows to be proposed as neither more nor fewer. Just as, then, if one should wish to make the divisions into more parts, if it should happen, of the fourth, beyond the three <gloss>f</gloss> or indeed for so many, <gloss>g</gloss> in chance intervals; or again <gloss>h</gloss> in defined ones, but in others <gloss>i</gloss> than those taken according to the fitting ratio; immediately both the reasonable and the apparent stand in opposition. So also in the tones contained by the octaves, being followers of the nature of the consonances, and having received their birth for the sake of those, so that even the whole systems may receive consonant differences, if one proposes either more than the seven species and ratios of the octave, or <gloss>k</gloss> according to equal intervals of all from one another, it is not to be conceded. Since they cannot even say a persuasive cause, neither <gloss>l</gloss> according to the pairing of the wholes of equality, this being found altogether unsuitable in harmony <gloss>m</gloss>; nor that all the intervals are such, <gloss>n</gloss> for example, or again semitonal, or <gloss>n</gloss> diesis-sized; from which being proposed, <gloss>o</gloss> they define the number of the tones, according to the multitude of those <gloss>i</gloss> making the octaves. For why would one make them of such a size <gloss>k</gloss> <gloss>p</gloss> for the sake of sounding together, when both these and those and many others are admitted, <gloss>l</gloss> both in the classes of the genera, <gloss>l</gloss> and in the arrangements of the distances <gloss>m</gloss>? For it is not possible <gloss>n</gloss> for them to say, that this magnitude divides the octave in a finished way <gloss>p</gloss>, but that one <gloss>q</gloss> not in a finished way; or, this one, if it should happen, in even parts, but that one <gloss>q</gloss> in odd. But if the tone divides the octave into six <gloss>r</gloss>, and the semitone into twelve, and the <gloss>s</gloss> third of a tone into eighteen, and the fourth into twenty-four; even so none of these is beyond reproach;

---

"to the", but "to the" should be retained (with the rest) because of "being composed" which follows. <gloss>e</gloss> C. "to the equal". AB. <summary>Ptolemy argues that there are only seven musical tones (modes), corresponding to the seven species of the octave, rejecting arbitrary divisions of the scale that lack mathematical or harmonic justification.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, music theory, octave species, consonances, fourth, fifth, harmonic ratios, musical modes</keywords>

# Chapter 9. HARMONICS BOOK II.

<margin>f</margin> ...has the difference. One might ask: how should we define the differences between the seven tones? For the <term>Dia-pason</term><note>The octave (interval of an 8th).</note> is not divided into seven equal ratios, nor, if they are unequal, is it easy to say which of them should be assumed. We must say they are those found by following the first <term>consonances</term><note>Harmonious intervals like the 4th and 5th.</note> in succession. That is to say: those remaining from the <term>Dia-tessaron</term><note>The interval of a 4th.</note> within the Dia-pason, when the same distance is added on either side as that of the <term>Dia-pente</term><note>The interval of a 5th.</note> toward the opposite side.

<column-break/>

...the imperceptible difference. Which then (someone might say) should be established as these seven excesses of the Tones among themselves? Since the Dia-pason is not divided into seven equal ratios; nor, if into unequal ones, is it easy to say which of them ought to be assumed. Those, it must be said, which are found from the first consonances through a just succession: Namely, those which, in either direction, are obtained from the continuous addition of the Dia-tessaron within the Dia-pason; which is the same as the continuous addition of the Dia-pente taken toward the opposite parts. (Which is explained more fully in the following chapter.)

<center>

| High | High |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Dia-tessaron** (Left) | **Dia-pente** (Right) |
| **Unisons** (Middle) | **Dia-pason** (Full Height) |
| **Dia-pente** (Left) | **Dia-tessaron** (Right) |
| **{ Tone }** | **{ Tone }** |
| Low | Low |

</center>

<margin>u</margin> For a sound that is lower than another by a <margin>s</margin> Dia-tessaron is higher by a Dia-pente than the note that is in <margin>y</margin> unison with it at the lower pitch. Or, that which is <margin>x</margin> higher than another by a Dia-tessaron is lower by a Dia-pente than the note in unison with it at the higher pitch. It is necessary <margin>z</margin> (not only here, but everywhere) that unisons precede and be assumed before consonances, and consonances before <term>concinnous</term><note>Melodic intervals that are not necessarily consonant but "fit" in a scale.</note> intervals. Thus, from the Tones, one must first take those that are consonant; then, those found through the difference between them, of whatever kind they may be. For a transition into the next adjacent Tone does not produce as pleasing a <margin>a</margin> change as a transition into those that differ by the primary <margin>b</margin> consonances.

<column-break/>

Indeed, a sound which is lower than some note by a Dia-tessaron is higher by a Dia-pente than the Unison of that same note placed at the lower pitch. Likewise, that which is higher than some note by a Dia-tessaron is lower by a Dia-pente than the Unison of that same note placed at the higher pitch. It is necessary, however (not only here, but everywhere), that Unisons precede and be assumed before Consonances, and Consonances before Concinnous intervals: And so, from the Tones, it is necessary to first take those which are Consonant; then, those which are found by the excess of those among themselves, of whatever kind they may finally be: Since a transition into the nearest Tone does not make so pleasing a change as one into those which differ by the primary consonances.

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical derivation of musical tones within an octave, arguing that intervals should be defined by the relationship between the 4th (Dia-tessaron) and 5th (Dia-pente) rather than by equal division. It emphasizes that consonant intervals are more fundamental to musical transition than simple step-wise motion.</summary>

# THE HARMONICS OF CLAUDIUS PTOLEMY Chapter 10.

->CHAPTER X.<-

->How the differences of the Tones may be correctly understood.<-

It appears that those who proceeded as far as the eight Tones (on account of one being added superfluously to the seven)^a fell somehow into their proper intervals, but not by the necessary method of consideration. For they simply assumed three, the most ancient of all, called the **Dorian**, the **Phrygian**, and the **Lydian**; (taking the names from the nations where they originated, or however else one wishes to assign the cause;)^b differing from one another by a **Tone** <note>original: "Tono"; a whole step</note>; for which reason they also called them "Equal-toned." From these they made the first consonant **Mutation** <note>original: "Mutationem"; a change in key or modulation</note>, by an interval of a **Fourth** <note>original: "Dia-tessaron"</note> toward the acute <note>higher pitch</note> from the lowest of the three, which is the Dorian: this tone they called the **Mixolydian**, because of its proximity to the Lydian; since it does not differ from it by a whole tone, but by a **Remainder** <note>original: "Limma"</note>, or that part of a Fourth which remains after a **Ditone** <note>two whole tones</note>, which is the distance from the Dorian to the Lydian. Then, since the Dorian was situated a Fourth below these: so that the others might also have tones placed a Fourth lower, they named the one below the Lydian the **Hypolydian**; the one below the Phrygian, the **Hypophrygian**; and the one below the Dorian, the **Hypodorian**; and the tone which was an **Octave** <note>original: "Diapason"</note> higher than this (being the same as it in quality)^l they called (by happenstance) the **Hypermixolydian**, as it was taken above the Mixolydian. (Using the prefix *Hypo-* <note>under</note> incorrectly to indicate the part in the Grave <note>low pitch</note>, and *Hyper-* <note>above</note> for that in the Acute.) And it follows (from those first ones)^m that the interval of the Hypodorian to the Hypophrygian is a Tone (as is the Dorian to the Phrygian): and similarly the Hypophrygian to the Hypolydian: but from this to the Dorian is a **Remainder**; (which they wish to be a **Semitone**.) But (as we have said) it was not proper to derive the Consonances from the Melodic intervals; but on the contrary, the latter from the former. (Since Consonances are both easier to grasp and more fundamental, both for all other things and for making Mutations.) This would be done correctly in this way: namely; if, having set forth a higher Tone, as A: we take, first, that which is a Fourth lower than this, as B: and another which is yet a Fourth lower than this—

<column-break/>

->1.<-

<margin>a. GIO. 1. b. So AB (which I follow), but CGIVO in reverse order, "Lydian and Phrygian." c. C. "placed." d. CV. "through." e. GI. "naming." f. C. "the." g. GIO. "remaining." h. GIVO. "since." i. C. "of these." k. AB. "to which." C. "in the." l. C. "passed down." m. He says they used these words "abusively" or incorrectly; because, among the ancients, the "Grave" was considered to be at the top (and the "Acute" at the bottom) whence the *Hypaton* tetrachord was the lowest of all, and the *Hypate* string the lowest of the tetrachord: but, before these names were applied to the Tones, they had begun to be used in the opposite sense (as they are still used today), "lower" for the grave and "higher" for the acute. n. D. "semitone." o. GIO. "we say." p. V. "is." q. GICVO. "again," not badly; but I prefer "all." r. V. lacks "the." s. GIO. "as," also not bad; but all others have "A." t. A. inserts "me," which is absent from the others, even B, from which I think A was copied. u. AB. "the lowest by a fourth," with the same sense. x. GIVO. "upon this." C. "to this."</margin>

<summary>Ptolemy critiques the traditional derivation of the eight musical modes (tones), arguing that they were named after nations and organized haphazardly rather than being derived from primary mathematical consonances like the Fourth and Octave.</summary>
<keywords>Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, Hypodorian, musical modes, Greek music theory, consonances, intervals, Ptolemy</keywords>
